# Chapter 1: Heavy Breath

*The Bond · 2431*

At nine hundred kilometres down, Tarrow stopped being weather and started being a weight.

Maren Tull felt it arrive the way she always did, first in the teeth. The *Gannet*'s hull took up a low note that had no business in a ship, a note she heard through the bones of her jaw before she heard it in the cabin, and then the straps across her chest began to tighten without anyone tightening them. Outside the forward glass the cloud went from rust to brick to the colour of an old bruise, and the light that came through it — Vigil's light, which was never much to begin with — thinned out until the only reliable thing in the world was the green wash of her own instruments.

"You've gone quiet," said Dov in her ear.

"I'm working."

"You go quiet when you're scared and you go quiet when you're working. Thirty-eight years I've been listening to Tulls go quiet and I still can't tell which."

"Then stop listening and spot."

He laughed, which turned into the cough, which went on long enough that she reached for the comm toggle before he came back. When he did his voice was wet. "Chimney forming at your two o'clock, eighty klicks. Big one. Don't go near it. There's a sink under you that I don't love, and I can't see the bottom of it."

"You never can."

"I can usually see the shape. This one hasn't got a shape."

*Old Patience* was up there somewhere above the decks, eleven hundred kilometres over her head in the thin cold where the cloud tops caught Vigil's light and turned it the pink of a healed scar. Dov flew the tender the way he drank, carefully and continuously, holding station over his diver on a laser thread so fine it could be broken by a squall, and reading the giant's weather off a radar that had been obsolete when Maren's mother first dove. Maren had never seen Tarrow from up there except on the climb. She had only ever seen it from inside.

At nine-forty the pressure alarm began its polite chirping. She thumbed it off. At nine-sixty the hull note climbed half a tone and her ears, sealed in the helmet, felt as though someone were pressing both palms against them and leaning. The scoop indicator on her left knee was still dark. Tarrow kept its heavy breath in seams, thin bright layers where the pressure and the cold and whatever else happened down here — nobody had ever properly explained it to her, and she had stopped asking at nineteen — folded hydrogen over on itself into something that wanted, very badly, to be something else. Find a seam, open the scoops, fill the bottles. Climb. That was the whole job. It was simple the way drowning was simple.

"Talk to me," Dov said.

"Nothing yet."

"You're at nine-seventy."

"I can read."

"Your mother never went past a thousand."

"My mother had a different tally-man." She eased the nose down three degrees. The *Gannet* shuddered and settled, a fat armoured seed sinking through soup. "Siggard's docked me twice this month for light loads. Third time it goes on the bond."

"It goes on the bond either way. Everything goes on the bond. Listen to me, Maren—"

The scoop light came on.

It came on amber and went to green so fast she nearly missed it, and then the whole left side of the board was lit, and beneath the hull, in the dark that was not quite dark, she could see it: a sheet of faint pale light lying in the murk like a seam of frost in black rock, stretching away farther than her lamps could follow. A good seam. A seam you told your grandchildren about, if anybody in the Sump lived long enough to have grandchildren.

"Dov."

"I see it on your telemetry. Christ. Open your mouth, girl."

She opened the scoops. The *Gannet* bucked as the intakes bit, and the sound changed — the low note under the hull broke up into a roar, a long rushing exhale, as if the giant were breathing out through her. The bottle gauges began to climb. Ten percent. Twenty. The cabin smelled faintly, as it always did on a full draw, of struck matches and pennies, which meant the seals were weeping a little of what she was stealing, which meant another week of the cough. She did not care. Thirty percent.

"How long can you hold?"

"Long as you keep me clear of your sink."

"Then you've got four minutes," said Dov, "and I'd like you to take three."

Forty. Fifty. She thought about nothing. You learned that, or you learned nothing else. You did not think about the eleven hundred kilometres of cloud above your head or the thousand-odd kilometres below you where the cloud became a sea and the sea became something without a name, where every skimmer that had ever gone missing was still falling, slow as snow, toward a floor that might not exist. You did not think about the bond. You did not think about the Nursery glass, or the way Pell had started, this last year, to press her palm flat against it before Maren did, as though it were Pell's turn now to be the one who reached. You watched the gauge. Sixty. Sixty-five.

"Maren." Dov's voice had changed. "The sink's moving."

"Moving where?"

"Up. Toward you. It's not a sink. I don't know what it is. Get your nose up."

"I'm at seventy."

"I don't care if you're at a hundred. Nose up, close your mouth, climb."

She closed the scoops. The roar stopped as though a door had shut on it, and in the sudden quiet she felt it before the instruments did: the floor of the world dropping away. The *Gannet* fell. Not sank — fell, nose first, a stone through a hole, and the straps that had been tightening on her chest let go entirely so that for one sick floating second she hung in them with her stomach somewhere up near her helmet ring and her hands already moving. Throttle forward. Pitch. Nothing. The downdraft had her, a whole column of the giant's atmosphere collapsing toward the deep like water down a drain, and the *Gannet* went with it, the altimeter unwinding, nine-ninety, a thousand, a thousand and twenty.

"Climb!"

"I am climbing."

The engines were at full and the needle was still falling. A thousand and forty. The hull note was not a note any more; it was a groan with words in it. Something in the starboard frame let go with a crack like a bone, and a red light came on above her head that she had only ever seen in drills.

She knew the number. Every breather knew the number for their own skimmer, the way you knew your own bond. With seventy percent bottles the *Gannet* weighed what she weighed, and her engines could lift what they could lift, and in a downdraft this strong there was a gap between those figures and Maren Tull was falling through it.

Her hand went to the dump lever.

It was a plain lever, bright yellow under a hinged guard. Pull it, and the bottles vented. Pull it halfway, and half of them did. Everything she had taken in the last four minutes would go back into the giant in a long pale plume, and the *Gannet* would be lighter by however much breath she had dumped, and the tally-man would write the dumped breath down not as a loss to Tarrow but as a loss to the Halloran-Vasquez Combine, product spilled by the negligence of bond H-11873, and charged accordingly.

"Maren."

A thousand and sixty.

She flipped the guard up and pulled the lever to the half mark.

The *Gannet* shrieked. That was what it felt like — a sound the whole ship made, every seam at once, as the bottles opened and the weight went out of her. The altimeter shivered, hung, and turned. A thousand and fifty. A thousand and forty. Climbing. Slowly, and then less slowly, out of the throat of the downdraft and into the shear at its edge, where the *Gannet* rolled ninety degrees and came back and rolled again and Maren sat with her hands on the controls and her teeth together and did not say anything at all.

"There she is," Dov said softly, somewhere at the top of the world. "There's my girl. Come on home."

* * *

The tally house at the Number Four refinery head smelled of hot metal and old sweat and the particular sourness of the Sump's recycled air, which had been breathed by thirty thousand people so many times that it had stopped being air and become a kind of shared memory. It was a long low hall cut into the ice, its walls lined with plastic so old it had gone the colour of tea, and down the middle of it ran the weighing line: a rail of brass-capped cradles where the breathers' bottles were racked and read.

Siggard read hers himself. He was a big man with the soft, careful hands of someone who had never had to make a fist in his life, and he moved down the rack touching each bottle with a little wand, and with each touch a number appeared on the board behind him. Maren stood with her helmet under her arm and her hair plastered to her skull and watched the numbers come up. They were good numbers. They were very good numbers. Thirty-five percent of a full draw from a seam like that was more than some breathers brought up in a fortnight.

"Thirty-four point eight," said Siggard. "Good seam."

"Very good seam."

"Telemetry shows a draw to seventy." He did not look at her. He looked at his slate. "Then a dump."

"There was a downdraft."

"There's always a downdraft." He tapped. "Product loss, thirty-five point two percent of draw, at standard valuation, less equipment damage — you've cracked a frame member, Tull, did you know that? Starboard, number six — less frame repair, less scoop recalibration, less the usual." He turned the slate around so she could see the line at the bottom. It was red. "You're down on the dive."

She looked at it for a long moment.

"I brought up thirty-five percent of a full draw."

"You lost thirty-five percent of a full draw. Combine owns the seam, Tull. Combine owns the breath in it. You took it out of the seam, which makes it Combine product, and then you poured it back into the giant, which makes it Combine product lost." He said it without malice, the way a man might explain the tides. "Count what's owed."

"And not a mark more," Maren said, because it was what you said. Her mother had said it, standing at this same rail. Dov said it. Every breather in the Sump said it, a little prayer, a little curse, the only weapon they had: *I know what you're doing, and I'm watching you do it.* "I want to see the valuation table."

Siggard sighed, and found it, and showed her. She read it line by line while the breathers behind her shifted and muttered and someone said *come on, Tull,* not unkindly. The table was correct. That was the worst of it. It was always correct. The Combine did not cheat you in the arithmetic; it did not need to. It cheated you in the rules, which were the arithmetic's grammar, and which you had never been asked to agree to.

"Fine," she said.

"Sign."

She pressed her thumb to the slate. Somewhere on Hesper High, in a machine she would never see, a number attached to her name got bigger.

* * *

The Sump had been dug into Hesper's crust by the first bonds two hundred years ago, when the Combine still thought of Vigil as a frontier and not a quarry, and it had been dug in the way that frightened people dig — down and inward, away from the sky. Its corridors were tunnels in the ice lined with foam and sheeting, and the ice was always there behind the sheeting, breathing out a cold that got into everything. It ran in seventeen levels down to the Drowned Decks, where the meltwater from the refinery heat had pooled forty years ago and never been pumped out, and above it all, on the outermost level, the bonds lived in cells that they called apartments because it cost nothing to call them that.

Maren's was on Level Three, Spoke Nine. Two rooms. A heater that worked one shift in three. A bunk she had built herself out of a scrapped scoop housing, and on the wall above it, held up with surgical tape, eleven pictures that Pell had drawn and sent down on the visitation shuttle, one a year since she was taken. The first was a crayon house with a yellow sun. Nobody in the Sump had ever seen a yellow sun. The most recent was a precise ink diagram of the *Gannet* in cross-section, with every system labelled in Pell's cramped capitals and a note at the bottom: THIS IS WHERE YOU SIT. IT'S THE SAFEST PART. I CHECKED.

She stood looking at it with her suit half-unpeeled and her body shaking with the slow rattle of exhaustion and what was left of the fear, and then she went back out, because she had been gone for three days and the Spoke would want to know she was alive.

There was a crowd at the junction board. There was always a crowd at the junction board on refinery shift-change, breathers and refinery hands and the children of the Sump who were not wards — whose parents' debts had not yet crossed the line — shoving to read the notices the Combine pasted up: tally rates, air rates, the list of the dead. But this crowd was different. It was quiet. People were reading something and then turning away from it and then turning back to read it again, the way you do with a thing you do not understand or do not trust.

She pushed in. The notice was printed on real paper, which nobody had seen the Combine waste in years, with the H-V seal at the top in silver.

> **THE HALLORAN-VASQUEZ COMBINE IS PLEASED TO ANNOUNCE THE VIGIL SCHOLARSHIP.**
>
> In recognition of the long service of the bonded community of Vigil, the Combine has selected wards of the Hesper Nursery for advancement to Combine academies in the Inner Worlds. Selected wards will receive full education, lodging and placement. The bond obligations of their surety parents will be reviewed for reduction on a schedule to be published.
>
> Selected wards will depart aboard the *Halloran's Grace* with the scheduled rotation convoy in forty days.
>
> The Combine thanks the families of Vigil for their gift.

Below that, in smaller type, ran the list. Ward numbers. Four columns of them, down to the bottom of the sheet and onto a second sheet beneath it. Maren read down the first column without breathing and then the second, and her eyes caught on it before her mind did, the way a hand catches on a nail.

0212.

"That's nearly all of them," a woman beside her said. Lin Okoro, from Spoke Six; her boy was 0188. "Isn't it? That's nearly all of them."

"Four hundred and twelve," said someone further along, an old refinery hand who had counted. "Out of four-thirty."

"What's an academy?"

"It's a school. In the Inner Worlds. Under a real sky." Lin's voice was high and strange. "They're going to go to school under a real sky."

"They're going to go," said the old man, and nothing else, and walked away.

Maren stood with her hand on the board, flat over her daughter's number, as if she could hold it there.

* * *

Visitation was on the fifteenth of every month, Combine reckoning, and the fifteenth was two days later. She rode up in the belly of the ward shuttle with forty other parents, strapped into jump seats in a cargo bay that still smelled of the ice-ore it carried on other days, and nobody spoke much. A few were talking about the Scholarship in low eager voices — what an academy would be like, what the children would learn, whether the bond reductions would be large. Most were silent. The Sump did not, as a rule, trust gifts.

Hesper High hung in orbit over the moon's dark side like a chandelier somebody had dropped and not bothered to sweep up: a central spindle of offices and docks, the long arms of the Residence where the Factor and his officers lived, and, ringed around the hub where the spin gravity was gentlest, the Nursery. Maren had been up here a hundred and thirty-two times. She knew the walk from the shuttle lock to the visitation hall the way she knew the dive line, every turning, every camera, every place where the Combine's guards stood with their hands folded over their weapons and their faces closed.

The visitation hall was a long room divided down its length by glass.

The parents' side had benches. The children's side had nothing; the children stood. The glass was thick, scratched, and cleaned every day by the wards themselves as part of their duties, and there were small voice grilles set into it every two metres, through which, if you leaned close and the child leaned close, you could hear each other. You had fifteen minutes. A light above the door counted them down.

Pell was already there.

She had grown again. Every month she had grown again, and every month it hit Maren like a slap — the length of her, the sharpness coming into her face, the way the grey Nursery smock that had swamped her at six now stopped short of her wrists. She had Maren's mother's hair, black and coarse, cut to the jaw by the Nursery barber, and she had her father's eyes, whoever he had been, and she had a mouth that was entirely her own and had been since she was two: small, straight, held tight over whatever she was thinking until she decided it was worth saying.

She put her palm flat on the glass. Maren put hers on the other side.

"You're thinner," Pell said.

"You're taller."

"That's not a fair trade." Pell leaned to the grille. "Mr. Okoro said you had a downdraft. He saw it on the refinery board. He said you dumped half."

"Mr. Okoro talks too much."

"Did you crack a frame?"

"Starboard six."

"I told you. I told you on the diagram. Starboard six is the weak one, it's a repair from before, you can see the weld line on the—" Pell stopped. Her mouth went straight. "Are you all right?"

"I'm here, aren't I?"

Pell tapped the glass three times with one knuckle: *still here.* Maren tapped back. It was a thing they had done since the first visitation, when Pell was six and too frightened to speak and had knocked on the glass because she did not know what else to do, and Maren, not knowing what it meant, had knocked back. Now it meant whatever they needed it to mean.

"You've seen the list," Pell said.

"I've seen it."

"Matron says it's the greatest honour the Combine has ever given the Sump." Pell said it in Matron Brask's voice, a fair imitation, clipped and pleased with itself. Then in her own: "Matron says we'll learn real maths. Not just tally. Orbital mechanics and statistics and actuarial—" she said the word carefully, like something with edges "—and there are libraries, and there's a sky. A blue one. She showed us pictures."

"That sounds good," Maren said. Her voice did something she did not want it to do and she stopped it.

"It does sound good." Pell looked at her. "Are you coming?"

Maren opened her mouth and nothing came.

"I asked Matron," Pell said. "I asked if the parents were coming on the convoy too, because the notice said bond reviews, and she said that wasn't how it worked, and I asked how it did work, and she said I'd understand when I was older. I said I'm twelve. She said exactly." Pell's hand was still flat on the glass. "I'm not stupid."

"I know you're not."

"So when the notice says *gift*," said Pell, "who's giving what to who?"

The countdown light above the door flicked from green to amber. Five minutes. Somewhere down the glass a man was crying, quietly, his forehead against the grille, while a small girl on the other side patted the glass in front of his face as though it were his cheek.

"I don't know yet," said Maren. "I'm going to find out."

"Will you tell me?"

"I'll tell you."

"Even if it's bad."

"Especially if it's bad."

Pell nodded, once, the way she nodded at a sum that came out right. Then she reached into the neck of her smock and pulled out the cord, and Maren did the same, and they held them up to the glass, one on each side: two halves of a steel disc the size of a palm, broken across the middle along a jagged line. Pell's half was stamped with 0212 and HESPER NURSERY. Maren's was stamped with H-11873 and SURETY. On the day a ward was taken, the Combine broke a disc and gave a half to each, and the rule was that when the bond was paid the halves would be fitted together again and the child returned. Maren had never heard of it happening. Nobody had. But the Combine's rules were the Combine's rules, and it had kept this one in the letter for two centuries — every child, every parent, every jagged line — and so you kept your half on its cord against your skin, and once a month you held it to the glass and watched your daughter hold up hers, and the two edges lined up, tooth to tooth, with fifteen centimetres of glass between them.

"Count what's owed," said Pell.

"And not a mark more."

The light went red.

* * *

The guards herded the parents out by the far door in a shuffle of bodies, and Maren let herself be herded. She was thinking about the word *academy*, and about how Lin Okoro had said *real sky* like a woman describing heaven, and about the old refinery hand who had counted the list and then walked away. She was thinking about the bond review schedule, *to be published*. She was thinking that in two hundred years of bonds and wards and broken discs, nobody had ever been given anything by the Combine that had not been entered somewhere, by someone, as a cost or a gain.

At the corridor junction outside the hall, where the parents' line bent toward the shuttle lock and the Combine's own staff came and went through a side passage to the hub, a young man in a clerk's grey jacket came out of the side passage too fast and walked into her.

It was a hard collision. He was taller than her and badly balanced, and he caught her by both arms to keep from falling, and for a moment his face was close to hers — narrow, pale, with the soft unweathered skin of someone born on a station and fed on station food, and eyes that were very frightened.

"Sorry," he said. "Sorry. I wasn't—sorry."

"Watch yourself."

"Yes." His hands were on her arms, and then one of them was gone, and then he was gone, back into the side passage and walking quickly away with his head down, and a guard was saying *move along, bond,* and she moved along.

She did not find it until she was strapped into the jump seat in the shuttle's belly with the moon swinging up huge and white beneath them. She put her hand in the thigh pocket of her coverall to find her ration chit and felt a fold of something that was not a chit, something thin and stiff. Paper. Real paper, like the notice.

She took it out under the cover of her arm, in the dim red cargo light, and opened it.

It was a strip torn from the margin of something larger. There were five words on it, written in pencil in a fast slanting hand, as though whoever wrote them had been afraid of being seen doing it:

*The Scholarship is a sale.*

And below that, smaller, pressed so hard the pencil had nearly gone through:

*Ask the price of 0212.*


# Chapter 2: The Ninth Ledger

*The Ledger · 2794*

The Count changed at the first bell, as it had every morning for three hundred and forty-one years, and Ysolde Marr was there to hear it.

She was always there. She had been told once, by a Chancery elder who meant it as a rebuke, that no one was required to attend the Turning, that it was a thing for pilgrims and novices and the sentimental, and that an auditor of the Third Chancery had better uses for the hour. She had agreed, and gone on coming. She could not have explained it in terms the elder would have accepted. It was something to do with the sound.

The Ledger Hall was the oldest enclosed space in the Nave, a long vault of grey metal ribbed like the inside of a whale, and at its far end, filling the wall from floor to ceiling, hung the Count Board. It was made of ten columns of flip-plates, each plate as tall as a man and painted with a single white numeral on black, and it had been built in the second century by people who no longer trusted screens. At the first bell, the night's reckonings having been carried up from all nine Chanceries and summed, the board turned. It began at the right, with the units, and moved left: a clattering rush like rain on a roof, plates falling over plates, the tens and the hundreds and the thousands spinning past their old values toward their new ones, slowing as they went left, until at last only the great digits at the top were moving — billions, hundreds of millions — and those only rarely, and those only by one.

This morning the clatter ran out in the tens of thousands. The big digits did not stir. The board read, when it was done:

**3,907,114,260**

There was a murmur from the pilgrims at the rail — Lungs folk mostly, divers in their grey coats with their tags on cords at their throats, come up the long ladders before their shift to watch the number fall by however much their sweat had made it fall. Some of them touched their tags. Some of them said the response, the novices' response, under their breath.

*What are we?*

*The owed.*

*What do we owe?*

*All that was spent to bring us.*

*Why is the Door shut?*

*Because the account is open.*

*When will it open?*

*When the Count is zero.*

Above the board, carved into the metal of the vault itself in letters a hand high, ran the words the First Auditor had written at the head of the first page of the first Count, three and a half centuries ago, in the Starving, when there was nothing in the world but hunger and arithmetic:

COUNT WHAT IS OWED AND NOT A MARK MORE

Ysolde read it every morning. Every morning it steadied her. There was, she had always thought, a kind of mercy in it that the pilgrims did not see — a promise not only that the debt would be paid but that it would be paid *exactly*, that no one would be asked for one mark more than was owed. That the Count, whatever else it was, was fair.

She went down to the Third Chancery to begin her day.

* * *

The Third Chancery kept the tithe of the Lungs. That was to say it kept the record of every hour of work done by every diver, every refinery hand, every scrubber-tender and hull-welder and ice-cutter of the old warrens on Hesper's surface, converted to marks at the posted rate and entered against the Holy Number, and it was, by volume, the largest of the nine. Ysolde had kept the Third's Seventh Ledger for four years. It was a good ledger, fat with diver families from the deep spokes, and she knew it the way she supposed some women knew the faces of their children: every column, every recurring error, every household that always tithed a little late and every household that always tithed a little early, as though afraid.

She was Lungs-born herself. It was not a secret. The Auditory took up perhaps a dozen children a year from the warrens — tested them at nine for numbers and memory and something the testers called *steadiness*, and took the ones who passed up the ladders into the Nave, to be robed in grey and taught to keep the Count. It was the only way up. It was, the Lungs said, half proudly and half not, the only tithe that took the child instead of the hours. Ysolde had been taken up at nine with a fever of excitement and a new pair of boots, and she had wept for six nights in the novice dormitory without making a sound, and on the seventh night she had stopped, because weeping did not balance.

That was twenty-two years ago. She sent a third of her stipend down to Piet every month. She went down to see him on feast days. She did not, as a rule, think about the rest.

She was at her desk with the Seventh Ledger open before her and her pen moving down a column of figures from Spoke Eleven when the whispering started.

It started at the doors and moved inward, desk to desk, the way the Turning moved left across the board. She saw heads go together. She saw old Brother Callum at the Fourth Ledger put down his pen and put a hand over his mouth. And then Sister Imke, who kept the Second, leaned across the aisle to her with her face gone the colour of paper and said: "Anselm. Brother Anselm. He went to the Breath last night."

Ysolde's pen stopped.

"What?"

"He was down in the Lungs. At the Number Two intake. Inspecting, they're saying — a discrepancy in the refinery tithe — and he went too near the gallery rail, and." Imke made a small falling gesture with one hand. "They found his lamp on the grating this morning."

"That's not possible."

"Ysolde—"

"He didn't go down to the Lungs. He hasn't gone down to the Lungs in ten years, his knees—" She stopped, because Imke was looking at her strangely, and because she had realised she did not, in fact, know where Brother Anselm Ferrow went or did not go. She knew he kept the Ninth Ledger. She knew he was sixty-eight and stooped and had a dry, whispering laugh like pages turning, and that he had been kind to her, in a distant way, for all her years in the Third; that when she had first come down from the novitiate he had taken one look at her figures and said *ah, a counter,* and lent her his own copy of Brenner's *Tables*, with his pencil notes in the margins, and never asked for it back. She knew he ate alone. She knew nothing.

The Bailiffs came at mid-morning.

There were four of them, in the black coats of the Prelate's household, and a fifth in black with the silver chain of a captain across his chest. Ysolde knew him by sight: Mattias Strand, a long-jawed man with pale, patient eyes, who was said to have been born in the Lungs himself and to have chosen the black over the grey. He did not speak to anyone. He went directly to the Ninth Ledger's desk at the end of the Chancery and stood over it while his men opened the drawers and lifted out what they found — pencils, a cup, a folded scarf, a stack of loose reckoning sheets, a little tin of the peppermints Anselm always had and never offered — and placed each thing in a canvas sack. The Ninth Ledger itself, the great book, lay closed on the desk in its grey binding. Strand opened it, turned a few pages, closed it again. He did not take it. A Ledger was the Auditory's, not a man's; it could not go in a sack.

Then the Prelate came, and the Chancery stood.

Aurel Quist was seventy-one years old and walked with a cane of black wood that he did not seem to need, and he wore the white of the Prelate-Auditor as though he had been born in it and found it a little tiresome. He was a small man. Ysolde was always surprised by how small. He had a broad, lined, humorous face, and white hair cut short as a novice's, and when he came into a room the room did not so much fall silent as lean toward him, the way plants lean toward a lamp.

"Sit," he said. "Please, sit. Callum, sit down, you'll do your back an injury. Thank you." He stood in the aisle with both hands folded on the head of the cane. "You've heard. I'm sorry you had to hear it in whispers; I'd have come sooner, but I was with the intake wardens. Brother Anselm was found at the Number Two gallery in the small hours. He'd gone down to look at a refinery discrepancy that had been bothering him — you know how he was — and the gallery there is old, and the rail is low, and the updraft from the intake is very strong." He paused. "It would have been quick. I want you to know that. The Breath is quick."

Nobody said anything. Down the aisle, Imke was crying, without fuss.

"He kept the Ninth for thirty-one years," said the Prelate. "Thirty-one years without an unbalanced Reckoning. I don't think there's a keeper in the Nave who can say that, and I can't say it myself. He'd have hated a speech. So I'll only say that I'll miss him, and that the Ninth will need a keeper, and that he told me some time ago who he thought it ought to be." He turned, and his eyes found her. "Sister Marr."

Ysolde rose, because her body did it for her.

"You'll take the Ninth," said Quist gently. "Hand the Seventh to Brother Tomas; he's ready for it. I know it's a burden on top of a grief. He spoke very highly of you." He smiled, a little sadly. "He said you were a counter. I gathered from his tone that it was the highest praise available."

"Yes, Prelate," she said. "Thank you, Prelate."

"Don't thank me," said Quist. "It's a lot of arithmetic."

He blessed them, the small brisk blessing of the seal hand, the right hand raised with the thumb folded in, and went out. Strand went with him, and the Bailiffs with their sack. And the Chancery sat in the hush they left behind, and then, because there was nothing else to do and because the Count did not stop for grief, the pens began again to move.

* * *

The Ninth Ledger kept the tithe of the deepest spokes of the Lungs — Seventeen through Twenty-two, the old breather warrens down near the Drowned Decks, where the diving families had lived for longer than anyone had kept records of living. It was the oldest of the Third's ledgers in continuous use. The book on Anselm's desk was its two hundred and sixth volume. The two hundred and five before it were shelved in the Chancery stacks behind her, going back in grey and then brown and then black bindings to the first decades after the Starving, when the ledgers had been kept on whatever could be written on: Combine plastic, ration-wrap, the backs of manuals.

Every page of every one of them was headed, in the keeper's own hand, with the Holy Number:

**HV-V-0001**

Ysolde had written it at the top of perhaps sixty thousand pages in her life. She had been taught, as every novice was taught, that it was the number of the account — the one account, the account of all of them, which was held by the Combine beyond the Door and kept for the Combine by the Stewards, and for the Stewards by the Auditors, and which would be closed when the Count was zero. She had been taught that the letters were the Combine's mark and the zeroes stood for the nothing that had been here before the Combine came, and the one for the people the Combine had brought. She had never questioned it any more than she questioned her own name.

She worked late. It was not unusual. The Ninth was in good order, as everything Anselm touched was in good order, but a ledger changing hands had to be read through and signed for, and she wanted, she found, to read it properly — to read it the way he would have read it. She sat at his desk, which still smelled faintly of peppermint, with the lamp drawn down low over the page and the Chancery empty and dark around her, and turned the pages one at a time.

His hand was small, upright and perfectly regular. Each entry was a household, a spoke, a number of hours, a rate, a sum. Each page totalled at its foot. Each total carried. It was as clean a ledger as she had ever seen.

And in the margins, very faint, in pencil, he had been doing something else.

It took her a long time to see it. The pencil was so light, and the marks so small — a single figure here, two there, tucked against the gutter of the page where the binding curved and the lamp did not reach. At first she took them for checks, the little tick-sums every keeper made to catch his own errors. But they did not match the entries beside them. They did not match anything on the page. They were running totals of something else, and they ran, she realised, turning back and back and back, not page to page but volume to volume. He had carried them over from the last book into this one. He had been carrying them for years.

She went to the stacks with the lamp, and took down the two hundred and fifth volume, and the two hundred and fourth. The same faint pencil in the gutters. The same running figure, growing.

Not growing. Shrinking.

She stood in the stacks with a book open in each hand and the lamp hooked over her wrist, and read the pencilled figures at the foot of the last page of each, and did not understand what she was looking at.

It was a balance. It had the shape of a balance. It was a sum that began somewhere and was being steadily reduced, page after page, by the very tithes the ledger recorded — as though Anselm had been keeping, alongside the Count, a second Count of his own. But it was not the Count. The Count was nearly four billion. This was a number in the low millions. And it was not falling by the tithes alone; it was falling faster, because every so often, at each year's Reckoning, where the Count leapt up by the Interest of Sorrow and the Lungs went into mourning, Anselm's pencilled figure did not leap. It went on falling. It ignored the Interest altogether, and added instead, at each Reckoning, a much smaller amount, marked with a tiny symbol she did not know — a little circle with a stroke through it — and the letters *F.T.*

She took the books back to the desk. She did not know how long she sat there with them. She did the arithmetic three times, the way Anselm had taught her to do it when she was twenty, on a scrap sheet that she would afterwards burn: his starting figure, his rate, the tithes. She could not check his starting figure; she did not know where it came from. She could not check his rate. But she could check that if you began where he began and charged what he charged, and credited every tithe the Lungs had paid, then the numbers did what he said they did. They fell. They had been falling for a very long time.

It was past the middle bell when she found the leaf.

She had closed the two hundred and sixth volume and was turning it to put it square on the desk, the way he had always left it, and she felt it shift in her hands: something loose inside the spine, where the boards met the binding. She opened the book flat and bent the cover back, gently, and there it was, a single sheet folded into eighths and slid into the hollow of the spine where no one opening the book to read it would ever see.

She unfolded it under the lamp.

It was a reckoning sheet, the plain ruled kind every Chancery used by the thousand, and it was covered on both sides in Anselm's small upright pencil. It was the same work she had just been tracing through the gutters of three books, but complete: a single column of numbers from top to bottom of one side and halfway down the other, one line for each year, from a year marked simply *Founding* down to the present. The starting figure at the head of the column had a note beside it: *per F.B.* The rate at each Reckoning was marked *F.T.* And the column ran down, year after year, smaller and smaller, until about two-thirds of the way down the second side it reached a line where Anselm had stopped writing numbers.

He had drawn a box around that line, instead. Inside the box, in the same small, steady hand, he had written:

*Zero. Year 280.*

And beneath the box, alone in the white space, one more line, written harder, as though he had pressed down:

*And since?*

Ysolde read it four times. She did the subtraction without wanting to. It was the three hundred and forty-first year of the Count. Sixty-one years. Sixty-one Reckonings since Year 280, sixty-one Sorrow Days, sixty-one times the great digits of the Count Board had clattered upward while the Lungs wept and touched their tags. Sixty-one years of every hour of every diver going down into the red and coming up again, or not coming up, to pay into an account that — if Anselm was right, if *per F.B.* meant anything, if *F.T.* meant anything — had been closed before her mother was born.

She did not believe it. She found that she was shaking, and she did not believe it, and the two things did not seem to have much to do with each other.

There was something else in the hollow of the spine. She had to tip the book to get it out. It fell onto the reckoning sheet with a small flat click.

A tag.

It was a diver's tag, the kind the Lungs folk wore on cords at their throats — a half-disc of dull grey steel with a broken, toothed edge, worn smooth by generations of hands, its old stampings long since rubbed into illegibility. Every diving family had one or two. They were heirlooms, luck charms, things your grandmother's grandmother had worn into the red and come back from; Piet wore one that had been their father's. No one knew where the custom came from. No one knew what the stampings had once said. You simply did not dive without a tag.

But someone had scratched this one. On its smooth face, with the point of a knife, someone had cut a circle. A plain round O, deep enough to catch the lamplight.

Ysolde knew what it meant. Every auditor knew. It was the sign of the Nullists — the heretics of the deep spokes, the ones who said the Count was a lie and the debt was void, who scratched their circles on the walls of the Lungs' corridors faster than the Bailiffs could grind them off. *Zero,* the circle said. *Nothing owed.* Two years ago a Nullist cell had been found in Spoke Nineteen, and the Prelate's court had sent four of them to the Breath, and the Count Board had been draped in black for a day, out of sorrow, the Chancery had been told, for the souls of the faithless.

Tucked through the hole in the tag where the cord should have been was a slip of paper rolled tight as a pin. She unrolled it with fingers that would not quite work.

*Corin Asch,* it said. *The Lungs, Spoke Twenty. Ask him about the Interest of Sorrow.*

Somewhere above her, through the metal of the Nave, she heard the middle bell begin to ring: the long slow strokes that meant half the night was gone and half remained. And under it — so faint that at first she thought she was imagining it — she heard boots in the corridor outside the Chancery doors. More than one pair. Not hurrying.

She folded the reckoning sheet into eighths again. She put the tag inside it and the slip of paper inside the tag. She opened the collar of her grey robe and slid the whole small bundle down against her breastbone, where the wool was tight, and buttoned the collar again, and drew the two hundred and sixth volume of the Ninth Ledger square on the desk, the way Anselm had always left it.

The doors opened. Lamplight, and the shape of a man in black with a silver chain across his chest.

"Sister Marr," said Bailiff-Captain Strand pleasantly. "Working late."

"The Ninth changes hands tomorrow, Captain. I wanted to read it through."

"Very diligent." He came down the aisle without hurrying, and stood at the end of the desk, and looked at the closed book, and then at her. His eyes were the palest she had ever seen, nearly colourless in the lamplight. "We missed some of the Brother's personal papers this morning, I think. Loose sheets. Notes. He was known to make a great many notes." He smiled. "You haven't come across any?"

"Only the Ledger, Captain," said Ysolde, "and it's in very good order."

He looked at her for what felt like a long time. Above them the bell went on ringing, stroke after slow stroke, and she counted them — she could not help it, she counted everything — and kept her hands still on the grey binding and her face as steady as the testers in the Lungs had once, long ago, written down that it was.

"I'm glad to hear it," said Strand at last. "Good night, Sister."

He went out. She heard the boots go away down the corridor, not hurrying.

She sat at Anselm's desk until the lamp burned low, with the dead man's sums lying against her heart like a second, colder skin, and in all that time she did not look up at the high Chancery window where, if you leaned, you could see across the gulf of the Ledger Hall to the Count Board, black and white and enormous, keeping its number in the dark.


# Chapter 3: Tithe

*The Door · 3190*

The girl I took for the Margrave came back to kill me in the thirty-first year of the Door's breathing, and the first thing I did when I saw her was save her life, which tells you most of what you need to know about how much sense the world makes.

I was out in the skirt that day, picking. The skirt is what we call the junk that orbits loose around Lanthorn — the cast-off, the broken-off, the drifted-in — and picking is what I did instead of living, by then. You take your boat out along the edge of the ring shadow where Tarrow's rings throw their long dark stripe across the habitat, and you sweep for metal, and when you find a piece worth the fuel you grapple it and bring it home and cut it up and sell it in the Kettles for breath or bread or whatever the Kettles are selling that month. There are a hundred pickers on Lanthorn. Most of them are old. Picking is what you do when there is nothing else left that you're any good for, and nobody left who would hire you to do it.

My boat was the *Mendicant*. She had been a reeve's cutter once, in the days when I was a reeve, and I'd stripped the Margrave's colours off her and the gun off her nose and most of the comfort out of her cabin, and what was left was an old fast hull with good engines and a grapple arm that pulled to the left. I liked her. She didn't talk.

"Rook," said the radio. "You're drifting into my eyeline, you old fool."

"Morning, Haddie."

"It's afternoon. I've been at the glass since the ninth hour, and I've got a good clear look at the Door for the first time in a week, and now I've got a picker's arse in the middle of it. Move."

I moved. Haddie Coyle had been watching the Door from the top of Lanthorn's spine since before I was born, out of a glass dome bolted to the highest hulk in the lash with a telescope inside it older than the habitat. She was seventy-four that year. She smoked dried kelp-leaf rolled in ledger paper and drank whatever was going, and she had been, in her time, a diver and a smuggler and a teacher and a wife to two men and one woman, all of whom she had outlived; and she was the only person on Lanthorn who could read the old hand — the ledger hand, the dead square letters that you still find stamped on the oldest metal in the system — and she was the only person on Lanthorn who had ever looked at me, after I quit the reeves, as though I were still a man and not a story people told their children to make them go to sleep.

"How's she breathing?" I said.

"Wider."

"How much wider?"

There was a pause on the line, and the scratch of her lighter. "Two hand-spans on the year," she said. "Two, Rook. In the spring it was one and a bit. I've measured it three times this week and I'll measure it again tonight, but I don't think I'm wrong."

I looked up through the canopy. You can see the Door from anywhere in the Tarrow system if you know where to look, and everyone knows where to look. It hangs off the giant's trailing shoulder, far out, a coin of the wrong sky: a little round place where the stars are smeared, as though somebody had pressed a thumb on the dark and twisted. For most of history it didn't do anything. It was just there, the Door that was shut, the reason for everything, and you were born under it and grew old under it and died under it and it never changed. And then, thirty-one years ago, it had started — so slowly that it took Haddie four years of measuring before she dared say so out loud — to breathe. To open. A hair's width a year, and then a little more than a hair. Now two hand-spans, at the scale Haddie measured in, which I never understood and never asked about.

"What happens when it's open?" I said.

"Nobody knows."

"You must have an idea."

"I've got seventy-four years of ideas, and they're all worth what you paid for them." The lighter scratched again. "Go and pick something. You're making me nervous, sitting there in my eye."

I went and picked something. It was a girl.

* * *

The skiff came in from Kell-ward, low and fast, on a line that didn't make sense for anything that wanted to arrive anywhere. I saw it first as a flicker on the scope, a hot spot crossing the ring shadow with its drive lit, and then I saw the drive go out — not cut, the way a pilot cuts a drive, but stutter and spit and die, the way a drive dies when it's been run three hundred percent over its rating for too long and something inside it has finally melted. After that it was just a tumble. A small blunt shape spinning end over end through the skirt, heading for the edge of Lanthorn's lash at a speed that would have put it through two hulks and out the far side.

I should have let it go. You don't chase hot iron coming in off Kell; nothing good comes off Kell. But it was heading for the lash, and the lash was home, and there were people on the hulks it was going to hit. So I lit the *Mendicant* and went after it.

It took me forty minutes to match its tumble and another twenty to get the grapple on it without the arm tearing off. When I finally had it steady I could see what it was: a refinery skiff, the kind the Margrave's people use for running crews between the vents at Kell Deep, with her colours scorched off her hull and a long black score down one side where someone had shot at her and nearly hit. The canopy was fogged from inside. I couldn't see if there was anyone in it.

I suited up and went across.

The skiff's lock was jammed. I cut it. Inside was air — thin, hot, foul, the smell of a cabin that's been closed with a body breathing in it too long — and the body. Small. Curled up in the pilot's chair with its knees to its chest and its arms wrapped round something big and dark wrapped in a refinery blanket, the kind of grey felt they give the vent-runners at Kell to sleep in. A refinery coverall. Black hair cropped close, the way they crop the tithed so the hair won't catch fire. Hands—

I saw the hands and knew where she'd come from, if I hadn't known already. They were burned. Not freshly; old burns, healed over, the shiny ridged scar you get on the hands of the ones who work the vents with their bare palms because the gloves don't fit children and nobody at Kell Deep has ever bothered to make gloves that would. Every tithed child who comes out of the vents has hands like that. I've seen them on grown women and on dead boys. I've seen them in my sleep.

She was breathing. Barely. I got her out of the chair, and the bundle came with her because she would not let go of it even unconscious, and I got the two of them across to the *Mendicant* and into the lock, and then into the cabin, and then I got her helmet off — she'd had it on, open; she must have been breathing the cabin's dregs through it for hours — and I put her down on my bunk and turned on the heat and sat down on the deck beside her, because my legs had decided I was done standing for a while.

That was when I saw her tag.

The tithed wear them on a wire round the neck. They're not like the old divers' tags, the half-moons of steel with the broken edges that families hand down; they're new-made, stamped in the Margrave's foundry at Kell, a plain rectangle with a hole in one end. They say where you were taken from and which tithe you were taken in and your number in it. When I was a reeve I carried a bag of them blank and a stamp, and when the lots were drawn I stamped the tags and put them on the children myself, because the Margrave liked it done by hand. She said it made it real.

LAN-21-437.

Lanthorn. The tithe of the twenty-first year. Four hundred and thirty-seven.

I sat on the deck of my boat and looked at it, and I'll tell you the truth, because I've decided that in this account I am going to tell the truth even where it costs me: I did not have to look twice. I knew the number before I'd finished reading it. I have taken four hundred and nine children for the Margrave Kask in nine years of tithes, and I don't remember all of them. I'd like to tell you that I do, because it would make me sound like a better man. I don't. Some of them are just a weight. A small hand in mine on the gangway. A smell of soap somebody's mother scrubbed them with that morning, so they'd go clean. But I remember some, and I remember four-three-seven better than any.

She was nine. She had a gap in her front teeth. Her mother was a scrubber-tender on the Number Six hulk and she screamed at me all the way down the gangway, and the girl didn't scream at all. She just looked at me, the whole way, with her chin up. Like she was writing me down.

She was nineteen now, I worked out, sitting there. Ten years in the vents. Most don't last five.

I got up and got the medical kit and a bulb of water, and that was when she woke up and put my own knife to my throat.

* * *

I don't know when she got it. I keep it in my boot. I must have bent over her to check her breathing and she must have come up out of whatever dark she'd been in already moving, because the first thing I knew about it was a hand on my collar and a cold line under my jaw, and her face very close to mine, and her breath on me, foul with the skiff.

"Don't," she said. Her voice was a ruin; she'd breathed too much bad air. "Don't move."

"I'm not moving."

"You're Rook."

"Yes."

"You're the reeve. Absalom Rook. The Margrave's reeve on Lanthorn."

"I was."

The knife pressed. It was sharp; I keep it sharp. I felt the skin go, a little. "You don't remember me."

There's a moment, sometimes, when the right lie is sitting there in front of you like a tool on a bench, and all you have to do is pick it up. *No. I don't.* I could have said it. I don't know that it would have saved me. I don't know that anything would have; I don't know, to this day, how close she came. But it was there.

"You're four-three-seven," I said. "Twenty-first year. Your mother worked the scrubbers on Six. You had a gap in your teeth. You didn't cry."

The knife didn't move. Her eyes did. They went very wide, and then very narrow, and something came into them that I'd seen in a lot of eyes, over the years, and never got used to: the look of somebody who has been carrying a thing for a long time and has just discovered it weighs more than they thought.

"Her name was Lise," she said. "My mother."

"I didn't know that."

"She's dead. She died two years after. Somebody came to Kell on a crew run and told me. Scrubber lung." The knife pressed. "Did you know that?"

"No."

"Did you ever find out? About any of them? After?"

"No," I said. "I made a point of not finding out."

She stared at me. I stayed very still. I could feel blood running warm down into my collar, not much, and the hum of the *Mendicant*'s heater, and under that, from the bundle in the felt blanket lying on the bunk behind her, something else — a sound so low I'd taken it for the engines. A kind of purr.

"My name's Wren," she said. "Nobody's asked. At Kell they call us by the number. But it's Wren."

"Wren."

"I came to kill you."

"I gathered."

"I came to kill you," she said again, as though I hadn't understood. "I've been going to kill you for ten years. I used to lie in the dormitory at Kell and work it out. How I'd get out, how I'd get here, how I'd find you. Whether I'd do it fast. I used to put myself to sleep with it." Her hand on my collar was shaking now; the knife wasn't, quite. "And then I got out, and I came here, and my drive blew, and the first person who came for me was you."

"I didn't know it was you."

"Would you have come?"

I thought about it. I owed her that.

"I don't know," I said.

She laughed. It was a horrible sound, cracked and wet, and it went on too long, and in the middle of it the knife came away from my throat and she sat down hard on the edge of the bunk with it still in her fist and put her face in the crook of her other arm.

I didn't move for a while. Then I put my hand to my neck, and it came away red but not too red, and I took the bulb of water I'd dropped and held it out to her, and after a while she took it.

* * *

She told me about the bundle when she'd drunk the water and a second bulb and eaten half a brick of the kelp cake I keep for myself and don't like, and when I'd taped the cut on my neck and she'd watched me do it without offering to help. She still had the knife. I didn't ask for it back.

"The Margrave's salvage crews caught it," she said. "Three weeks ago. Out by the Door."

"Caught what?"

She pulled the felt back.

I'm not going to be able to describe it properly. I've tried, since, for people who never saw it, and I always end up making it sound like a stone or a fruit, and it wasn't either. It was black. Not painted black, not dark metal: black the way the inside of a hole is black, so that your eye slid off it and couldn't find the edges. It was the shape of a seed, or a teardrop, or the head of a spear with the point blunted, about as long as my forearm and as thick as a child's skull. And it was warm. I could feel it from where I sat, a slow even heat like a body's. And it hummed. That was the purr I'd heard. It hummed so low you felt it in your chest more than your ears, a sound that seemed to come and go, very slowly, like breathing.

"It came out of the Door," said Wren. "That's what they said. One of the Margrave's tugs was out there measuring — she has people watching it too now, did you know? Like your old woman on the spine. Since it started going faster. And this came out. Just came out, like it was spat. They went and got it. They brought it back to Kell and put it in the vault under the Margrave's house and nobody was allowed to go near it except her people and her."

"And you."

"I work the vents under the house. The vents under the house run past the vault. I've been in those vents since I was nine." She looked at her hands, the shiny burn-ridges. "I know every vent at Kell Deep. I know which ones are hot and which ones you can crawl. There isn't a wall there I can't get behind."

"So you stole it."

"I heard her talking. Kask. She was in the vault with it and two of her people, and they didn't know there was anybody in the vent. She said—" Wren stopped, and her mouth went thin. "She said, *This thing is worth more than every brat in the Deep put together, and more than every soul on Lanthorn, and I am going to find out who it belongs to and sell it to them.* She said it like that. *Every brat.* And I thought: if it's worth more than all of us, then whoever has it can buy all of us. And that night I went back in the vent with a cutter and a blanket."

"That's very stupid," I said.

"Yes."

"She'll have every boat she owns looking for it."

"Yes."

"She'll have every boat she owns looking for *you*."

"I know." She looked at me. "That's why I need you."

I'll admit I didn't see it coming. I'd been waiting, I think, for her to pick up the knife again. "Me."

"You know her patrols. You flew them. You know the dark routes and the old picket lines and the places her boats can't follow. You know where she keeps her ships and how fast they are and who flies them. I was going to make you tell me all of it," said Wren, "before I killed you. That was the plan. I was going to make you fly me somewhere safe and then I was going to do it." She shrugged, an awful, tired, nineteen-year-old shrug. "I don't know what the plan is now."

"What's the thing for?" I said. "You've got it. What do you want it to buy?"

She looked at me as though I were simple.

"The Deep," she said. "The children. There's three hundred and twelve in the vents right now. There's sixty in my dormitory. Some of them are nine. I want to trade it for them." She held my eye. "All of them. Every brat."

* * *

I called Haddie because I didn't know what else to do. I'm not proud of that either. I was fifty-two years old and I'd been a reeve and a soldier and a father, and there was a tithed girl with my knife on my bunk and a thing from the Door humming on my blanket, and the only thing I could think of was to call an old woman on the radio and ask her what to do.

She came out in her own boat, a little puttering skimmer she'd flown since before the breathing, and docked at my lock, and came aboard in a cloud of kelp smoke, and stopped dead in my cabin doorway with her mouth open.

"Haddie," I said. "This is Wren."

"I'm sure she is," said Haddie, not looking at Wren at all. She was looking at the thing on the blanket. She was looking at it the way I imagine people once looked at their gods. "Where did you get that?"

"It came out of the Door," said Wren.

"Of course it did," said Haddie softly. "Of course it did. Look at it." She came closer. She put her hand out, not quite touching. "It's warm."

"It's been warm the whole time," said Wren. "It's been humming. It got louder when I came past the ring shadow. When I could see the Door again."

"It's listening," said Haddie. "Or looking. Or—"

The humming stopped.

It just stopped, the way a held breath stops. The silence in my cabin was enormous. And then, from somewhere inside the black seed, so clear and close that all three of us flinched back from it, a voice began to speak.

It was a woman's voice, or the shape of one. Calm. Even. Unhurried. Perfectly courteous, the way the Kettle traders are courteous right before they rob you. It spoke for perhaps a minute without pausing, and I didn't understand a word of it. The sounds were almost our sounds — I could hear things in it that were nearly words, the way you can hear nearly-words in water running — but they came in the wrong order and the wrong shapes, as though our own talk had been taken apart and put back together by someone who had only read about it. Then it stopped. There was a pause exactly as long as a breath. And then it began again from the start, word for word the same.

Wren had the knife up. I hadn't seen her move. "What is it saying?"

I looked at Haddie. Haddie's face had gone grey under its seams, and her lips were moving very slightly along with the voice, and her hand, the one she had put out toward the thing, had closed slowly into a fist.

"Haddie."

"It's ledger-tongue," she said. "The old hand. Spoken." She swallowed. "I've never heard it spoken. I didn't know anyone could. I've only ever—I've only read it. On metal. In the old Count-books." She listened. The voice went on, even and kind. "It's very old. It's older than the books."

"Can you understand it?"

"Some. Not all. Not yet." She closed her eyes. "Be quiet. Both of you. Let me listen."

So we were quiet. The three of us sat in the cabin of my boat in the shadow of Tarrow's rings, with the heater humming and the Door hanging out there off the giant's shoulder like a coin of wrong sky, and listened to the thing that had come out of it say the same words again and again in its patient, pleasant voice. The fourth time through, I saw Haddie's lips stop moving. The fifth time, I saw her open her eyes, and look at Wren, and then at me, and I saw that whatever she had understood, she did not want to say it.

"Well?" I said.

"There's a word it keeps saying," Haddie said. "Near the start. And again at the end." She wet her lips. "It's an old word. It's in all the Count-books, on the first page. Nobody uses it any more. I don't think anybody's said it out loud for a long time."

"What word?"

The voice reached the end, and paused for a breath, and began again.

"*Account,*" said Haddie Coyle.


# Chapter 4: Liquid Assets

*The Bond · 2431*

He found her, in the end. She had spent two days trying to work out how to find him — a clerk in a grey jacket, in a station she was allowed to enter once a month for fifteen minutes under guard — and on the third day, coming off a short dive with a quarter-draw and no downdraft and nothing to dump, she went to rack her bottles in the Number Four tally house and found Siggard gone and a stranger at the weighing line with an auditor's slate.

"Combine audit," he said, to the room, not to her. "Routine. Racks one through twenty, please. Tally-master Siggard has been asked to wait in the office." He did not look at her once while he read her bottles. He read them slowly and carefully, touching each with his own wand, and when he came to the last one he wrote something on his slate and said, in exactly the same flat audit voice, "Bond H-11873, there's a discrepancy on your rack. Bring your bottle tags to the back store, please."

There was no discrepancy. Every breather on the line knew it, and a few of them looked at her the way you look at someone being called out of a crowd by a Combine man: sorry, and glad it wasn't them. She went through to the back store.

It was a cold cave cut into the ice behind the tally hall, stacked to the ceiling with empty bottles waiting to be cleaned, and it smelled of struck matches. He came in a minute after her and shut the door and stood with his back to it, breathing hard, as though he had run.

"You got it," he said. "The note."

"I got it."

"I didn't know if you'd understand. I didn't know if you'd tell someone. I didn't know—" He put a hand over his face. "Sorry. I haven't slept. My name's Lucan Vey. I'm in the Factor's office. Actuarial."

"I don't know what that is."

"I work out what things are worth," he said. "What people are worth. How long they'll live and how much they'll bring up before they die and what it costs to keep them alive in between. I've been doing it for four years. Your mother's on my tables. She's under *Expired, debt transferred.* So are you, under *Active.* I know your draw rate and your lung function and how many years you've got left at your current rate of exposure, and the answer's about nine." He took his hand down. His eyes were red-rimmed. "I'm sorry. That isn't why I came. I'm telling you so you'll understand that I'm not guessing about any of this. I'm the one who does the sums."

"What sale," said Maren.

He took a sheet of paper from inside his jacket. It was folded small, and when he unfolded it she saw that it had been printed and then cut down with a blade, the edges uneven, as if he had taken only what he dared from something larger. He held it out to her, and she took it, and she read it standing in the cold with the bottles rising round her like the pipes of some enormous dead organ.

> **HALLORAN-VASQUEZ COMBINE — VIGIL ACCOUNT HV-VIGIL-0001**
> **SCHEDULE C: LIQUID ASSETS FOR TRANSFER**
>
> Surety contracts, Hesper Nursery (412). Consigned to Brannock Deepworks Ltd., Corvid System, under master sale agreement BD-2431-07. Delivery on arrival. Transfer price per contract at schedule rate plus aptitude and health premium (Appx. 4).
>
> Total consignment value: 31,880,500 marks.

Underneath, one line had been cut from somewhere else and pasted on — she could see the faint edge of the strip — and it read:

> 0212 | TULL, P. | F | 12y | Health A | Aptitude A+ (numeric) | 88,400

She read it three times. She read the number at the end of Pell's line, and the word *numeric* in its little brackets, and she understood all at once, with the cold of the store going into her through the soles of her boots, exactly why her daughter was worth more than the girl on the line above her and the boy on the line below.

"What's Brannock Deepworks," she said.

"Mines," said Lucan. "In Corvid. Deep-crust mines on a heavy world — two and a half gravities. They use children in the narrow galleries, because children fit, and because their bones are still soft enough to take the weight if you start them young." He was looking at the floor. "Brannock buys indentures from all over the Combine. They pay a premium for aptitude because they train the clever ones as gang-tallies. Overseers. Pell would — she'd be kept out of the galleries, probably. With an A-plus. She'd be put to counting the others."

Maren was silent. She found that she had folded the sheet of paper in half and then in half again, small, and that her hand around it was a fist.

"There's more," said Lucan.

"Tell me."

"There's no rotation convoy," he said. "There's no convoy after this one. The *Grace* isn't coming back. Nothing's coming back." He made himself look at her. "The pins are failing. The Throat's drifting — it's been drifting for ten years — and to hold it open, the pins burn more breath every year, and the breath costs more every year to bring up, and last year the Vigil account ran at a loss for the first time since the colony was founded. The board in Halloran voted in the spring. They've invoked the Exodus Clause."

"I don't know what that is either."

"It's a clause in the colony charter. Every Combine colony has one. It says that when an account can no longer be profitably maintained, the Combine may withdraw its personnel and its liquid assets and place the account in Recovery." He said it like a man reciting. "The *Grace* takes the staff. All of us, from the Factor down. The *Grace* takes the breath stockpile — there's eight months of it in the tanks at High, enough to run the pins for the passage and sell the rest at Corvid. And the *Grace* takes the liquid assets. Schedule C. That's the children. They're the only thing on Vigil that's worth more than it costs to move."

"And us."

"You're not liquid," said Lucan. "You're fixed."

The store was very quiet. Out past the door she could hear the weighing line, the click of wands on bottles, someone laughing at something.

"The engineers give the pins eighteen months without fresh breath," he said. "Maybe two years. After that the Throat narrows. It doesn't close, exactly; it just stops being something a ship can pass. And after that—" He shrugged, a small helpless jerk of the shoulders. "Recovery. The account goes on the books as abandoned, and a lien's filed, and if the Throat ever opens again, the Combine sends a receiver to collect what's owed. They never close an account. Not really. They just stop coming."

"How long have you known."

"Four months." He said it to the floor. "I knew the Clause was being drafted. I didn't know about Schedule C until six weeks ago, when they asked me to price it. I priced it. That's my job. And then I went to my section chief and I asked—" He laughed, a short, terrible sound. "I asked whether the aptitude premium was *appropriate*. That was the word I used. I asked whether, given that the parents had been told it was a scholarship, the premium might be *reputationally sensitive*. And he looked at me for a while and then he said I was clearly very committed to Vigil, and it was good to have people who were committed to Vigil, because the Clause required a caretaker detail."

"A what?"

"Twelve officers," said Lucan. "To stay behind. Under sealed orders. To keep the account in order until Recovery." He looked up. "They put me on it the next morning. I'm staying, Mrs. Tull. When the *Grace* goes, I'm staying here with you. Eleven others and me, in an empty station, with thirty thousand people in the ice below who don't know yet what's been done to them." His voice cracked. "How long do you think we'd last, once they knew?"

So there it was. She had wondered, and now she knew, and it made her trust him a little more rather than less, because it was a reason she understood.

"You want them to know now," she said. "While you've got a chance to get on that ship."

"I want—" He stopped. "Yes. All right. Yes. I want there to be some other way this goes. I don't know what it is. I'm not brave. I'm not a— I don't know anything about—" He gestured, a vague sweep that took in the store, the Sump, the ice, the bonds. "There's a union. Isn't there? Down here. There's a woman who runs it. Everyone in the Factor's office knows her name, they joke about her. Hatch."

"I'm not union."

"I didn't think you were. That's why I came to you." He took a breath. "I asked around the tally lists for a breather with a ward on Schedule C who wasn't on the security watch. Somebody they'd believe. You're the best diver in the Sump, Mrs. Tull. You've never been union, never been disciplined, never been late on a tithe. People listen to you because you've never asked them to. If you take that sheet to Hatch, it's real. If I take it, it's a trap."

"And if they find it on me?"

"Then you'll tell them I gave it to you," said Lucan Vey simply, "and they'll put us both out of an airlock, and you'll have had a worse week than you were expecting." He put his hand on the door. "I have to go back. I've been in here too long for a discrepancy. Siggard will talk."

"Wait."

He waited.

"Why'd you write 0212," she said. "On the note. Why her? Why not just say a sale?"

He looked at her for a moment as though he did not understand the question.

"Because I knew you'd ask the price," he said. "Anybody would. And I thought — if you knew it — you wouldn't be able to stop."

* * *

She tried the honest way first. She owed herself that.

The bond office was on Level One, near the shuttle docks, a long counter behind a wire screen where Combine clerks sat at slates and dealt with the bonds' paper: transfers, inheritances, deaths, the endless small adjustments of the debt. Maren had been there perhaps a dozen times in her life. Once at nineteen, the day after her mother died, to press her thumb and take the debt. Once at thirty-two, the day after the accident on Number Two head that had cracked the *Gannet*'s first hull and put eighteen thousand marks of repairs on her bond in an afternoon, taking it over the forty-thousand line; she had stood at this counter and been told, very kindly, that the bond now required surety, and that her daughter would be collected from the crèche on Level Four that evening. She remembered the clerk's face. She remembered that he had had a cold, and kept apologising for it.

The clerk today was a woman she did not know, with short grey hair and a manner of terrible patience.

"I want to buy out a surety," Maren said.

"Ward number?"

"0212."

The woman tapped. Her face changed very slightly — a flicker, there and gone — and she tapped again. "That contract's committed."

"Committed to what?"

"The Scholarship." The woman looked up. "I'm sorry. All Scholarship contracts are committed as of the announcement. They're not available for buyout."

"I haven't told you what I'm offering."

"It doesn't matter what you're offering."

"I'm offering," Maren said, "everything. I'll take double draws. I'll dive the deep seams. I'll sign over the *Gannet*. I'll sign over my own bond — put it on her contract, put it all on me, I don't care what the number is. Tell me the number."

"There isn't a number," said the clerk. Not unkindly. Almost gently. "That's what committed means. It means it's not for sale."

Maren looked at her through the wire.

"It's for sale," she said. "It's just not for sale to me."

The woman's face did not change. But she looked, for a moment, down at her slate, and then up again, and in that look Maren saw that she knew. Maybe not all of it. Maybe not Brannock and the narrow galleries and the aptitude premium. But enough. Everyone in the Combine knew enough.

"Is there anything else, bond?" the clerk said.

"No," said Maren. "Count what's owed."

The woman's mouth moved, and for a second Maren thought she was going to answer — *and not a mark more* — the way anyone in the Sump would have. But she was not of the Sump. She only nodded, and called the next in line.

* * *

Ostrander's was a bar in the sense that it sold drink and had a counter. It was a cave on Level Six that had been a coolant pump room once, and still had the pumps in it, dead and huge, and the drinkers sat on the pump housings and on crates and on the floor, under a ceiling that dripped. It sold a spirit that Ostrander made himself from hydroponic waste, and the spirit was the colour of weak tea and tasted of solvent and was the only thing in the Sump, apart from children, that nobody had ever found a way to put on a bond.

Dov was where he always was, on the second pump housing from the door, with a cup between his hands and his back against the cold iron. He looked up when she came in and his face did a thing it had done for thirty years — lit up, and then caught itself, and settled into a kind of rumpled pleasure that pretended to be less than it was. He was fifty-five that year and looked seventy. He had flown tender for Maren's mother for twenty years and for Maren for eighteen, and in all that time she had never once seen him sober at Ostrander's or drunk on the dive line. He kept the two worlds as separate as a man could. It was, she sometimes thought, the most disciplined thing about him.

"Starboard six," he said. "I put a request in. They say a week."

"Let it wait."

"It can't wait, it's a frame member, you can't dive on a cracked frame—"

"Dov." She sat down next to him on the housing. "Give me some of that."

He gave her the cup without a word, which was how she knew she must look bad. She drank. It went down like a burning wire.

She told him. She did not show him the paper; she had not shown the paper to anyone, and it was inside her coverall against her skin, folded small, where Pell's tag hung on its cord. But she told him what it said, all of it, in a low voice with her head close to his, while around them the bar went on drinking and dripping and somebody at the back sang the first two lines of the breathers' hymn and gave up.

When she was done he was silent. He took the cup back and looked into it. His hands, she saw, were shaking, which they never did.

"Brannock," he said at last.

"You know it?"

"Everyone knows it. Everyone my age. They used to say it, when I was a boy — the old ones — when you were bad. *You'll go to Brannock.*" He drank. "I thought it was made up. Like a story."

"It's not made up."

"No." He put the cup down on the housing between them, very precisely, the way he put the *Patience* down in a cradle. "How many seats on the *Grace*?"

"What?"

"The *Grace*. How many does she carry? If she's taking the staff and the breath and four hundred kids—" He was frowning, doing the sum. "That's a big ship, but it's not that big. There'll be crew quarters. Escort berths. There'll be room."

"Room for what?"

"I don't know." He laughed, without humour. "I don't know, Maren. I'm just thinking out loud. I'm an old drunk sitting in a hole in the ice thinking about ships." He picked the cup up again. "We could run. You know that? The *Patience* could make the Throat. She's slow but she's got the legs for it. Six days at a steady burn."

"And then what? We've got no transit codes. No papers. The pickets would burn us before we got within a thousand klicks of the mouth."

"I know."

"And Pell would still be on the *Grace*."

"I know. I said I was thinking out loud." He drank. "I flew for your mother twenty years," he said. "Did I ever tell you about the day you were born?"

"About a hundred times."

"She dove that morning. She was eight months gone and she dove anyway, because it was a good seam and she was behind on the bond, and she came up with a full draw and her waters broke in the cradle. Right there in the *Patience*'s cradle. And I had to fly her home with her screaming in my ear and me screaming back at her that it was her own damned fault, and you came out in the shuttle lock at Level Two with me holding the light." He was looking at the dead pumps. "You were the ugliest thing I ever saw. You looked like a boiled rat."

"You've said."

"And she held you up, in the lock, all wet, and she said—" He stopped. His mouth worked. "She said, *Count what's owed, Dov.* That's what she said. First thing. And I said, *And not a mark more.* And she said, *No. Look at her. Count what's owed.*" He shook his head. "I never knew what she meant by it. I never asked."

Maren took the cup out of his hands and finished it.

"I'm going to Hatch," she said.

Dov didn't answer. He sat with his empty hands on his knees and his back against the dead iron and looked, for a moment, like a man trying to work out a sum that kept coming out different.

"Be careful," he said finally. "With Hatch. With all of them. Unions get people killed."

"So do scholarships."

"Yes," he said. "Yes, they do."

* * *

She did not go straight to Hatch. She went home first, to the two rooms on Spoke Nine, and sat on the bunk under Pell's eleven drawings with the heater ticking and not working, and took the folded paper out and smoothed it flat on her knee and read Pell's line again.

*Aptitude A+ (numeric).*

She had known since Pell was three. Pell had counted the drips from the ceiling of the old crèche on Level Four and told Maren, gravely, how many there would be by bedtime, and been right.
