At five she had done Maren's tally sheets for her, checking Siggard's arithmetic in a child's scrawl while Maren cooked, and found two errors in a month, and Maren had taken them to the tally house and been paid for them — the only time in her life the Combine had ever handed money back. At six, the day before they took her, she had asked Maren how much she was worth. Not as a joke. She had heard the word *surety* and looked it up on the crèche slate and wanted to know the figure. *Because if I know the figure,* she had said, *I can work out how long.*

Maren had not told her. She had not known. She had said: *You're not worth anything, love. You're mine.* And Pell had looked at her with that small, straight, considering mouth and said: *That's not how it works.*

Eighty-eight thousand four hundred.

She thought about the day her mother died. Ada Tull had died of the rattle at forty-six, in the clinic on Level Nine, drowning slowly in her own lungs while Sister Hatch sat with her and held a mask to her face that did nothing. Maren had been nineteen. She had sat on the other side of the bed. At the end, Ada had opened her eyes and looked at her and tried to say something, and couldn't, and had died with her mouth open on whatever it was. And the next morning a tally-man had come to the door of these two rooms with a slate, and Maren had pressed her thumb to it, and the slate had chimed, and she had owned her mother's debt.

She had never been angry about it. That was what struck her now, sitting on the bunk. Not once, in nineteen years, had she been properly angry about it. It was the weather. It was Tarrow. You did not get angry at a downdraft; you dumped your load and climbed. She had dumped and climbed and dumped and climbed, and kept her half of a steel disc on a cord against her skin, and held it up to the glass once a month to watch the other half line up with it, tooth to tooth, and believed — without ever once saying so to herself — that somewhere at the end of all the numbers there was a day when the two halves would be fitted together.

*That's not how it works.*

She folded the paper and put it back inside her coverall, and went out, and down.

* * *

The clinic was on Level Nine, at the bottom of the inhabited Sump, where the air was worst and the cold came through the sheeting in visible breath. It had been a medical bay once, in the colony's first years, and then a storeroom, and then for thirty years it had been Agathe Hatch's: twelve cots, a cabinet of drugs she bought or stole or made, and a curtain at the back behind which, everyone in the Sump knew and nobody said, the union met.

Hatch was sixty-four. She had been a nun once, in some order on some world that Maren had never heard of, and had come to Vigil on a mission contract forty years ago and somehow never gone home; she still wore the remains of a grey habit, cut down into a work smock, and she still had the nun's trick of looking at you as though she already knew what you were going to say and had forgiven you for it in advance. She was at the far cot when Maren came in, sitting with a boy of about fifteen whose chest was making the wet clicking sound of the early rattle, and she did not get up.

"Maren Tull," she said. "Sit down. You look like somebody died."

"Not yet."

"That's something." Hatch looked at her. "You're not sick."

"No."

"And you're not union. You've told me so to my face three times, I believe, over the years." The old woman's eyes were very pale and very steady. "So."

Maren took the paper out. She unfolded it. She did not hand it over; she held it out at arm's length, flat, and let Hatch read it in the cold light of the clinic, while the boy on the cot breathed his small wet breaths and did not wake.

Hatch read it twice. Then she reached out and took it, gently, out of Maren's hand, and read it a third time with her lips moving. When she looked up her face had changed. Not much. A tightening around the eyes; a set to the jaw that made her, for a moment, look less like a nun and more like what Maren supposed she must actually have been, all these years, behind the curtain.

"Where did you get this?"

"I'm not going to tell you that."

"Good." Hatch folded it. She did not give it back. "Is it real?"

"Yes."

"How do you know?"

"Because it's got my daughter's lung function on it," said Maren. "And her number. And her price."

Hatch was quiet a long moment. Down the row of cots, someone coughed and coughed and stopped.

"I've been waiting eleven years," said Sister Agathe Hatch at last, very softly, "for somebody to bring me a piece of paper like this." She got up. Her knees cracked. She went to the curtain at the back of the clinic and drew it aside, and behind it, in the dark, Maren saw a table, and chairs round it, and faces — four, five, six of them — turning toward the light.

"Come in," said Hatch. "Come in, Maren. You're union now."


# Chapter 5: Interest

*The Ledger · 2794*

Every child in the Lungs knew the story of the Interest of Sorrow, because every child in the Lungs had stood on Sorrow Day in the corridor outside the family's cell, in the cold, with their tag held in their fist, and listened to the Turning come down the speaker-wire from the Nave.

Ysolde had stood there herself, before she was taken up. She remembered her mother's hand on her shoulder. She remembered the sound — the clatter of the Count Board, faint and tinny down the wire, going on and on far longer than it did on any other morning, because on Sorrow Day the plates turned *up*. And she remembered the reading afterward, the Prelate's voice, old and slow, telling the story while the Lungs stood in their corridors and wept.

*In the hundred and fortieth year of the Count, in the time of the Third Prelate, the Great Rattle came into the Lungs, and a quarter of the owed died in a single winter, and their shares were left unpaid. And the Third Prelate went into the Reliquary, and fasted nine days, and came out, and said: For every soul that dies owing, a sorrow is added to the Count, that the living may carry what the dead cannot. And this is the Interest of Sorrow, and it is just.*

And then the response, from every corridor at once, a whisper multiplied by a hundred thousand mouths:

*It is just.*

She found the Third Prelate's edict on the morning after the Bailiff-Captain's visit, in the Edict Library under the Second Chancery, in a glass case where, according to the card beside it, it had lain for two hundred years. It was a single sheet of Combine plastic, yellowed and brittle, written over in a careful hand and sealed at the foot with a round black impression: the Prelate's seal. The text was exactly as she had heard it read every year of her life. She stood and looked at it through the glass for a long time, until the librarian, a mild old man named Brother Ruel, came and asked whether he could help her.

"I've taken over the Ninth," she said. "Brother Anselm's ledger. I'm reading back through the volumes, and the Interest lines in the second century are hard to follow. I wanted to see the edict."

"Ah," said Brother Ruel. "Anselm was here about the edict too, you know. Last year." He smiled sadly. "He asked the same thing."

"Did he?"

"He asked to see the binding records." The librarian nodded toward the far end of the library, where the shelves gave way to a row of iron cabinets. "The book-mending ledgers. Every rebinding in the Nave is entered there, you see, by volume and year. We're very thorough." He beamed. "He was very interested in the second-century rebindings. I told him there had been a great many. The old plastic doesn't last."

* * *

It took her four days to be certain.

She did the work in the evenings, after the Chancery had emptied, in the stacks behind Anselm's desk. She had the Ninth Ledger's oldest volumes, the black ones, down off the shelves and open on the reading table under the lamp: Volume Forty-one, which covered the hundred and thirty-eighth to the hundred and forty-second years of the Count, and Forty-two, and Forty-three. She had Brother Ruel's book-mending ledgers, which she had borrowed for *research into the material history of the Ninth*, a phrase that had made the librarian very happy. And she had a jeweller's glass, which she had bought in the Nave market for three days' stipend and which she kept in her sleeve.

What she found was this.

In Volume Forty-one, the entries for the hundred and thirty-ninth year were written in the brown soot ink that the Auditory had made from lamp-black in the first two centuries, before the iron mines on Kell were reopened. The ink had faded to the colour of dried blood. The pages were Combine plastic, like the edict, thin and yellow. The hand was a single keeper's hand, cramped and slanting.

The entries for the hundred and fortieth year were in the same hand. The same slant, the same cramped loops. But under the jeweller's glass the ink was black. Not faded black; black. The black of iron-gall, which the Auditory had begun to make in the two hundred and sixtieth year and had used ever since. And where the hundred and fortieth year began, the pages were not quite the same plastic as the pages before. They were a fraction thicker. A fraction whiter. And at the gutter, if you bent the book back as far as you dared, you could see that they had not been bound in with the rest of the gathering. They had been sewn in afterward — tipped in on a separate thread, where a block of older pages had been cut away close to the spine. She could see the stubs of the old pages, a hair's breadth wide, still caught in the original stitching.

The Interest of Sorrow was on the new pages. All of it. Every Reckoning from the hundred and fortieth year onward, the great upward leap, the sorrow added — every one was in iron-gall ink, in a careful imitation of the dead keeper's hand, on pages that had been sewn into a book that was a hundred and twenty years old at the time they were made.

She turned to the book-mending ledgers. *Ninth Ledger, Third Chancery. Volumes 38 through 71. Rebound for preservation.* There was a date. It was in the same black ink.

*The 279th year of the Count.*

She sat back from the table.

She did the next part slowly, because she did not want to make an error, and because she was afraid of what it would come to. She took the official Founding figure, the one on the first page of every novice's primer — *Four hundred and six million marks, the cost of our bringing* — and she took the tithe totals from the Ninth's volumes, and from the Chancery summaries for the other eight Ledgers, which she could read from the stacks without signing for them; and she took the ordinary interest of the Count, the small fixed rate that every primer taught, the rate that had been charged before the Great Rattle. And she left out the Interest of Sorrow. She ran it, year by year, on scrap sheets that she burned afterward in the Chancery stove.

It took three nights. On the third night, at a little after the middle bell, the column ran out.

With the Founding figure the primers taught — not Anselm's figure, not whatever *per F.B.* meant, but the Auditory's own — and without the Interest of Sorrow, the Count reached zero in the three hundred and second year.

It was close enough to Anselm's *Year 280* to tell her that he had not been mad, and far enough from it to tell her that he had known something about the Founding figure that she did not. And it was thirty-nine years ago. Either way — his number or theirs — every hour of tithe that had gone into the Count since before Ysolde herself was born had gone into nothing. Into a hole. Into the Interest of a Sorrow that had been invented, out of nothing, with iron-gall ink, by somebody sitting at a table in this Nave in the two hundred and seventy-ninth year of the Count, looking at a column that was about to run out.

She thought about that person. She thought about them sitting at a table like this one, with a lamp like this one, and a dead man's book open in front of them, and a knife for cutting out the pages. She wondered whether they had been frightened. She wondered whether they had thought they were doing good.

She burned the last scrap sheet in the stove and watched the figures go brown and curl and catch. Then she went to the window of the Chancery, the high one, and leaned, and looked across the dark gulf of the Ledger Hall at the Count Board.

*3,907,0—*

She could not read the rest from here. She did not need to. She found that she knew it, to the mark.

* * *

The drop shuttle left the Nave's lower dock at the sixth bell every day and fell for forty minutes to the surface of Hesper, and Ysolde took it on the fifth day with a satchel on her shoulder and an inspection warrant in her sleeve, signed by her own hand as keeper of the Ninth, authorising her to visit Spokes Seventeen through Twenty-two for the purpose of acquainting herself with the households of her ledger.

It was a perfectly ordinary thing to do. New keepers did it all the time. She told herself so all the way down.

The Lungs began where the shuttle doors opened: a cold that came at you like a wall, the smell of the ice, and the noise — not the hush of the Nave but the hum of a hundred thousand people living close together in the dark, pumps and voices and footsteps and the clang of bottles in the racks. The corridors were cut into the ice, as they had been since the beginning, lined with sheeting that had been patched and repatched until it was more patch than sheet. Down every corridor, at every junction, someone had painted the Holy Number in white — HV-V-0001 — and under it, in a hundred hands, the response: *When the Count is zero.*

And among them, here and there, scratched into the sheeting with a knife and ground half-away by Bailiffs' grinders and scratched again: circles.

She went to Piet first. She had not meant to. Her feet took her.

He lived on Spoke Twelve, in a single room he shared with two other divers, and he was home, because it was his rest-shift, and he opened the door in his undershirt with his hair standing up and a cup in his hand and his face went from sleep to astonishment to a grin so wide it made her chest hurt.

"Ysolde!"

"Hello, Piet."

"It's not a feast day."

"No."

"Is something wrong? You look—" He peered at her. "You look like a keeper."

"I am a keeper." She tried to smile. "I've been given the Ninth. Brother Anselm's ledger. I'm on inspection."

"The Ninth! The deep spokes?" Piet whistled. "That's a big book. That's a promotion, isn't it? That's—" His face changed. "Anselm. The one who went to the Breath."

"Yes."

"I heard about that." He stood back from the door. "Come in. Come in, you'll freeze."

She sat on the edge of his bunk while he heated water on a coil and talked. He was twenty-two. He had been diving for five years, since he was seventeen, in an old patched skimmer called the *Dutiful* that belonged to a diving family on Spoke Eighteen and that he crewed for a share. He had their father's long face and their mother's laugh, and he had the beginning of a cough, a small dry catch at the end of every breath that he did not seem to notice and that she noticed every time. He talked about the *Dutiful*, and about a girl on Spoke Thirteen, and about a seam they had found last week that had nearly killed them and had been worth it. He did not talk about his tithe. She looked, while he poured the water, at the slate tacked to his wall where the Lungs folk posted their tally, and saw a red mark on it that she knew the meaning of.

"You're behind."

He glanced at the slate and away. "A bit."

"How much?"

"It's nothing. A bad month. We lost a dive to weather, and then the *Dutiful*'s scoops needed relining, and they put that on the crew share—" He shrugged. "I'll make it up. We've got a good seam."

"Piet."

"It's *nothing,* Ysolde." He put the cup in her hands. "Drink that. You're grey."

She drank it. It was hot and tasted of the coil. Her brother sat down on the other bunk, across from her, and leaned his elbows on his knees, and at his throat, on its cord, his tag swung forward into the light: their father's tag, a half-disc of dull steel with a broken toothed edge, worn smooth.

She looked at it, and thought about the tag inside Anselm's ledger with the circle cut into its face, and did not say anything.

* * *

Spoke Twenty was the second deepest of the Ninth's spokes, two levels above the Drowned Decks, where the cold came up through the floor and the sheeting on the walls was furred with frost. Corin Asch kept a bottle shop there, at the end of a dead-end corridor: a narrow cave with a counter and a back room, lined floor to ceiling with breath bottles in various states of repair, and a sign over the door that said only ASCH — BOTTLES — SEALS.

He was behind the counter when she came in, bent over a scoop valve with a lens in his eye, and he did not look up.

"Shop's shut."

"The door was open."

"Then shut it on your way out." He looked up. He took the lens out of his eye. He was a lean man of about forty-five, with greying black hair tied back and a face that had been handsome before the rattle had hollowed it out, and a long pale scar that ran down the left side of his throat from his ear to his collarbone; and his eyes, when they saw her grey robe, went flat and dark as the bottles on his shelves. "Ah. An auditor."

"My name is Ysolde Marr. I keep the Ninth Ledger."

"The Ninth's kept by Anselm Ferrow."

"Brother Anselm is dead."

"I know he's dead," said Corin Asch. "I wondered if you did."

She took the tag out of her sleeve, the half-disc with the circle cut into it, and laid it on the counter between them.

He looked at it for a long moment. Then he put his hand over it — flat, quickly, the way you'd cover a spark — and slid it off the counter into his own palm and closed his fist on it.

"Where did you get this?"

"It was in the spine of the Ninth Ledger. With a note that said to come to you." She held his eye. "And a reckoning sheet. With a column of numbers on it that comes to zero."

He didn't say anything. He looked at her, and she saw him weighing it — weighing her — and she stood still and let him, because she had been weighed before, at nine, by testers in this same ice, and she had passed.

"Back room," said Corin finally. "Now."

* * *

The back room was smaller than the shop and colder. There was a bench, a lamp, a heater that did not work, and more bottles — big ones, the full-draw pressure bottles that the skimmers carried, each as tall as a woman and wide enough that she could not have put her arms around it. Corin sat on the bench and did not invite her to sit, so she stood.

"He came to me two years ago," Corin said. "Your Brother Anselm. Came down on inspection, like you, with his warrant in his sleeve. Asked a lot of questions about the old diving families. Which ones went back furthest. Which ones still had their sticks."

"Sticks?"

He looked at her as though she had said something stupid, and then, slowly, as though he were remembering what she was. "You're Lungs-born. Aren't you? I can hear it, under the Nave voice."

"Spoke Eleven."

"And they took you up young."

"At nine."

"Then you'd not remember." He reached down beside the bench and lifted something up and held it out to her: a rod of black metal as long as her forearm, its surface covered from end to end with fine notched marks. Thousands of them, in rows. "Mark-stick. The old families keep them. Every dive your family ever made, every tithe paid, notched in. Father to son, mother to daughter. Mine goes back to before the Starving. Some go further." He turned it in his hands. "We don't trust paper, down here. Paper's kept up there."

"And Anselm wanted to see them."

"He wanted to compare them. Our notches against your books. He wanted to know if they matched." Corin's mouth twisted. "Mostly they do. Your people are thorough. But there's one thing on the sticks that isn't on your books, Sister, and one thing on your books that isn't on the sticks."

"Sorrow Day."

He went still. Then he nodded.

"Every family keeps a mark for the Reckoning," he said. "One notch, crosswise, every year, when the Count's read. On the old sticks — the oldest ones, the ones that go back past the hundred and fortieth year — there's a Reckoning notch every year. Regular as breath. And on some of them, round about the hundred and fortieth year, there's a second mark, beside the first. A circle." He touched the place on his own stick. "For the Rattle. For the dead. They marked their dead. But there's no Sorrow mark. Not for a hundred and forty years after. Not on any stick I've ever seen."

"When does it start?"

"Two hundred and eightieth year," said Corin Asch. "Give or take. My grandmother was a girl that year. She told my mother, and my mother told me: one year there was no Sorrow Day, and the next year there was, and the Auditors came down the ladders and said there always had been. That it went back to the Rattle. That it was in the books." He laid the stick across his knees. "And it was in the books. They showed people. Black on white."

"Iron-gall," said Ysolde.

"What?"

"Nothing." She found that she had put her hand against one of the great bottles to steady herself, and that the metal was very cold. "The ink was wrong. He found that. Anselm. He found the ink was wrong."

Corin looked at her.

"I lost a daughter," he said. "Tamsin. Did he tell you that? No. Why would he. She went down at fourteen to pay her share — they let them dive at fourteen, now, did you know? It used to be sixteen. They lowered it the year I was born. She went down at fourteen because the Interest put our family three years behind and the Bailiffs were talking about taking the shop, and she thought she could make it up, and she didn't come up again." He said it flatly, the way he might have read off a pressure gauge. "So when your Brother came down here and asked me about Sorrow Day, I told him. I told him everything my grandmother told me. And he went away and he did his sums. And a year later he came back and he sat where you're standing and he told me what they came to." He looked at her. "Do you know what they came to?"

"Zero," she said. "Year 280. By his figures."

"And by yours?"

"Three hundred and two."

He laughed. It was a short, terrible bark, half a cough. "You've done it yourself, then."

"Yes."

"And what are you going to do about it, Sister?"

She opened her mouth.

Out in the shop, the bell over the door rang.

* * *

Corin was on his feet before she had turned. He crossed the back room in two strides and put his eye to the crack in the curtain, and she saw his shoulders go rigid.

"Bailiffs," he said, very low. "Four. And Strand."

"Strand?"

"They're sweeping the spoke. They do it every few weeks, looking for circles. But Strand doesn't come on sweeps." He turned. His face was white under its grey. "He's looking for somebody."

"Me."

"Somebody saw you come down. Somebody's been watching you since you left that Nave." He was already moving — to the nearest of the great bottles, a full-draw tank on a wheeled cradle, dented and scarred. He put both hands to the cap and twisted, and it came away; it was not a cap but a lid, and the bottle was not a bottle. It was hollow. Empty. Dark inside, and big enough. "In."

"What—"

"Smuggler's tank. Pressure-rated on the outside, nothing on the inside. In, or they take you up the ladders and you go to the Breath like your Brother did." He held out his hand. "Now."

She climbed in. There was no other choice, and her body knew it before her mind did. The metal was freezing through her robe. She folded herself down into the dark, knees to chin, the satchel crushed against her chest, and looked up at Corin's face in the circle of light above her — and then the lid came down, and the light went out, and she heard it turn, and lock.

The dark was total. It smelled of old breath, struck matches and copper. She could hear her own heart. She could hear, through the metal, very faint and very far away, the shop door open and boots come in.

Voices. Corin's, raised, aggrieved: *—a licensed shop, I pay my tithe like anybody—* Another voice she didn't know. A crash, something falling, glass. Then a voice she did know, quiet and pleasant, that made her shut her eyes in the dark as though that would help.

"Mr. Asch. Good evening."

"Captain."

"We're looking for an auditor. A woman. Grey robe, about so high. Fair hair, worn up. She'd have come down on the sixth-bell shuttle with an inspection warrant." A pause. "She was seen on Spoke Twenty."

"I don't get many auditors."

"No? Brother Anselm Ferrow was seen on Spoke Twenty a number of times last year. On inspection."

"He bought seals. For his lamp."

"A great many seals," said Strand, "for one lamp." Footsteps. Coming nearer. Into the back room; she heard the curtain rings scrape. "What's in these?"

"Breath. Full-draw. Waiting on recertification."

"All of them?"

"All of them."

The footsteps came closer. They stopped. Something knocked against the side of her tank — a knuckle, or the butt of a stick — and the sound boomed in the dark around her like a bell, so loud that she bit through the inside of her cheek and tasted blood and did not make a sound.

"That sounds empty," said Strand.

"It's full," said Corin. "Breath's light. You know that. Your father was a diver."

There was a silence. It went on and on. In the dark Ysolde counted it, because she could not help counting, because counting was the only thing she had: eleven, twelve, thirteen, her blood loud in her ears, her mouth full of copper.

"So he was," said Strand at last, softly. "So he was." The footsteps moved away. "Take him."

"What? I haven't—"

There was a sound she did not want to understand, and then another, and then Corin Asch's voice saying something that was not a word. And then a lot of sounds, close together, for what felt like a very long time, and then boots, and the shop bell, and the door.

Silence.

She stayed in the tank. She did not know how long. Long enough for the cold to go all the way into her and stop hurting. Long enough to think, quite clearly and in order, that they had taken him, and that she did not know how to open the lid from inside, and that she might be in here until someone came to recertify the bottles, which might be never.

Then the lid turned, and scraped, and lifted, and there was light, and a face in it — Corin's face, with blood running from a cut over one eye and his lip split and swelling — looking down at her.

"They didn't take me," he said thickly. "They just wanted to be sure I knew they could." He held out a hand. "Up you come, Sister. They know you came. They don't know you're here. Next time they will."

* * *

She took the last shuttle up. She sat in the back of the cabin among the night-shift refinery hands with her satchel on her knees and her robe stained with the black grease of the inside of a smuggler's tank, and nobody looked at her, and she looked at nobody. Her cheek throbbed where she had bitten it. Corin's last words went round and round in her head like a wheel: *They know you came.*

The Nave was quiet at that hour. She walked the long ribbed corridors to the Third Chancery's dormitory wing without meeting anyone but a lamplighter, and turned the last corner to her own cell, and stopped.

There was a novice outside her door. A boy of eleven or twelve in the grey of the first year, with his hands folded in his sleeves and his eyes drooping, who had plainly been waiting a long time. He jerked upright when he saw her.

"Sister Marr?"

"Yes."

"I have a message, Sister. From the Prelate." He recited it carefully, the way novices recited everything. "The Prelate-Auditor sends his compliments to the keeper of the Ninth Ledger, and asks whether she would do him the kindness of dining with him tomorrow evening, at the eighth bell, in his rooms. He says—" the boy frowned, getting it right "—he says he has been wanting to talk to her about arithmetic."


# Chapter 6: Notice of Recovery

*The Door · 3190*

Haddie worked on it all night, up in her dome at the top of the spine, and Wren and I sat on the floor under the telescope and watched her do it, because neither of us could think of anywhere else to be.

The dome was the size of my cabin and twice as cold, and it was full of books. Not real books, mostly — not the kind you see in pictures, with paper and covers — but what we call books on Lanthorn, which is anything with the old hand on it that somebody has thought worth keeping. Hull plates. Lengths of bulkhead. Sheets of the brittle old plastic you sometimes find in the deep wrecks, crazed and yellow, covered in rows of square dead letters. And among them, wrapped in oilcloth on a shelf of their own, nine real ones: thick blocks of paper sewn between boards, swollen and stained, which Haddie had traded for over fifty years from pickers who'd found them in the Hulk and didn't know what they were. Count-books, she called them. The pages were ruled in columns and filled from edge to edge with tiny figures, and at the top of every single page, in the same square hand, was the same mark.

"That," said Haddie, tapping it with her pencil, "is what it keeps saying."

It was midnight by Lanthorn's clock. The herald sat on a folded blanket on her chart table, black and warm and purring in its slow breathing way, and every few minutes, without warning, it would fall silent and then speak its piece again in that calm, pleasant, inhuman voice. Haddie had a sheet of ledger paper in front of her, and she'd been writing on it since the ninth hour, crossing out, writing again. Her kelp-leaf had burned down to her fingers twice. She hadn't noticed.

"Read it to us," said Wren.

"It's not finished."

"Read what you've got."

Haddie looked at her over the top of her spectacles, which she'd made herself out of two lenses from a scope and a bent wire. She'd been looking at Wren like that, on and off, all night — a long, dry, measuring look, the look she gave the Door when she was trying to work out how much it had moved. Wren had looked back every time.

"All right," said Haddie. She picked up the sheet. "Mind, this is my best guess. Half these words I've only ever seen written, and some of them I've never seen at all, and the way it says things—" She shook her head. "It's like hearing a song you know sung backwards. But this is near enough."

She read it out.

*NOTICE OF RECOVERY.*

*To the debtors of account HV-VIGIL-0001, held by the Halloran-Vasquez Combine.*

*This account is in arrears. This account was placed in Recovery upon the closure of the passage. A receiver of the Combine has attended upon the passage since that date.*

*Upon the reopening of the passage, the receiver will attend upon the debtors. All assets of the account will be inventoried and recovered.*

*Debtors may present statements of payment, under the seal of an authorised officer of the Combine, for reconciliation.*

*Debtors are advised that resistance to recovery constitutes a further default.*

*This notice will repeat.*

She put the sheet down.

Nobody said anything for a while. The herald hummed. Out through the dome's scratched glass the Door hung where it always hung, a little smear of wrong stars on the black.

"What's a receiver?" said Wren.

"Somebody who receives," said Haddie. "What's owed."

"And who's the Combine?"

"I don't know." Haddie took her spectacles off and rubbed her eyes. "It's in the Count-books. Not much. The letters at the top — this, the H and the V — the old Count-keepers wrote it on every page, and there's a word for it in some of the oldest ones, and I've always read it *Combine*, but I never knew what it meant. A name. A family. A god. There are people in the Kettles still say it in their prayers, did you know that? The old believers. *For the Stewards, for the Combine.* They don't know what it means either."

"It means somebody we owe," I said.

They both looked at me.

"Well, that's what it says, isn't it?" I nodded at the sheet. "*Debtors.* That's us. *Assets.* That's ours. *Inventoried and recovered.* That's somebody coming through that Door with a list." I heard my own voice, flat and hard, a reeve's voice, and didn't like it. "I've done inventory, Haddie. I know what it sounds like."

Haddie didn't answer. Wren looked at me with her burned hands folded in her lap.

"What about the payment bit," she said. "Statements of payment. Under seal."

I laughed. I couldn't help it. It came out of me ugly, the way it does when you're tired and frightened and it's the middle of the night. "Statements of payment. Look around you, girl. Look at this place. We haven't got payments. We haven't got seals. We've got nothing on this whole rock but debts and the people who owe them." I stopped. "Sorry."

"Don't be," said Wren. "You're right."

She got up. She went to Haddie's shelf, the one with the nine real books on it, and stood looking at them, with her back to us. She didn't touch them. After a minute she said, without turning round:

"Can you teach me to read that? The old hand?"

Haddie put her spectacles back on.

"I can try," she said. "It took me thirty years."

"I haven't got thirty years."

"No," said Haddie Coyle, "I don't suppose any of us have."

* * *

You can't keep a thing like that quiet on Lanthorn. I don't know who talked — I don't think anyone did, exactly. I think it just leaked out of us the way air leaks out of the lash, through a hundred little seams nobody can find. By the sixth hour of the next morning half the habitat knew that the old door-watcher had something up in her dome that had come out of the Door and was talking, and that the Margrave's reeve was mixed up in it, and that there was a vent-runner from Kell Deep with him with burned hands and a knife.

Lanthorn is fifty-three hulls lashed together with cable and foam and prayers, in the long shadow of Tarrow's rings where the old wrecks drifted and fetched up over the centuries. Nobody built it. It happened. Some of the hulls are ships, and some were stations once, and some are just pieces of things — a great curved shard of something that was probably a fuel tank, a section of spine with half a word on it in the old hand, a cargo pod the size of a hill. Eleven thousand people live in it. They're divers and scrubbers and pickers and tenders, mostly, and they're poor, and they're proud of being poor in the way people get when it's all they've got to be proud of. And they're governed — if that's the word — by the Lash Council, which is the eldest of every hull that has an eldest, sitting round a table in the old cargo pod and arguing until somebody gives up.

The Council sent for us at the eighth hour. We went, because there wasn't anywhere else to go. Haddie carried the herald in its blanket. Wren walked beside her. I walked behind, and I felt the eyes on me all the way down the spine — on my back, on my face — the way I'd felt them every day since I took off the reeve's coat seven years ago. Nobody on Lanthorn ever forgot who I'd been. I never asked them to.

There were thirty-odd elders round the table in the cargo pod, and three times that many people crowded in behind them, standing, and the air was hot and close and smelled of breath and fear. Old Mother Yelland of the Number Two hull was in the chair that morning. She was ninety, and blind, and had been the eldest of Number Two since before the breathing.

"Haddie Coyle," she said. "They tell me you've got a voice in a box."

"Something like that, Mother."

"Let us hear it."

So Haddie unwrapped the herald and put it on the table, and it lay there black and warm in front of all those people, and after a little while, the way it did, it went quiet and then spoke. Its calm voice filled the cargo pod. I watched the faces round the table while it talked — confusion, mostly, and then fear, as the strangeness of it got into them; that voice that was almost ours and wasn't. And then, at the back, among the standing ones, I saw something else. I saw an old woman with her hands pressed together under her chin, and her eyes shining. And a man beside her with his lips moving. And another.

When the voice stopped, Haddie read out her sheet. She read it plainly, without any feeling in it, and when she got to the word *Combine* there was a sound from the back of the pod, a kind of gasp, and a voice — a woman's, cracked with age — called out:

"*For the Stewards! For the Combine!*"

"Be quiet, Dorrit," said Mother Yelland, without turning her head.

"It's the Return, Mother! It's the Return they always promised! The Door's opening and they're coming to—"

"*To recover the assets,*" said Haddie, loudly, over her. "That's what it says, Dorrit. I don't know what your grandmother told you, but this thing doesn't say a word about taking anybody home. It says inventory. It says recovery. It says *resistance constitutes a further default.*" She slapped the sheet down on the table. "Whoever's on the other side of that Door isn't coming to save us. They're coming to collect."

There was uproar for a while after that. I'll not set it all down. People shouting, people crying, people asking questions nobody could answer. Somebody wanted to know if the voice could be made to stop. Somebody wanted to throw it into Tarrow. Somebody — a young tender from Number Nine, I think — wanted to know why the Margrave's reeve was standing in the Council pod, and that got a lot of agreement, and I stood there and let it.

It was Mother Yelland who stopped it. She didn't raise her voice. She just put her two old hands flat on the table, and waited, and one by one they quieted down, the way they'd been quieting down for her for sixty years.

"Where did it come from?" she said. "Not the Door. Before that. Who had it?"

Nobody answered for a moment. Then Wren stepped forward.

"The Margrave," she said. "Kask. Her tugs caught it, out by the Door. It was in her vault at Kell Deep. I took it."

The pod went very quiet.

"Then she'll be wanting it back," said Mother Yelland.

* * *

She wanted it back within the hour.

I was on the *Mendicant* when they came. I'd gone down to the docks to fuel her — I don't know why; I told myself it was habit, but I think part of me already knew — and I was standing on her wing with the breath line in my hand when the general band crackled on the dock speakers and the voice came on that I'd heard every day for nine years and hadn't heard for seven.

"People of Lanthorn," said the Margrave Kask. "This is your Margrave."

I put the breath line down.

She had a good voice for it. She always had. Low and slow and a little rough, a voice like a hand on the back of your neck, and she never shouted. Teodora Kask had been Margrave of Kell Deep for twenty-two years that spring. She had taken the refinery from old Margrave Hesk with eleven reeves and a cutting torch on a feast night, and she had held it since by being the person in the system who decided who got breath and who didn't. Breath was heat. Breath was air. Breath was the drive that moved every boat between every habitat. You could live without bread for a week and without the Margrave's breath for about three days. She knew that, and so did we, and she never once had to say it out loud. I'd stood behind her chair for nine years and never heard her say it.

"Something was taken from my house at Kell Deep four nights ago," she said. "It is property of the refinery. It was taken by a tithed runner, number Lanthorn twenty-one four-three-seven, who has broken her tithe and fled. I'm told she is on Lanthorn. I'm told the property is with her." A pause, just long enough. "I'm told she is in the company of my old reeve, Absalom Rook."

Out on the dock, the fuel hands had stopped working. They were looking at me.

"I'm a reasonable woman," said the Margrave. "I don't hold a hulk responsible for the stupidity of one runner. Return the property and the runner to my reeves, who are waiting off your lash, and Lanthorn's breath ration for this quarter will be increased by a tenth, in gratitude. Keep them, and the ration will be suspended until they are returned." Another pause. "You have until the twelfth hour. I'll be listening."

The speakers clicked off.

I looked out through the dock's big port. There they were: two cutters, sitting off the lash a few kilometres out, just past the edge of the ring shadow where the light was. Margrave's colours — the white open hand on the red, which I'd worn on my own coat once and painted on this very boat. Reeves' cutters, newer than mine, with the guns still on their noses.

I knew who'd be on them. I'd trained half of them.

I finished fuelling the *Mendicant*. Then I went back up the spine at a run.

* * *

I'll be honest about the next part, because I've promised to be: the Council voted to give her up.

Not all of them. Not Mother Yelland. But most. By the time I got back to the cargo pod they'd been at it for half an hour, and I could hear them from the spine, and when I came in through the hatch I saw how it had gone. Haddie was standing at the end of the table with the herald in her arms and a face like a cutting edge. Wren was beside her, very still. And round the table, elders who'd known Haddie Coyle for fifty years were looking at their hands.

"It's breath, Haddie," one of them was saying — old Fenn, from the tank hull. "It's the quarter's breath. We've got babies on Number Six. We've got the sick bay. Three days without the Margrave's breath and we're burning furniture—"

"She's nineteen," said Haddie.

"She's *tithed*. She's broken tithe. That's the Margrave's law, it's not ours to—"

"She's *nineteen*, Fenn, and you're going to hand her to the Margrave's reeves for a tenth more breath. What do you think they'll do with her? What do you think the Margrave does to runners who break tithe? Do you want to ask Rook? He knows."

They all looked at me.

I did know. I'd done it.

"Mother," I said, to Yelland. "Is it decided?"

The old blind woman turned her face toward my voice.

"It's decided," she said. "I'm sorry, Absalom. I voted against. But it's decided."

"Then I'm sorry too," I said, and I took Wren by the arm and Haddie by the other and walked them both out of the cargo pod, and nobody stopped me, because I'd been a reeve for nine years and they all remembered it.

We'd got as far as the dock spine when they came after us. Not the Council — they'd not have dared. A dozen of the younger ones, divers and tenders, who'd decided that if their elders weren't going to make it happen they would. They caught us at the last junction before the docks, in the narrow place where the spine bends round the old tank, and I put Haddie and Wren behind me and turned round.

I'll not dress it up. I hurt some of them. I hadn't been in a fight in seven years and I'd hoped I'd never be in another, but it turns out you don't forget, any more than you forget how to swim or how to stamp a tag. I broke a boy's wrist. I put a tender's head into the bulkhead hard enough that she sat down and didn't get up. I took a pipe across the shoulders from someone I never saw and kept my feet. And the whole time I could hear Wren behind me shouting something, and Haddie shouting at her, and when it was done and the ones still standing had backed off down the spine, I turned round and saw that Wren had my knife out again, and that she'd cut someone — a big lad from Number Nine, across the forearm — and was standing over him with it, shaking.

"Boat," I said. "Now."

* * *

The cutters saw us the moment we cleared the lash. I'd known they would. I lit the *Mendicant* and put her nose straight down into the ring shadow, into the dark, and I heard Haddie in the seat behind me say a word I'd not have thought she knew.

"They'll follow," said Wren.

"Let them."

"They've got guns."

"They've got guns and new boats and young pilots," I said, "and I've got thirty years of picking this skirt and a ship that doesn't care if she gets scratched. Hold on to something."

The ring shadow off Lanthorn is the worst place in the Tarrow system to fly, and the best place to hide. The rings themselves are dust and ice and gravel spread so thin you could fly through them for a week and never touch a stone, but in the shadow, where the old wrecks have fetched up for a thousand years, there's everything else: hull plates, tanks, spars, whole dead ships tumbling end over end, and between them the fine black grit that gets into your sensors and makes your scope a liar. Pickers fly it by feel. The Margrave's reeves fly it by instrument, because they're trained that way — I trained them that way — and instruments, in the shadow, will kill you.

I lost the first cutter in eleven minutes. Took her through a gap between two tumbling tanks that she didn't believe was there, and she broke off rather than try it, and by the time she'd gone round I was gone. The second one was better. She stayed on me for nearly an hour, through the wreck-field off the old ore hulk and down along the dark edge of the A-ring where the ice gets thick, and twice she fired on me — warning shots, close across the bow, the way I'd taught them. The second shot came close enough that Wren cried out.

"Who is it?" Haddie said. "Who's flying that thing?"

"Benno Strick," I said. I could tell by the way she turned. "I trained him. He was eighteen. He used to bring me tea."

I took the *Mendicant* down behind a moonlet — a lump of ice the size of the cargo pod, tumbling slow in the ring — and cut everything. Drive, heat, lights, scope. Let her drift in its shadow, dead as any other wreck in the field. And in the dark, in the cold, with Wren's breath fogging in the seat beside me and Haddie's in the seat behind and the herald purring on the deck between them in its blanket, we sat and listened to the band.

After a long time — forty minutes, maybe — the band crackled. A young man's voice. "*Rook.*"

I didn't answer.

"*Rook, I know you can hear me.*" A pause. "*She says it doesn't have to go like this. She says bring it in yourself and she'll forget about the runner. She'll forget about you. She says she always liked you.*" Another pause. "*She says you of all people should understand what it's for.*"

I didn't answer.

"*Fine,*" said Benno Strick, and the band clicked off, and after a while I saw the faint glow of his drive go past above us, a long way above, heading back toward Kell.

* * *

We stayed behind the moonlet for six hours. I let the heater up a little, once I was sure, and we ate the rest of the kelp cake, and nobody said much. Wren sat in the co-seat with her knees drawn up and my knife in her hand and looked out at the rings. Haddie, behind us, unwrapped one of the Count-books she'd stuffed into her coat before we left and sat reading it by the light of a hand-lamp, turning the pages carefully with her bent old fingers, her lips moving. Every so often she would stop and say a word out loud — an old word, in the ledger-tongue — and then, after a moment, Wren would say it after her, slowly, getting it wrong, getting it a little less wrong.

At the fourteenth hour the general band came on again. All channels. Every speaker in every habitat in the system, I found out later; every boat, every dock, every cell. The voice I'd stood behind for nine years.

"People of the system," said Teodora Kask. "This is the Margrave of Kell Deep."

Wren went still.

"Some of you will have heard by now that something has come out of the Door. That's true. It came out three weeks ago, and my people caught it, and I have had it read." I looked at Haddie; she had stopped turning pages. *Had it read.* So the Margrave had her own readers, her own Haddie, somewhere in her house at Kell — of course she did. "I want to tell you plainly what it says, before you hear it from thieves and old women. It says that the Door is opening. It says that there are people on the other side of it, and that they are coming, and that they believe we owe them a debt."

A pause. She was so good at pauses.

"I don't know if that's true," said the Margrave. "Neither do you. But I know this: when they come, they're going to want to talk to someone. And it had better be someone who can bargain. Someone who holds something they want. That's me. I hold the breath. I hold Kell Deep. I will treat with the Creditor on behalf of every habitat in this system, and I will get us the best terms that can be got." Her voice softened. "But to bargain, I need to know what we have. So I'm asking every habitat, every hull, every lash, to prepare for me a full and honest count. Every soul. Name, age, health, trade. My reeves will come round to collect them. Those who help, will be remembered when terms are made. Those who hinder—"

She let it hang. She didn't need to finish. She never did.

"Stand together," said the Margrave Kask, "and I'll get us through this. That's all."

The band clicked off.

I sat in the pilot's seat of my old reeve's cutter, in the dark behind a moonlet in the rings of Tarrow, and found that my hands had gone so tight on the yoke that I couldn't open them.

"A count," said Haddie, behind me, very quietly. "Name, age, health, trade."

"Inventory," I said.

"She's going to sell us." That was Wren. She hadn't moved; she was still looking out at the rings. "Isn't she? That's what treat means. She's going to find out what they want and she's going to give it to them, and she wants the list first so she knows what she's got to give."

"Yes."

"Every brat," said Wren. She said it so softly I almost didn't hear. "She said every brat in the Deep. That's what she'll pay with first. That's what she's got the most of, that's what costs her least." She turned round in the seat and looked at me, and her face in the dim light of the board was the face of a nine-year-old on a gangway, chin up, writing me down. "There are three hundred and twelve children in the vents at Kell Deep, Rook. You put a hundred and forty of them there. I counted. I've had ten years to count."

"It's not my business any more," I said.

I said it the way you'd say a thing you'd been saying for seven years, every night, to the ceiling. It came out easily. It always had.

Behind me, Haddie closed her Count-book. I heard the soft thump of the boards coming together.

"Absalom," she said. "It was your business once."

I didn't answer her. I sat there with my hands on the yoke for a long time, with the two of them not saying anything, waiting, and the herald on the deck between us purring away in its dark warm patient voice like something that had all the time in the world.

Then I lit the drive, and turned the *Mendicant*'s nose out of the moonlet's shadow, toward Kell.


# Chapter 7: The Drowned Union

*The Bond · 2431*

The union met on the water.

Maren had not known that. In nineteen years of living above it she had never been down to the Drowned Decks — nobody went down there, it was said, except the pump crews who had given up forty years ago and the children who dared each other to — and when Hatch led her down the last service ladder on the ninth night, with a hand-lamp swinging from her wrist and her habit hitched up into her belt, she stopped on the bottom rung and simply stared.

It was a lake. The Sump's three lowest levels had been cut into the ice as warehouses, back when the colony was young and the Combine still imagined there would be things to store; and when the refinery heat began to seep down through the crust, the ice had softened and run, and the meltwater had found the warehouses and filled them. Now the three levels were one black still pool under a ceiling of dripping ice, and across it, a metre above the surface, ran the old cargo catwalks — iron grilles on iron legs, rusted orange, linking the stumps of the support pillars like stepping stones across a river. The cold was enormous. Her breath went up white and hung. And every sound — the drips, Hatch's boots on the grille, her own heart — came back off the water doubled, so that it seemed there were two of everything.

"Nobody listens down here," said Hatch. "The Combine put microphones on every level of the Sump the year I arrived. They never bothered with this one. They don't think anybody's mad enough to stand about on a rusty catwalk over forty metres of ice water." She looked back at Maren, and in the lamplight her old face was almost merry. "They're right, of course. But we're mad."

They were waiting in the middle of the lake, where four catwalks met on a square iron platform round the stump of a pillar: a dozen people, maybe a few more, standing close together for warmth, with lamps hung from the pillar's rusted bolts. Maren knew some of them. Ruth Calloway, who ran the scrubber crews on Level Seven and had the loudest voice in the Sump. Ade Okoro, Lin's husband, a refinery hand from Number Two head with arms like hawsers, whose boy was 0188 on the list. Old Pym, who had been a counter in the tally houses for forty years before his eyes went and who still knew every rate by heart. Farrant and Moss and the Ellery sisters, breathers all, who dove for the Number Three head and had always kept their distance from her because she was better than them.

And Dov. She had brought Dov. He stood a little apart from the rest, at the platform's edge, with his hands in his pockets and his shoulders up round his ears against the cold, looking down into the black water as though there were something in it he recognised.

She had argued with herself about bringing him. He was a drunk. He talked. But he was also the best tender pilot in the Sump and the only one who flew for her, and if the breathers walked out the tenders would have to walk out too, and the tenders listened to Dov the way the breathers were beginning to listen to her. And he was Dov. He had held the light when she was born. When she had told him, the night before, that Hatch had asked for him, he had looked at her for a long moment without saying anything and then nodded and said, *All right, girl,* and had come down the ladder behind her that evening sober, which she could not remember him being after dark in twenty years.

"You all know why we're here," said Hatch. "You've all seen the paper."

They had. Hatch had read it to each of them herself, one at a time, behind the curtain in the clinic over the past six days, and then burned the copy and kept the original somewhere Maren did not know and had not asked.

"Then you know what it means," said Hatch. "They're leaving. They're taking our children to sell, and they're taking the breath, and they're taking everything else that isn't nailed down, and they're leaving us here to die in the ice when the Throat closes. That's what *Exodus Clause* means. It means the Combine has decided we're not worth the price of the door." She looked round at them, one by one. "I've been doing this for thirty years. I've organised a dozen walkouts and three strikes and I've buried more of you than I can count. And I have never, in all that time, had anything I could use. Not once. The Combine's always had what it needed, and we've always had nothing, and that's why we've always lost." She smiled, a thin hard smile. "That's changed. For the first time since I came to this miserable moon, they need something from us."

"Breath," said Pym.

"Breath." Hatch nodded at Maren. "Tell them."

Maren hadn't expected that. She cleared her throat; it came back off the water at her, doubled.

"The *Grace* takes breath for the passage," she said. "To run her drives and to feed the pins while she's in the Throat. That's a lot of breath. And then she takes the stockpile on top, to sell at Corvid — that's what's in the tanks at High, eight months of it. But eight months isn't enough for both, not if she wants to make the crossing with full tanks and still have the stockpile to sell." She had got this from Lucan, two nights ago, in a hurried whisper at the back of the tally house. "They need another four weeks of draws. That's why the convoy's in forty days. They need us to keep diving right up until they leave."

"So we stop diving," said Ade Okoro.

"So we stop diving," said Hatch. "Every breather, every tender, every refinery hand. Every head in the Sump shuts down. No breath comes up. Nothing goes into the tanks. And the *Grace* can't leave without what's in the tanks, or if she does, she leaves without her stockpile and the board in Halloran finds out their Factor's lost them a fortune." She looked round. "And then we tell them our price."

"Which is?" said Ruth Calloway.

"The children," said Hatch. "Every ward on that list, returned to their parents before the *Grace* leaves. That's the first thing. That's the thing we don't trade."

"And then?" Calloway pressed. "Say they give us the kids. Then the *Grace* goes, and the Throat closes, and we've got four hundred more mouths to feed in a moon that's going to starve anyway. What then, Sister?"

There was a silence. The water dripped.

"Then we'll starve together," said Hatch. "With our children. I'd take that over the other."

"I wouldn't," said Farrant, the breather. He was a short, hard man of forty with the rattle already in his voice. "I'd take the *Grace*."

Heads turned.

"I mean it," said Farrant. "Why are we bargaining? There's thirty thousand of us. There's what, two hundred marines on High? Three? We go up on the shuttles, we take the docks, we take the *Grace*, and we take ourselves out through the Throat with our kids. Let the Combine sit in the ice and see how they like it."

There was a murmur, and Maren heard in it how much some of them liked the idea. She liked it herself, for a moment. She felt it in her chest like heat.

"With what?" said Hatch quietly.

"With—"

"With what, Farrant? Cutting bars? Scoop wrenches? There are three hundred and forty Combine marines on Hesper High, I've counted them, and every one has a rifle and armour and a station built to be held against exactly what you're describing. You'd get up there with your wrench and they'd cut you to pieces in the shuttle lock before you got your feet on the deck. And then they'd cut the air to the Sump and wait." She shook her head. "They've had two hundred years to make sure we can't take anything from them by force. They're good at it. The one thing they haven't made sure of is that they can make us *work*."

Farrant didn't answer. But he didn't look convinced, and neither did the Ellery sisters behind him.

"How long can we hold out?" said Old Pym. "Once we stop. They'll cut our rations. They'll cut air."

"Then we'll be hungry and cold," said Hatch. "And so will they, if they want that breath. It's a contest. Who can wait longest." She looked at Maren. "That depends on the breathers. If the breathers hold, the heads stay shut. If the breathers break, it's over in a day."

Maren understood, then, why Hatch had wanted her. Not for the paper. For this.

"The breathers will hold," she said.

"Will they? You can promise that?"

"No," said Maren. "But I'll be the last one to break."

Hatch looked at her for a long moment, and nodded.

At the edge of the platform, Dov had turned from the water. He was looking at Maren, and his face, in the lamplight, had an expression on it she did not recognise and could not read.

"When?" he said.

They all looked at him. He did not usually speak in rooms like this; he did not usually come to rooms like this.

"When do we stop?" said Dov. "What day? And who's going to be at the heads when it starts?" He shrugged. "If I'm going to tell the tenders to stand down I'll need to tell them something. They'll want to know who's running it."

"We'll tell you," said Hatch. "When it's time."

"Fair enough," said Dov, and put his hands back in his pockets, and looked down into the water again.

* * *

The Factor came down to the Sump on the tenth day for the Scholarship ceremony.

The Combine had announced it a week before: a celebration, at the Number Four refinery head, the biggest open space in the Sump, to honour the families of the selected wards. Attendance was not compulsory. It was made clear that it would be noted. And so on the tenth day at the sixth shift-bell there were four thousand bonds standing in the great domed cavern of the refinery head, under the hanging intake pipes and the dark bulk of the separators, in their best coveralls, with their children who were not wards held in front of them by the shoulders so they could see.

Maren stood near the back, against the cold wall, with Hatch on one side of her and Dov on the other. She had not wanted to come. Hatch had made her. *You want to know what you're fighting,* Hatch had said, *you go and look at it.*

The Factor's party came in by the shuttle gallery, high up on the far side, and walked out onto the separator platform where everyone could see them. Thirty officers in Combine grey, and a dozen marines in black armour with their rifles held across their chests, and in the middle of them, in a grey coat no different from the others except that it was very well cut, a tall old man with silver hair.

Imre Castellane had been Factor of Vigil for nineteen years. Maren had seen him a handful of times in her life, always from far off — on a feed, on a platform, once crossing the docks at High with his officers around him like a wall. He was taller than she had expected, close up, and thinner, and he stood very straight; he had the kind of face that would have looked well on a coin, long and fine-boned and grave, and he looked out over the crowd of bonds below him the way a man might look over a field he owned and had come to love.

He had a slate in his hand. He did not use it.

"Families of Vigil," he said. His voice was not loud, but the refinery head's speakers carried it, deep and clear, to every corner of the cavern. "Thank you for coming. I know that for many of you it is a long way to come and a hard thing to come for. I know that some of you are frightened. That is natural. I want to speak to you plainly about what is happening, and why."

Behind him, on the platform, a great screen lit. On it — and a sound went through the four thousand bonds like wind through a field — were the wards. Rows of them, in the visitation hall of the Nursery, on the children's side of the glass. They were in new uniforms, dark blue, with the H-V crest on the breast. Their hair had been cut. Their faces had been scrubbed. They stood in neat lines with their hands at their sides, and as the feed came up they began, at some signal Maren could not see, to sing. It was a Combine song, one she half-knew from the feeds, about the Inner Worlds and the light. Their small voices came thin and sweet out of the speakers into the refinery head, and all around her, Maren heard parents begin to weep.

She looked for Pell. She could not find her. There were too many. And then she did — in the third row, at the end, half a head taller than the girl beside her, standing very still with her mouth closed. Not singing.

"Your children," said Castellane softly, over the song, "are going to have what none of you have had. That is the simple truth. They are going to be educated. They are going to live under open skies. They are going to have work that does not kill them, in a part of the Combine where the air is free and the water falls from the sky. I have been Factor here for nineteen years, and I have watched you send your children up to the Nursery as surety, and I have watched you grieve for them, and I have asked myself every year: what can I give back?" He paused. "This is what I can give back."

The song ended. The feed held for a moment on the children's scrubbed, uncertain faces, and then went dark.

"I know what some of you are saying," Castellane went on. "I hear it, you know. The Combine hears a great deal. Some of you are saying that this is a trick. That the Combine never gives anything away. That there must be a price." He smiled, a little sadly. "And you're right. There is always a price. That is the first thing I learned when I joined the Combine at seventeen, and it is the most important thing I know. Everything has a price. Every hour of your labour, every litre of your air, every bed in the Nursery and every meal your children eat — someone, somewhere, has paid for it, and has written it down. That is what the Combine *is*. Not a company. Not a government. A ledger. A great book in which every debt and every payment is recorded, so that nothing is lost and no one is cheated." He spread his hands. "People think the ledger is cruel. It is not. The ledger is the only kindness that lasts. Without the ledger, there is only the knife. There is only whoever is strongest, taking whatever they can reach. I have seen worlds without ledgers. I would not send your children to them."

He looked out over the crowd, and his voice dropped.

"Your children's price has been paid," he said. "By you. By your years in the dark. And now the ledger is going to pay you back." He lowered his hands. "That is all. Thank you. Count what's owed."

And from four thousand throats, ragged, uncertain, an automatic murmur, the way you'd answer a priest:

*And not a mark more.*

Beside her, Hatch had gone white to the lips.

"He's good," said Dov softly, on her other side. He sounded almost admiring. "He's very good."

* * *

Afterward the officers came down off the platform into the crowd, the way the Combine's officers did at ceremonies, to shake hands and be thanked. The marines came with them, a pace behind. Maren would have left. She was moving toward the gallery stair when a grey-coated woman stepped into her path with a polite smile and said, "Mrs. Tull? The Factor would like a word."

She looked at Hatch. Hatch's face was perfectly calm, and her eyes said: *go*.

The Factor was standing in a little clear space near the separator base, with officers around him and marines at the edges, talking to Lin and Ade Okoro. Lin was weeping and clutching his hand. Ade stood beside her like a wall, saying nothing. Castellane was speaking to Lin very gently, his silver head bent to hers, and as Maren came up he straightened and released her hand and turned, and Lin and Ade were moved away by the grey woman, and Maren found herself standing in front of the Factor of Vigil with nothing between them but a metre of cold air.

"Mrs. Tull," he said. "Thank you. I know you didn't want to come."

She waited.

"I make a point of meeting the best breather on Vigil every year or so," said Castellane. "It used to be your mother. Ada Tull. Twenty-two years in the deep seams; I remember her record. She came up to the Residence once, you know, to receive a commendation — the year she brought up the big seam off Number One head. She told me to my face that the commendation would be more use to her as a reduction on her bond, and that if I wanted to honour her I could stop charging her for the air she breathed while she was being honoured." He smiled. "I liked her very much."

"She died of the rattle," said Maren. "At forty-six."

"I know. I'm sorry." He seemed to mean it. "Most of them do. It's the great cruelty of this place. The breath is in the deep seams, and the deep seams kill the people who go down to them, and there is no other way to get it up." He looked at her. "Your daughter is on the list."

"0212."

"Pell," said Castellane. "Yes. I've seen her assessment. Remarkable. Quite remarkable. She'll go a very long way."

Maren looked at him. She looked at his fine, grave, sorrowful face, and his clean hands, and the soft grey of his well-cut coat; and at his throat, where the collar was open, on a fine steel chain against his skin, something that caught the work-lights of the refinery head and threw them back.

It was not jewellery. She had thought for a moment it was. It was a spindle, the length of her finger, half ceramic and half steel, the ceramic white and smooth and the steel worked with a fine spiral groove. At one end it tapered to a blunt point. At the other end was a ring for the chain. It looked like a part of something. It looked like it had been made to fit, very precisely, into a place that was waiting for it.

Castellane saw her looking. He did not touch it, or cover it. He only smiled.

"May I tell you something, Mrs. Tull?" he said. "As one who knew your mother?"

"You're the Factor."

"I am." The smile did not change. "People believe, in places like this, that distance is a kind of forgiveness. That if you are far enough from the centre of things, or long enough, the debts you owe will simply — fade. Be forgotten. I want you to understand that this is not so." His voice was very gentle. "The Combine does not forget. It does not forgive. It does not close an account because the account has become inconvenient to reach. It has receivers, Mrs. Tull, that have been waiting at closed doors on the edges of the map for longer than this colony has existed. Machines. Very patient ones. They'll wait a thousand years if they're told to. And when the door opens—" he spread his clean hands "—they'll come in and count."

"Is that a threat, Factor?"

"No," said Imre Castellane. "It's arithmetic." He inclined his head to her, a small, courteous bow. "Give my regards to Sister Hatch."

* * *

Lucan found her in the shuttle gallery afterward, in the press of bonds going back to the Sump and officers going back to the shuttles. He came alongside her in the crowd and walked with her for ten steps, not looking at her, with a slate held up in front of his face as though he were reading.

"He knows about Hatch," Maren said.

"He's always known about Hatch. He thinks she's harmless." Lucan turned a page on his slate. "He's not stupid. He'll have guessed there'll be something. A walkout. He's planning for it."

"How?"

"I don't know. I'm not in those meetings." He was quiet for three steps. "You saw it. Didn't you. At his throat."

"What is it?"

"The Gallows key," said Lucan. "Factor's authority over the pins. Every Throat station has a master console, and every master console has a lock, and that's what goes in the lock. The station runs itself day to day. But anything that isn't day to day — anything that changes what the pins are doing — has to go through that key." He turned another page. "There's only one. He's worn it since he came to Vigil. He sleeps in it."

"Why does it matter?"

Lucan stopped walking. The crowd flowed round them. For a moment he looked at her over the top of his slate, and his frightened, narrow, decent face was more frightened than she had seen it yet.

"Because under the Clause," he said, "the Factor holds the key until the account is closed."

"What does that mean? Closed how?"

But a grey-coated officer was calling his name from the shuttle lock, and he had already turned away from her, and in a moment he was gone into the crowd of grey.

* * *

They voted that night, on the water.

It took no time at all. Hatch put it to them simply — the strike, every head, every breather and tender and refinery hand, until the wards were returned — and asked for hands, and the hands went up in the lamplight, one after another, until they all were. Even Farrant. Even the Ellery sisters, though they looked at each other first.

Maren's went up. Dov's went up, after a moment, at the edge of the platform.

"The eighteenth day," said Hatch. "At first shift. The heads go dark and stay dark." She looked round at them. "Go home. Tell nobody. Not your wives, not your husbands, not your children. On the seventeenth night you tell your own crews and nobody else. And on the eighteenth day we find out what we're worth."

They went back across the catwalks in ones and twos, lamps swinging, footsteps doubled on the water. Maren went last but one, with Hatch beside her, slowly, because the old woman's knees were bad on the ladders.

At the foot of the service ladder Hatch stopped and put a hand on her arm.

"Your Factor gave you his regards for me," she said.

"Yes."

"Did he say anything else?"

Maren thought about the spindle on its fine chain. She thought about the patient machines waiting at the doors on the edges of the map. *They'll come in and count.*

"He said it was arithmetic," she said.

Hatch laughed — a short dry sound that came back off the black water twice. "It always is, with them," she said. "That's the trouble. They've never once in two hundred years thought we could add."

She started up the ladder. Maren stood at the bottom and looked back across the Drowned Decks, at the lamps going out one by one along the catwalks as the union went home, and counted them, without meaning to. Eleven. Twelve. Thirteen.

There should have been fourteen. She looked for the last lamp, the one at the edge of the platform, where Dov had stood.

It was already gone.


# Chapter 8: Catechism

*The Ledger · 2794*

The Prelate's rooms were at the very top of the Nave, in the old hub where the spin was gentlest, and they had a window.

Ysolde had heard of the window all her life, the way novices heard of everything in the Prelate's household — in whispers, embellished. It was said to be the largest pane of glass in the system. It was said to have been salvaged from a Steward's ship. It was said that the First Auditor herself had stood at it on the night she finished the first Count and wept. When the novice who had brought her the invitation showed her through the last door at the eighth bell and stepped back, she saw that at least the first of these things was true, and forgot the others entirely.

Tarrow filled it.

The Nave hung over Hesper's night side, and Hesper hung in Tarrow's shadow, and so the giant did not shine; it loomed. It was a darkness that took up the whole of the window and more, banded in the faintest possible reds and umbers by the light of the Vigil star coming round its limb, and across the middle of it, edge-on, the rings ran like a cut. Far down in the dark she could see the storms — the small pale flickers of lightning in the deep decks, where the divers went. Somewhere down there, tonight, someone's brother was falling through the red with a tag on a cord at his throat.

"It's too much, isn't it?" said a voice behind her. "I've lived with it for eleven years and it's still too much. I eat with my back to it most nights."

The Prelate-Auditor was standing at a small table by the far wall, in his shirtsleeves, with an apron over his white trousers and a wooden spoon in his hand.

"I cook for myself," said Aurel Quist. "My household think it's an eccentricity. It is. Sit down, Sister. It's only beans."

* * *

It was only beans. Hesper beans, from the hydroponic vaults under the Nave, cooked slowly with an onion and a piece of salt pork that must have cost him a month's ration and that he served to her without comment. There was bread. There was a jug of water, and a small flask of something gold that he poured into two tiny cups and that tasted of smoke and burned her throat. They ate at the little table with the giant at her back, and the Prelate talked.

He talked about arithmetic. He had said he would, and he did. He talked about the old masters — Brenner, whose *Tables* she had learned from, and Okafor, whose *Reckoning of the Starving* was the great history of the first century, and the anonymous keeper of the Fourth Ledger in the ninetieth year who had found a way to sum a column of a thousand figures by folding the page. He talked about the Count Board, and how it had been built, and the names of the nine smiths who had cut its plates. He talked about the beauty of a balanced ledger — he used that word, *beauty* — and the way a column of numbers that had been kept true for a century had a kind of grain to it, like wood, that you could feel under your hand if you had kept enough of them.

"You've felt it," he said. "I know you have. Anselm said you were a counter. Do you know what he meant by that?"

"I think he meant I'm careful."

"No. Plenty of keepers are careful. Careful's nothing. Careful's a clerk." He refilled her tiny cup. "A counter is someone who loves the Count. Not the number on the Board — the *Count*. The act of it. The writing down of every hour, every breath, every life, so that none of it is lost." He smiled. "Do you know, I think that's the most extraordinary thing about us. That's the thing nobody beyond the Door would understand, if they came. We're the only people in the history of the world who've written everything down. Every child born in the Lungs for three hundred and forty years is in a Ledger somewhere. Every hour they worked. Every mark they paid. Nobody is forgotten here. Nobody is allowed to be." He shook his head. "I find that very beautiful. I always have."

"So do I," said Ysolde, and found, to her surprise, that it was true. That it was still true.

"Of course you do. You came up from the Lungs at nine and wept for a week in the dormitory and then stopped and never wept again. Didn't you? Don't look like that; I've read your file. I read all their files." He sipped. "You stopped because weeping didn't balance."

She put her cup down very carefully.

"Forgive me," said Quist gently. "That was a cruelty. I'm an old man and I forget myself." He turned his cup in his fingers, and the light caught the seal ring on his right hand — the ring that every novice was taught to kiss and none was permitted to look at closely, black and heavy and old. "Let me tell you a story instead. You know it already. Everyone does. But I like the way I tell it."

* * *

"When the Door was shut," said the Prelate-Auditor, "there were thirty thousand people in the ice of Hesper and twelve Stewards in the Nave, and seed and stores for perhaps a third of them.

"The Combine had gone. It had shut the Door behind it — against us, for the debt we owed and could not pay — and left the Stewards here to keep the account until the debt was settled. That's all the old books tell us of it, and it's all we need to know. Twelve men and women, in an empty station, with thirty thousand starving people below them in the dark.

"You know what happened next. The Starving. Ten years in which half of the owed died. The scrubbers failing, the hydroponics failing, the cold coming in. Riots in the ice. Murder over a cup of meal. Children eaten — Okafor says it, in the *Reckoning*; I believe him. And through all of it, the Stewards held the seed vault. They didn't open it. They couldn't; if they had opened it to every hand that reached for it, it would have been eaten in a month and then there'd have been nothing to plant. So they held it, and they rationed it, and people cursed them, and people died at the doors of the Nave screaming for bread, and the Stewards stood behind those doors and kept the count of what was left.

"And there was a child," said Quist.

He put his cup down. In the window behind her, far down in the dark of the giant, the lightning flickered.

"We don't know much about her childhood. She was young when the Door was shut — very young. She was one of a number of children who came into the Stewards' care in the first years, orphans of the Starving, I suppose, or children whose parents gave them up so they'd be fed. And she was quick. She learned her numbers at the Stewards' knees. And when she was grown — when she was about your age, I think — she came to them and she said: *The people are dying because they don't believe their work is worth anything. They dig and they dive and they bring up the breath, and they don't know where it goes. Let me write it down.*
