"And they let her. And she wrote the first Count. Every hour of every person in the ice, entered against the Holy Number. Every mouthful of meal from the vault, entered against it too. So that a diver who went down into the red would know, when he came up, that his hours had been written in the book, and that his children would eat because of them, and that one day, when there were enough hours, the Count would be zero and the Door would open." He spread his hands. "And people believed her. And they went back to work. And the Starving ended. Not all at once — it took another generation — but it ended. The Count ended it. That's not a story. That's history. Okafor proves it."

Ysolde said nothing. Her hands were folded in her lap.

"*Count what is owed and not a mark more,*" said Quist. "She wrote that at the head of the first page. I've seen it, you know. The page. It's in the Reliquary." He smiled. "I've always loved that she wrote *not a mark more*. As though she knew. As though she knew that somebody, some day, would be tempted."

"Tempted to what, Prelate?"

"To make it heavier," he said simply. "Or lighter. Either one. To change it." He picked up his cup again and looked into it. "Brother Anselm was troubled at the end. Did you know that?"

There it was. She had been waiting for it since the eighth bell, the way you wait in the dark of a dive for the floor to drop away, and still it came as a shock.

"No, Prelate," she said. "I didn't know him well."

"No. Few did." Quist turned the cup. "He came to me last winter. He was troubled about the Interest. He'd been reading the old books — the second-century volumes of the Ninth — and he'd found something about them he didn't like. He wanted to talk to me about it." He looked up. "I wonder if you've found anything you don't like, Sister. In the old volumes. Bailiff-Captain Strand tells me you've been in the stacks a good deal, these last nights. And down in the deep spokes on inspection. Very proper."

"The Ninth's a big book, Prelate. I'm trying to learn it."

"Of course you are." He held her eyes. They were brown, and kind, and very tired. "I'll tell you what I told Anselm," he said. "Because I liked him, and I like you, and I would not want you to make his mistake.

"Suppose," said the Prelate-Auditor, "that someone were to discover tomorrow that the Count was wrong. Never mind how. An error in the old books, a slip of some long-dead keeper's pen. Suppose they found that the Count ought to be — oh — a little less than it is. Or a great deal less. Or nothing at all." He watched her. "What do you think would happen?

"I'll tell you. The Lungs would stop diving. Not all at once. But they would. Why go down into the red, if the Count was zero and the Door was still shut? Why die at forty of the rattle, for nothing? And with no divers, there would be no breath, and with no breath there would be no heat in the ice and no drive for the drop shuttles and no power for the hydroponic vaults. And the Nave would stop sending meal down the ladders, because there would be no meal to send. And the Lungs would come up the ladders to take it. And the Bailiffs would meet them at the docks." His voice was very quiet. "We'd be back in the Starving inside a year. A hundred and ninety thousand people. I've done the sum, Ysolde. I've done it many times. I'm sure you could do it too."

Behind her, the giant hung in the dark. She could feel it there, the way you feel someone standing behind you.

"The Count," said Aurel Quist, "is not a number. It never was. It's the thing that holds us together. It's the only thing that ever has. And a man who would take it away from a hundred and ninety thousand people — however right his arithmetic — is not a counter. He's a murderer who hasn't done the sum." He smiled at her, sadly. "Anselm hadn't done the sum. I tried to show him. I don't think he understood."

"Is that why he went to the Breath?"

She had not meant to say it. It came out of her like a breath let go at depth.

Quist did not flinch. He looked at her for a long, long moment across the little table, with the beans gone cold in their bowls between them; and then he got up, slowly, leaning on the table, and came round it, and put his old hand on her shoulder, very lightly, the way her mother had in the corridor on Sorrow Day.

"Brother Anselm went to the Breath," he said, "because the rail at the Number Two gallery is low. I've had it raised. Good night, Sister. Thank you for eating my beans."

* * *

She did not sleep. She lay on the narrow bunk in her cell in the Third Chancery's dormitory wing with her eyes open in the dark and went over it, again and again, the way she went over a column that would not balance: every word, every pause. *I've done the sum.* *Not a counter. A murderer who hasn't done the sum.* *I've had it raised.*

He knew. That was the first thing. He knew about the Interest, about the iron-gall, about the pages cut out of the old books. He had not said so. He had not needed to. And he had told her, as plainly as a Prelate-Auditor could tell anyone anything, why it did not matter.

And the worst of it was that she did not know if he was wrong.

She lay and did his sum. She could not help it. A hundred and ninety thousand people. The breath that kept them warm. The divers who went down for it. If the Count were gone—

At the fourth bell of the morning there was a knock at her door.

It was a novice — not the same one; a girl this time, younger, from the Lungs by her accent — with a folded paper. "From the drop dock, Sister. A diver brought it up on the night shuttle and asked for you by name. He said it was family."

It was from Piet. It was written in pencil, in his big careless hand, on the back of a tally slip.

*Yz — don't be cross. Dutiful's crew got posted in default last night. Bailiffs say if we don't make it up by the Reckoning they'll take the boat and put us on refinery share. Fourteen months. I'll be all right. Don't do anything stupid. — P.*

She read it twice. Then she got up and dressed in the dark and went down through the sleeping Nave to the Third Chancery, and let herself in with her keeper's key, and lit the lamp at Anselm's desk — her desk — and opened the two hundred and sixth volume of the Ninth Ledger.

The *Dutiful* was there. Spoke Eighteen, the Harrow family's boat; crew of five, including *Marr, Piet, diver, share one-fifth*. She found the line. She found the red default mark beside it, entered by some Bailiff's clerk the evening before. She found the shortfall: forty-one hours.

Forty-one hours. That was all. Forty-one hours of diving, out of the four hundred and some the crew had logged that quarter. A lost dive to weather and a scoop relining. That was what would take her brother's boat away and send him into the refinery for fourteen months, to work the separators on the Number Two head beside the gallery where Anselm's lamp had been found.

She thought about the Count Board. She thought about three billion, nine hundred and seven million marks.

She dipped her pen.

It took no time. It was the easiest thing she had ever done, and her hand shook so badly while she did it that she had to stop twice and hold her wrist with her other hand. She entered forty-one hours to the *Dutiful*'s crew under the date of the last dive, in her own small careful keeper's hand, as though the dive had gone longer than it had. She carried the hours to the page total. She carried the page total to the volume. She struck the default mark through with a single neat line and initialled it, the way a keeper did when an error was corrected. It looked exactly like what it was not.

Then she sat back from the desk and looked at it for a long time.

It was a lie. It was the first lie she had ever written in a Ledger. In twenty-two years she had written perhaps sixty thousand pages, and every figure on every one of them had been true, and she had been proud of it, prouder of it than of anything; and now there were forty-one hours of nothing in the two hundred and sixth volume of the Ninth, in her hand, and her brother would keep his boat.

And — she understood it slowly, sitting there, the way the cold had come into her in Corin's tank — it was not a lie. Not really. Not by the Count's own law. The Count had been zero for thirty-nine years. Every hour her brother had worked in his life had gone into nothing. She had not taken forty-one hours from the account. The account had taken his whole life from him, and she had given back forty-one hours of it.

She turned the pages. Spoke Nineteen. Spoke Twenty. Red marks, here and there, all through the deep spokes. Default. Default. Arrears.

She dipped her pen again.

* * *

"Why?" said Corin Asch.

It was the next evening, in the back room of the bottle shop, among the great hollow tanks. His face was still swollen from the Bailiffs' visit, the cut over his eye scabbed black, and he was sitting on the bench with his mark-stick across his knees and looking at her as though she were a valve he could not get to seat.

"I told you why."

"You told me you've been cooking your own ledger. Taking families out of default. Nineteen of them, you said, in two nights." He turned the stick in his scarred hands. "I believe you. I just don't know why you'd do it."

"Because it's already paid."

"That didn't stop you before."

"I didn't *know* before."

"You knew three days ago. You stood in this room and told me Year three hundred and two." He leaned forward. "Something's happened."

She told him about the Prelate. She had not meant to. It came out in pieces, the beans and the window and the gold drink and the story of the child at the Stewards' knees, and at the end of it the old man's hand on her shoulder and his voice saying *I've had it raised*. Corin listened without interrupting, without moving, and when she was done he sat looking at the floor.

"So he knows," he said at last.

"He knows."

"And he thinks it's worth it. The lie."

"He thinks without it we'd starve." She heard her own voice, thin and strange. "And I can't prove he's wrong, Corin. I've been doing his sum for a day and a night and I can't make it come out any other way. If the Lungs stopped diving—"

"Then the Nave would have to come down the ladders and ask us nicely," said Corin, "instead of sending Bailiffs." He looked up at her, and his ruined face was very hard. "You think the Lungs would stop diving if the Count was zero? Sister, the Lungs dive because it's the only thing there is to do and the only way anybody eats. That's not faith. That's weather. We'd dive tomorrow if the Count Board fell off the wall. We'd just stop sending our children down at fourteen to pay a debt that doesn't exist." He set the mark-stick down beside him on the bench, carefully. "Your Prelate's sum has a wrong figure in it. He's counted us as cattle that only walk while you whip them. He's never once been down a ladder to check."

She did not answer. She found she could not.

"Anselm said something like that," Corin said, more quietly. "Near the end. He said the Prelate had all the right numbers in the wrong places." He was quiet a moment. "He said there was only one thing that would ever make the Nave listen. Not sums. They'd only ever answer sums with more sums. He said it would have to be something older than the Count."

"What?"

"The First Book."

She looked at him.

"*Per F.B.*," said Corin. "You saw it on his sheet. That's what it meant. The First Book. The ledger from before the First Auditor — the Stewards' own book, from the very beginning. When the Door was shut. It's supposed to have the Founding in it, the real one, the figure the Combine left. It's supposed to have what the Stewards swore when they took the account." He shrugged. "Anselm saw a page of it once. Forty-odd years ago. He was a novice copying for old Prelate Hanne, and the Prelate left a page out on his desk one night — just one page — and Anselm read it before he could stop himself. There was a number on it. The Founding. It wasn't the number in the primers." He looked at her. "He never forgot it. He built his whole sheet on it."

"Where is it?"

"Where would it be? Where everything is that you lot don't want us to see." Corin's mouth twisted. "In the Reliquary. Under your Ledger Hall. Where only the Prelate goes, on Founding Day, to pray."

"Anselm wanted to get in."

"He wanted to. He was sixty-eight, Sister, with knees like rotten ice. He couldn't climb a ladder without stopping halfway." Corin looked at her steadily. "He used to say it was a pity he'd never had a daughter. Somebody young. Somebody who could count."

* * *

She came up on the night shuttle and went to the Turning at first bell, because she always went to the Turning, and she stood among the pilgrims at the rail with her hands folded in her grey sleeves and watched the Count Board change.

The clatter came from the right, as it always did. The units, the tens, the hundreds, rushing over like rain; the thousands, slowing; the tens of thousands, slower. It ran out further left than it had in days. She knew why. She had done it. Nineteen families in the deep spokes, taken out of default — four hundred and thirty-eight hours of tithe, entered in the Ninth Ledger in her careful hand, summed in the Third Chancery last night with all the rest and carried up here, to the Board, and subtracted. The great plates fell and settled.

**3,906,871,104**

The pilgrims at the rail touched their tags. An old woman beside her, a diver's widow by her coat, let out a little breath of satisfaction at the size of the fall. *A good night,* she said to nobody. *A good night for the Lungs.* And she began the response, under her breath, as she always would, as they all always would.

*What are we?*

*The owed.*

Ysolde looked up past the Board at the words cut into the vault above it. COUNT WHAT IS OWED AND NOT A MARK MORE. She thought of a child at the Stewards' knees, learning her numbers in the Starving. She thought of a page in the Reliquary, under her feet, under this floor, in the dark.

*Why is the Door shut?*

*Because the account is open.*

Every person in this hall was watching a lie get smaller. She had made it smaller. Four hundred and thirty-eight hours' worth. It was still three billion, nine hundred and six million marks of nothing, and tomorrow it would still be that, and the day after, and at the Reckoning it would leap upward again by its invented sorrow and the Lungs would weep, and she could cook the Ninth Ledger every night for the rest of her life and never make a mark of difference to it.

*When will it open?*

*When the Count is zero.*

Sums. They would only ever answer sums with more sums. It would have to be something older.

*Who keeps the Count?*

She did not say the response. She stood at the rail with her hands in her sleeves and her eyes on the vault, and said, in her head, very quietly, a different one.

*I do.*


# Chapter 9: Kell Deep

*The Door · 3190*

Kell is the colour of a scab.

That's the first thing anyone says about it, and the last thing I'll say, because there's not much else to it from the outside. It's a moon of black rock about a quarter the size of Hesper, close in to Tarrow, and the giant's pull kneads it the way a baker kneads dough, so that it's hot inside — hot enough that the rock cracks, and the cracks glow, a dull rust-orange that never goes out. From space it looks like something that was burned and hasn't healed. There's no ice on Kell. There's no water. There's rock and heat and, down in the biggest crack of all, a chasm forty kilometres long that the first refinery-builders called the Deep, the Margrave's house and the Margrave's refinery and three hundred and twelve children, give or take whoever had died that week.

I'd been there more times than I could count. I'd stood on the gangways at the chasm's lip with a bag of blank tags on my shoulder and a line of children behind me holding hands. I'd never once gone in the back way.

"Here," said Wren. "This one."

She had her finger on the scope, on a blister of rock about sixty kilometres north of the Deep's lip where the surface was dark — not cracked, not glowing, just a dead grey dome with a black hole in the top of it like a pockmark.

"That's a vent?"

"It was. Number Forty. The Old Chimney." She was leaning over my shoulder in the cramped cockpit, so close I could smell the refinery on her still, under the soap from my boat's basin; it gets into the skin. "It's the oldest vent in the Deep. It runs all the way down from the surface to the dormitory galleries on Level Nine. It used to be a cooling stack, a hundred years ago, before they cut the new ones. Then the rock shifted and the bottom half went hot and they sealed it." She shrugged. "They sealed it from the top. They forgot about the bottom. There's a grating at the Level Nine end, behind the old latrines, that's been rusted through since before I came. We used to go up it on dares."

"How far up?"

"Eleven of us got to the top, in ten years. I was one." She held up her hands — those shiny ridged palms. "It's hot in the middle. You have to go fast."

"And down?"

"Down's easier," said Wren. "You just let go."

* * *

I put the *Mendicant* down in the shadow of a crater wall a kilometre from the Old Chimney, with her drive cold and her lights out and her hull pressed so close to the rock I heard it scrape. Haddie stayed with her. She'd wanted to come — she'd argued for the whole of the six-hour run from the rings, in that dry, furious, reasonable way she argued — and in the end it was Wren who'd said no. *You can't go fast,* Wren had said. *I'm sorry. It's the only thing that matters in there.* And Haddie had looked at her for a long moment and then nodded, and gone and sat down in the back of the cabin with the herald in her lap and a Count-book open on top of it, and not spoken to either of us again until we were suiting up.

Then she'd said, "Absalom."

"What?"

"How many will you bring out?"

"As many as fit."

"The boat's rated for six." She was looking at me over those wire spectacles. "Air for six. Seats for six."

"I know what she's rated for. I flew her."

"Then you know," said Haddie, "that you're going to have to choose." She looked down at her book again. "You did it before. For the other side. I suppose you'll remember how."

I didn't answer her. I sealed my helmet and went out.

* * *

Wren went up the side of the Old Chimney's dome like a spider. I went up after her slowly, the way a fifty-two-year-old man goes up a rock wall in a suit at a quarter of a gravity, which is to say badly and with a lot of swearing on the closed channel. At the top she was waiting for me, crouched on the lip of the hole with her helmet lamp off, looking down.

It was a shaft about a metre and a half across, its walls smooth and black and glassy where the heat had once run up them. It went straight down. My helmet lamp found the walls for twenty metres or so and then just found dark.

"How far?"

"Eleven hundred metres to the hot section. Then four hundred of hot. Then three hundred to the grating." She was clipping a line to an old iron staple driven into the lip. "There's staples all the way down. Every two metres. Somebody put them there when it was a stack. Use them, don't use the walls. The walls in the hot section will cook your suit."

"How hot?"

"You'll know," said Wren, and swung herself over the edge.

I'll not tell you much about the climb down. I don't like thinking about it. It took us nearly two hours, staple to staple to staple, my lamp swinging over the black glass and Wren's below me, a little bobbing point in the dark that got smaller and smaller and then stopped and waited for me to catch up, every time, without a word. About seven hundred metres down I started to feel it: a warmth coming through the soles of my boots and the palms of my gloves, faint at first, the way you feel the sun come round on a hull. By a thousand metres it wasn't faint. By eleven hundred my suit's alarms were singing, and the black glass of the walls had a dull red glow in it, not from my lamp but from inside, and I could hear the staples ticking under my hands as they expanded.

"Fast now," said Wren on the channel. "Don't stop. Don't stop for anything."

I didn't stop. I went down those four hundred metres hand over hand with the alarms screaming in my ears and the sweat running into my eyes inside the helmet, and the heat coming through the suit like a living thing, a hand on my back pressing, and at the bottom of it I was shaking so hard I could barely work the staples. And somewhere in the middle of it I understood, all at once and very clearly, that Wren had done this when she was eleven. On a dare. With her bare hands, probably; the tithed don't have suits. And that she'd done it ten more times after that, going up, for the sake of standing at the top of a dead vent on a dead moon and looking at the stars.

The last three hundred metres were cool. At the bottom there was a grating, rusted to lace, and beyond it a smell I'd know anywhere: hot rock, and latrines, and children.

* * *

The dormitory galleries of Kell Deep are on Level Nine, which is the lowest level the Margrave's people bother to heat with anything but the rock itself. They're long tunnels cut through the basalt, maybe four metres wide, and down both sides of each one are bunks — shelves, really, cut into the wall, three high, each with a felt blanket and a number painted over it. There's a lamp at each end of the gallery that never goes out. There's a reeve's post at the far end where a reeve sits all night and watches. There's no door. Where would they go?

There were sixty bunks in Wren's gallery. Forty-one of them had children in them. The rest were on shift.

I'd known it would be like that. I'd planned it with her, on the run in from the rings. The vents ran day and night, and a third of the children were always down in them, and the shifts changed at the twelfth hour and the fourth and the eighth. We'd come in at the half past three, when the shift going down was gone and the shift coming up wasn't back. The best we could do was forty-one.

And the boat was rated for six.

Wren went down the gallery like a ghost, touching shoulders, whispering. I stood in the shadow by the latrine grating with my helmet off under my arm, and watched them wake. They woke the way the tithed wake — all at once and silent, the way small animals wake, with their eyes open and their bodies still. They saw Wren and their faces changed. They saw me behind her and their faces changed again.

I knew some of them. I don't know why I hadn't expected that. A girl of about eighteen with a burned ear, sitting up in a top bunk near the lamp, staring at me — Kesse, Lanthorn twenty-two. A boy of seventeen with a squint — Odo, Sallow twenty-three. I'd held their hands on the gangways. They knew me. I watched them know me.

"It's all right," Wren was whispering. "It's all right, he's with me, he's — never mind what he is, he's with me. Get your boots. Don't talk. Kesse, get the little ones. Odo, get Mattie, he's in the end bunk, he sleeps like a stone."

"Wren," Kesse whispered. Her eyes were on me. "Wren, that's—"

"I know who he is."

"He's a *reeve*."

"He was a reeve," said Wren. "Now he's a boat. Get the little ones."

They got up. Thirty-odd of them, in their felt shifts and their bare feet with their boots in their hands, and they came down the gallery toward the grating in a silent shuffling crowd, the big ones carrying the small ones. Some of them couldn't have been more than nine. I counted them as they came — I couldn't help it, it was what I'd done for nine years, it was what my hands knew — and I got to thirty-three and stopped.

"There's more," I said to Wren, low. "There's eight still in the bunks."

"They can't come."

"Why not?"

"Because they're sick," said Wren flatly. "Or they're too small to climb. Or they're the ones who'd tell." She didn't look at me. "I've had ten years to think about this too, Rook. I know which ones can come."

So she'd chosen. And I hadn't had to. I'll not pretend I wasn't grateful.

"That's still thirty-three," I said. "The boat's rated for six."

"Then you'll have to fly carefully," said Wren, and turned away.

* * *

We had them at the grating, and the first of them climbing — Kesse first, because she knew the way, with a little girl clinging to her back like a monkey — when the reeve at the far end of the gallery woke up.

I hadn't seen him. That's the plain truth of it. The post at the gallery's end was in shadow past the lamp, and I'd seen a chair and a shape in it, and Wren had said *he sleeps*, and I'd believed her because she'd had ten years to know. But reeves get old and reeves get tired and sometimes reeves get up in the night to piss. I heard a scrape. I turned. And there was a man coming down the gallery between the bunks, a big grey-bearded man in a reeve's red coat with the white hand on the breast, with his trousers half-buttoned and a lamp held up in one fist, saying, "What the hell—"

He saw the children at the grating. He saw Wren. And then he saw me, and stopped dead, five metres off, with his lamp held up.

"Absalom?"

It was Garra. Garra Moss. He'd been a reeve when I was a reeve. We'd ridden the tithe runs together for six years. He'd been at my boy's burial. He'd stood beside me at the graveside on Lanthorn and put his big hand on my shoulder and said nothing, which was the right thing, and the only kind thing anybody said to me that day.

"Garra," I said.

"What are you—" His eyes went to the children. Back to me. I watched him understand. I watched his face go from bewilderment to something like grief. "Oh, Absalom. Oh, you bloody fool."

"Go back to your chair, Garra."

"I can't do that."

"Go back to your chair and sit down and go to sleep. You never saw anything. You never woke."

"*I can't do that,*" said Garra Moss. "You know I can't. You know what she does to a reeve who loses a gallery." He was backing up. His free hand had gone to his belt, where the alarm was. "Put them back, Absalom. Put them back in their bunks and go back up whatever hole you came down, and I'll give you an hour. For Jory's sake. I'll give you an hour before I—"

"Garra—"

His hand closed on the alarm.

I'd like to tell you I thought about it. I'd like to tell you I weighed it, the way Haddie weighs the Door: Garra's life on one side and thirty-three children on the other, and the sum came out the way it had to, and I did what the sum said. That would make me sound like a man who'd thought about something. I didn't think. I'd been a reeve for nine years and I'd been trained by Margrave Kask's own people, and my body went across those five metres before my mind had done anything at all, and my knife was in my hand and then it was in Garra, low, under the ribs, the way I'd been taught, and he made a sound like a man surprised by a stair that isn't there, and the lamp fell and broke on the floor.

He took a long time to go down. He held on to me. He was a big man. His face was close to mine and his eyes were open and he was trying to say something, and I held him up while he tried, the way you'd hold up a drunk friend, until he stopped.

Then I put him down on the floor between the bunks.

Behind me, very quietly, one of the small children was crying.

"Rook," said Wren.

I didn't turn round.

"Rook. The alarm. Did he—"

I looked at his belt. His hand was still on it. I took his fingers off it, one at a time. The little light on its face was dark.

"No," I said. "He didn't."

"Then come on."

I came on.

* * *

I'll tell you about Mattie, because if I'm telling the truth I have to tell all of it.

He was ten. He was Odo's friend from the end bunk who slept like a stone, a skinny little boy from the Kettles with a burned cheek and a cough, and he went up the Old Chimney behind Odo in the middle of the line, holding on to the staples the way Wren had shown them all at the bottom: *don't touch the walls, don't stop, don't stop for anything*. We'd made them strip off their felt shifts and wrap them round their hands, for the hot section. It wasn't enough. I knew it wasn't enough. I went up last, behind all of them, in my suit with its screaming alarms, and I could hear them above me on the channel Wren had rigged from her helmet speaker — the children, gasping and sobbing and climbing, and Wren's voice saying *go, go, go, don't stop* over and over like a prayer.

Mattie stopped.

I don't know why. Maybe his hands gave out. Maybe he looked down. He was a little above the middle of the hot section and I was about thirty metres below him, and I saw his small shape in my lamp stop moving, and cling, and then I heard him on the channel — not crying, just a small, high, surprised noise, like a bird — and he let go of the staple with one hand and put it against the wall. To rest. I suppose. Just to rest for a moment.

The wall in the hot section was hot enough to cook a suit.

He came down past me. I reached for him. I touched him — I touched his shoulder as he went by, I felt it, through my glove — and I didn't hold on.

I want to say that I couldn't have. That the weight would have pulled me off. That I'd have gone too, and then who would have flown the boat? I want to say all that, and it's probably true. But I've thought about it every night since, and I'll tell you the thing I actually remember, which is that my hand closed on air because I'd hesitated. Half a second. Because I'd thought: *that's one less.*

I've had a lot of thoughts I'm ashamed of. That's the one.

Wren went back down for him. When we were all at the top, all thirty-two of them, huddled on the lip of the dome in their bare feet and their burned hands with the stars over them — the first stars some of them had seen since they were nine — she clipped her line back onto the staple without a word and went back down the shaft. I didn't stop her. It took her an hour. When she came back up she had him over her shoulder, wrapped in her own coat, and she carried him across the kilometre of rock to the *Mendicant* with the rest of us, and she wouldn't let anybody help her, and she didn't say a word.

* * *

Thirty-two children, a girl of nineteen, an old woman of seventy-four and me, in a reeve's cutter rated for six.

I'll not describe it. You can imagine. They were in the cabin, the cargo bay, the lock, the cockpit footwell, three to a seat and two to a locker. The air went bad inside the first hour, and the scrubbers screamed, and I turned the heat down to nothing to spare them and the children shivered and pressed together for warmth and nobody complained. Not one of them. I've carried a lot of tithed children in my time, and I'd always thought the silence was because they were frightened of me. That night I understood it was just what they'd learned. You don't complain in the Deep. Nobody's listening.

Haddie moved among them in the dark with a water bulb, going from one to the next, letting each one take a mouthful. She didn't speak to me. When she got to the cockpit she held the bulb to Wren's lips, and Wren drank, and Haddie put her old bent hand on the top of Wren's cropped head for a moment, and left it there.

Mattie was in the cargo bay, wrapped in Wren's coat, between two crates.

"Where?" said Haddie, eventually. To me. "Where are you taking them?"

"Somewhere she won't look."

"There's nowhere she won't look."

"There's one place." I had the boat on a long, slow, dark arc away from Kell, curving out round the giant's limb, burning as little as I dared. "She won't look there because she doesn't think anybody's mad enough to go. It's dead. It's been dead for two hundred years. There's nothing left to pick. Everybody knows it's haunted." I glanced back at her. "I know a part that's still got air."

Haddie looked at me for a long moment. Then I saw her understand.

"The Hulk," she said.

* * *

The Hulk orbits Hesper, the way it always has, the way it did before there was a Lanthorn or a Margrave or anybody at all to remember what it was before it was the Hulk. It's the biggest made thing in the Tarrow system. It's a great dark chandelier of a station, spokes and rings and docks and towers, all dead, all black, half of it open to the vacuum and the other half sealed and cold and full of nothing. Pickers have been stripping it for two hundred years. There's nothing left in it worth the fuel — no metal you can easily cut, no machine that still turns. There's paper, in some of the deep rooms, rotting in heaps. There's the old hand written on everything. There are, if you believe the pickers, voices in the dark, and lights where no lights should be, and once in a while a picker who goes in and doesn't come out.

There's also a room I found when I was thirty, picking alone in the deep core where nobody goes, and patched myself, and put a scrubber in, and never told a soul about. I used to go there, after Jory died. I used to go there and sit.

I brought the *Mendicant* in to a dead dock in the core, and we got the children out and through the lock and down a long cold corridor in the dark, and I opened the last door, and turned on the lamps I'd strung up years ago, and stood back.

The children stopped in the doorway, all together, and stared.

It's a hall. That's all I've ever been able to call it. A long, high, curving vault, ribbed with grey metal like the inside of a great animal, so big that the lamps only light the near half of it and the far end goes off into the dark. There are railings along one side, worn smooth. There are rows of something that might have been benches. And at the far end, where the lamplight just reaches, the whole wall is a heap of fallen plates: hundreds of them, each as tall as a man, black with white marks painted on them, tumbled over each other in a great slope like a rockfall, where whatever they'd once hung on had given way.

The marks on them are numbers. The old numbers, the ones Haddie can read. A one, a seven, a nought, a four. Jumbled, upside down, sideways, half-buried. They don't make anything. They're just there.

"What was it?" whispered Kesse.

"I don't know," I said. "Nobody does."

Haddie had gone past me, into the hall. She was standing in the middle of it with her hand-lamp up, turning slowly, looking up at the vault. Her face had a strange expression on it.

"There's writing," she said.

I looked up. I'd never noticed. I'd sat in that hall a hundred nights and never once looked up. But there it was, high on the curve of the vault above the heap of fallen plates, cut into the metal itself in square letters a hand high, so worn and dark you'd never see it unless you held a lamp up and looked for it. Half of it was gone, where a section of the vault had buckled. Half was still there.

Wren had come up beside Haddie. She was still in her suit. Her face was grey with exhaustion and smeared with soot and with something else I didn't want to think about, and her eyes were on the letters.

"Can you read it?" said Haddie softly. "Go on. You know the letters now. You've been learning them for three days. Try."

Wren looked up at the vault. Her lips moved.

"*Count,*" she said slowly. "*Count. What. Is.*" She stopped. Frowned. "*Owed.*"

"Go on."

"*And not.*" Her voice cracked. "*A mark. More.*"

She stood under it with her burned hands at her sides and the dead boy's blood on her suit, and said it again, to herself, the whole thing, slowly, as if it were a sum she was checking. *Count what is owed and not a mark more.* And then she sat down, very suddenly, on the cold metal floor of the hall, among the children, and put her face in her hands, and for the first time since I'd pulled her out of that skiff, she wept.

* * *

Haddie found me later, at the dock, where I'd gone to check the *Mendicant*'s scrubbers and to be somewhere the children weren't. She'd brought the herald. She'd brought her glass, too — the little portable scope she carried in her coat — and she'd set it up at the dock's cracked port, pointed out past Hesper's limb at the place where the Door hung.

"Look," she said.

I looked. I didn't know what I was meant to see. It looked the way it always looked: a smudge of wrong stars.

"I measured it at the seventh hour," said Haddie, "on the way in from Kell. And again just now. Five hours apart." She took her spectacles off. Her hands were shaking. "It's moved, Absalom. In five hours. As much as it used to move in a year."

"That's not possible."

"No."

"Why would it—"

"It's like something's pushing," said Haddie. "From the other side."

Between us, on the dock floor in its blanket, the herald stopped humming.

We both looked down at it. It had been speaking its notice every hour or so, the same pleasant calm words in the same order, for four days. I'd stopped hearing it. Now it was silent, and the silence went on longer than it ever had before. Long enough that I'd started to think it had died.

Then it spoke. But not the notice. Something else. Something short — a dozen words, no more — in that same patient voice, once, and then again, and then it went back to its humming.

Haddie had gone very still.

"Well?" I said.

She didn't answer for a moment. Then she put her spectacles back on, and looked at the herald, and then out through the port at the Door, and when she spoke her voice was barely above a whisper.

"*Passage at threshold,*" she said. "*Receiver attending.*"


# Chapter 10: Inversion

*The Bond · 2431*

The Number Four head went dark at first shift on the eighteenth day, and the silence was the loudest thing Maren had ever heard.

She had never heard the head silent. Nobody had. The intakes had roared since before she was born — the great exhale of the separators drawing breath off the bottles, the hiss of the condensers, the deep thudding pulse of the pumps that pushed it all up the long pipe through the ice to the tanks at High. It was the Sump's heartbeat. You stopped hearing it at about three years old. And at first shift on the eighteenth day the refinery hands on the separator floor reached up, one after another, and threw the bank switches, and the roar went down through every note it had and stopped; and in the silence that came after, four hundred people standing in the domed cavern of the head heard their own breathing, and the drip of the ice, and somewhere far up in the dark of the roof a single bird-like creak of cooling metal; and nobody said anything at all.

Then Ruth Calloway, standing on a crate by the separator base with her arms folded, said in her enormous voice, "Well. That's that, then," and somebody laughed, and the laugh went round the cavern like a wave.

Maren was on the breathers' rack. She had been there since before first shift, with the *Gannet* racked and cold behind her and her suit hung on its hook, and as the breathers came in for their dives — Farrant, Moss, the Ellery sisters, a hundred and forty of them from all four heads — she had met each one at the rack gate and said the same thing. *We're not diving.* Some had known already. Some hadn't. A few had argued. One, a young breather from Number One head named Corley, had stood in front of her for a long time with his helmet in his hands and tears running down his face and said, *I've got a ward too, Tull, she's on the list, I can't, they'll put it on the bond,* and Maren had said, *I know. I've got one too. Rack your bottles,* and after a long moment he had.

Siggard came at the second bell. He walked down the empty weighing line with his soft careful hands at his sides and his slate tucked under his arm, and stood at the breathers' rack gate in front of her, and looked at her for a while without speaking.

"Tull," he said at last.

"Tally-master."

"You'll want to dive."

"No."

"It's going on the bond. All of this. Every hour the heads are dark is product not delivered. You understand that? They'll charge it. Every hour, to every bond in the Sump, at full valuation."

"Let them."

He looked at her. And then, to her astonishment, he sighed — a long, tired, human sigh — and took his slate from under his arm and put it face down on the rail.

"My father was a breather," he said. "Did you know that? Number Two head. He went down in a sink in the ninety-eighth year. They charged my mother for the skimmer." He picked up the slate again, and tucked it back under his arm, and turned away. "Count what's owed, Tull."

"And not a mark more," she said, to his back.

* * *

The Sump sent its terms up to High at the third bell, over the head's link to the Factor's office. Hatch spoke them herself, standing at the old comm board in the tally master's office with Maren and Pym and Ade Okoro behind her and her hands flat on the board's edge. She did not raise her voice.

"This is Agathe Hatch, for the bonded community of Vigil. Every refinery head in the Sump is shut. No breath will come up until the wards on the Scholarship list are returned to their parents, every one of them, in the Sump, before the *Grace* departs. We know about Schedule C. We know about Brannock. We know about the Clause. That's all. We'll be here."

She let go of the board.

There was no reply. They waited an hour, and then two. Hatch went back to the clinic. The bonds in the head sat down on the floor of the cavern, and on the separator housings, and on the empty weighing line, and somebody brought food from the hydroponics on Five and passed it round, and somebody else began to sing, and for a while — Maren would remember this afterward, and hold on to it — for a while it was almost cheerful. Four hundred people in the cold, with the machines quiet and nothing to do but wait, singing the breathers' hymn in a dozen keys while the old ones corrected the young ones on the words.

*Down in the red where the weight comes on,*
*down where the light's a bruise,*
*bring up the breath for the ones who are owed,*
*and bring yourself up if you choose.*

The reply came at the sixth bell. It was not a voice.

It was the fans.

Maren did not notice at first. Nobody did. The circulation fans in the Sump ran all the time, a hum even more constant than the head's roar, so constant that you did not hear it even as a child. It was only when someone near her — one of the Ellery sisters — said, *Is it cold?* and someone else said, *It's always cold,* and the first one said, *No, listen,* that they all stopped singing and listened, and heard what was not there.

The fans had stopped. Not here — the head had its own plant — but everywhere else. Up through the ice from the levels below, from Seven and Eight and Nine, where the poorest of the Sump lived and the clinic was, came a silence that should have been a hum.

"They've cut the air," said Ade Okoro.

Nobody answered him.

"They've cut the air on the bottom levels." He was on his feet. "That's where the old ones are. That's where the clinic is. That's—"

"That's where they'll do it first," said Maren. "Where it'll hurt most."

* * *

She went down to the clinic at the eighth bell, because there was nothing she could do at the head and because she could not stand to be there, and she found it already full.

Hatch had moved every cot together at the near end, by the door, where the air from the corridor still moved a little, and had put two people in every cot and more on the floor between them. They were the old and the very young and the sick: the rattle cases, mostly, breathers and ex-breathers whose lungs were already half-full of whatever Tarrow had put in them, who could manage in the Sump's thin air on an ordinary day and could not manage now. The room was very cold. The light was very dim — Hatch had turned the lamps down, to save power, though there was no reason to think the Combine would cut power too, or perhaps every reason. And the sound in it was the sound Maren had last heard nineteen years ago in this same room, sitting by her mother's bed: a wet, slow, clicking labour, many times over, like a room full of clocks running down.

Hatch was kneeling by a cot near the curtain. She had a mask in her hand, connected by a hose to a small grey cylinder on the floor, and she was holding it to the face of a boy of about fifteen — the same boy, Maren realised, who had been on the far cot the night she first came down here with the paper. His chest was heaving. His lips were the colour of slate.

"How long?" said Maren.

"For him? I don't know. I've got eleven cylinders of oxygen and forty people who need them." Hatch did not look up. "For the rest of us, on Nine? Without the fans the carbon dioxide builds. Two days before it's dangerous for the healthy ones. Three before it starts killing them. For this lot—" she glanced along the cots "—tonight. Some of them, tonight."

"Ruth's crews are trying to hotwire the local scrubbers on Seven."

"Ruth's crews are very good," said Hatch, "and the local scrubbers on Seven haven't worked since the hundred and sixtieth year." She moved the mask, gently, and the boy's chest heaved and caught and heaved. "It's a contest, Maren. I told you. Who can wait longest. I didn't think they'd start with the children." She was quiet a moment. "I should have."

Maren knelt down on the other side of the cot. The boy's eyes were open, very wide, fixed on the ceiling. She took his hand. It was cold and wet and it closed on hers so hard it hurt.

"What's his name?"

"Teo Brand. Number Three head. His mother's a breather. She's up at the head with you." Hatch looked at her across the cot. "She doesn't know he's down here. I sent her up there myself this morning. I told her he'd be fine."

* * *

Teo Brand did not die that night, though four others did. Two of them were in the clinic, old men with the rattle who went in the small hours, one after the other, without making much noise about it. One was a baby on Level Eight whose mother brought her to the clinic door at the second bell of the morning, already gone, and stood in the doorway holding her, and would not come in, and would not go. And one was a man on Level Seven, a scrubber-tender on Ruth Calloway's crew, who had crawled into the old local plant with a lamp and a cutting bar to try to make it run and been found at dawn with the lamp still burning beside him, in the airless dark at the plant's heart where the carbon dioxide had pooled, like water in a sump.

By the morning of the nineteenth day there were people in the Number Four head who wanted to go up and fight.

Farrant was the loudest. He stood on the separator platform where the Factor had stood ten days before and shouted it at the crowd: *They're killing us. They're killing our parents and our babies in the dark. Are we going to sit here on the floor and sing while they do it?* And there was a roar back at him, a real roar, ugly and frightened, and Maren saw people she had known all her life with their fists up and their mouths open. She saw Corley, the young breather from Number One, among them. She saw a woman with a cutting bar.

Hatch came up from Nine at the third bell and stood on a crate by the separator base and waited for them to stop, the way she always did. It took longer than it ever had. When they did, she said only, very quietly:

"There are three hundred and forty marines on Hesper High. They are waiting for you to come up. They're *hoping* for it. And when you do, they will kill you in the shuttle locks, every one of you, and then they won't have to cut the air any more, will they? There'll be nobody left to strike." She looked at Farrant. "Is that what you want? To save them the bother?"

Farrant said nothing. But he did not get down.

* * *

The Combine's answer, the real one, came down at the fifth bell on the nineteenth day, on a single shuttle under guard, and it came with Lucan Vey in it.

He was the clerk. Maren saw him as the shuttle's party came through the gallery lock into the head — eight marines, a grey-coated woman Maren did not know, and behind her, carrying her slates, with his narrow face as blank as a wiped board, Lucan. The woman was Deputy Factor Annis Vole. She was perhaps fifty, small and plump and pleasant, with the manner of someone who negotiated for a living and was very good at it. She walked into the domed cavern of the silent head between her marines as though she were walking into a dinner, and smiled at the four hundred bonds sitting on its floor, and asked, in a clear carrying voice, for Sister Agathe Hatch.

They met in the tally master's office: Vole and Lucan on one side of Siggard's desk, Hatch and Pym and Ade Okoro and Maren on the other, and marines at the door. Vole did not sit. She laid out the Combine's terms standing, briskly, as if reading a menu.

The strike would end. The heads would reopen at first shift tomorrow and remain open until the convoy's departure. In exchange, air circulation would be restored to the lower levels within the hour, and no bond would be charged for the hours lost. No disciplinary action would be taken against any individual.

"And the wards," said Hatch.

"The Scholarship stands," said Vole pleasantly. "That isn't negotiable, I'm afraid. The contracts are committed. But the Factor is very conscious of the distress this has caused the families, and he wishes to make a gesture." She glanced at Lucan, who lifted a slate and read from it, in a flat clerk's voice that did not sound like his own.

"The Factor will permit the families of Scholarship wards to attend a farewell on Hesper High, at the convoy docks, before embarkation. Parents will be brought up by shuttle. They will be permitted to see their children in person, without the Nursery glass, for one hour, before the wards are embarked on the *Grace*."

There was a silence in the office.

"Without the glass," said Ade Okoro.

"Without the glass," said Vole. "It's a great kindness, I think. It's never been done." She smiled. "Of course, it will need to be soon. I should tell you that the convoy schedule has been revised."

Maren's hands, under the desk, closed on her knees.

"Revised how?" said Hatch.

"The *Grace* departs in four days," said Deputy Factor Vole. "On the twenty-third. The farewell will be on the twenty-second. That gives the families time to prepare." She tilted her head. "The Combine thanks you for your patience."

* * *

Hatch asked for an hour to put the terms to the Sump. Vole granted it graciously and sat down, at last, in Siggard's chair, and asked whether there might be anything hot to drink. And in the confusion of the bonds leaving the office and the marines shifting at the door, Lucan Vey picked up one of his slates and said to no one in particular that he needed the latrine, and went out, and Maren gave it a count of twenty and followed.

The latrine for the tally house was a cold cell cut into the ice off the back corridor. He was standing in the middle of it with his back to the door, and when she came in he turned round and she saw that the blank board of his face had cracked straight across.

"I've got two minutes," he said. "Maybe less. Listen to me."

"Four days," she said. "You didn't tell me."

"I didn't know. It was decided last night. The strike frightened them — not because of the breath, because of what it meant, that you knew. If you knew about Schedule C you might know about everything. So they're going now. They've got enough breath in the tanks for the crossing. They'll sell less at Corvid. The board will be unhappy and the Factor will explain." He was talking very fast. "Your strike is over, Maren. It was over when they decided. There's nothing you can stop them doing by not diving, because they don't need you to dive any more."

"Then why the terms? Why the farewell?"

"Because they need the heads open for four days to fill the last tanks, and because it's cheaper than killing you all, and because—" He stopped. "Because the Factor wants the union in one place. On High. Where he can see it." He looked at her. "I don't know that. I'm guessing. I'm not in those meetings."

"And the air?"

"They'll turn it back on. They don't need you dead. Not yet." He put a hand over his face, and took it away. "That's not why I came. Listen. Listen to me. I found out something else."

"What?"

"The key," said Lucan. "The Gallows key. I told you the Factor holds it until the account is closed. I didn't know what that meant. Nobody in the office did; it's a phrase from the Clause, it's in the sealed annex. I got into the annex last night." He took a breath. "The *Grace* isn't going straight through. She stops at Gallows. The station crew come aboard — there's six of them, they're on the list for Schedule A, staff. And then the Factor goes to the master console, and puts in the key, and keys the pins to invert."

"Invert," said Maren.

"Run backward. The pins hold the Throat open by pushing on it. Invert them and they pull. All four together, on a timer — he sets it, and the *Grace* goes through, and two hours after she's clear, the pins invert, and the Throat collapses." His voice had gone very quiet. "Not narrows. Not eighteen months. Collapses. To a thread. In about ten minutes. Nothing could ever get through it again — not a ship, not a signal, not anything. Not for centuries. Maybe not ever."

"Why?"

"Because the Clause says so. *Upon withdrawal, the passage shall be closed to prevent fixed assets from following.*" He laughed, horribly. "That's you. Fixed assets. They're afraid you'll follow them. They're afraid somebody in the Sump will build a ship out of scrap and bring thirty thousand angry bonds through the Throat in five years' time and turn up at Corvid asking for their children back. So they're going to shut the door. And then they're going to put Vigil on the books as abandoned, and file the lien, and leave a receiver at the far mouth in case it ever opens, and forget you." He was trembling. "Eighteen months was a lie. It was for the board, it was for us, it was so the staff wouldn't ask questions. There's no eighteen months. There's no *maybe*. There's two hours after the *Grace* clears the mouth, and then you're shut in. Forever. With the twelve of us."

The latrine was very cold. Maren could hear water dripping somewhere in the ice, and the faint far-off voices of the bonds in the head.

"The key does that," she said.

"The key does that."

"Only the key."

"Only the key, at the master console, at Gallows." He looked at her. "Why?"

She did not answer for a long moment. She was thinking about the *Gannet* in a downdraft, falling, and the yellow lever under its hinged guard. She was thinking that every lever in the world worked both ways.

"If the pins can shut it behind them," said Maren slowly, "they can shut it in front of them."

Lucan Vey went white. Not pale; white, as though every drop of blood in his narrow face had gone somewhere else all at once. He stared at her. His mouth opened, and closed. And in the silence between them she heard the boots of a marine in the back corridor, coming to see what was keeping the clerk.

"I have to go," he whispered.

He went. He did not say anything else. He did not need to. She had seen his face.

* * *

Hatch put the terms to the Sump at the sixth bell, standing on her crate, and the Sump took them. Not happily. Not all of them. But the fans on Nine had been off for thirty hours and there were nine dead now, and the farewell — *without the glass, for one hour* — went through the four hundred bonds in the head like warm water, and Maren saw it go, saw it soften faces that had been fists. Lin Okoro was weeping again. Even Farrant got down off the platform.

They met on the water that night for the last time, the fourteen of them, on the iron platform in the middle of the Drowned Decks, and Hatch told them what she meant to do.

"The farewell's on the docks at High," she said. "Four hundred parents, four hundred children, in one place, with no glass between them. For an hour." She looked round at them in the lamplight. "Farrant's right. We can't take the *Grace*. But we don't need to take the *Grace*. We need to take the children."

They stared at her.

"The ward shuttles bring us up," said Hatch. "The ward shuttles take us down. Six of them, at the dock locks, waiting. The marines will be on the docks, but they'll be watching the parents, not the children; they won't think we'd dare, with the kids right there in the crossfire. So at the end of the hour, when they call for the embarkation—" she held up a hand and closed it "—every parent takes their child by the hand and walks into the nearest shuttle. Not the *Grace*. The shuttle. And Ruth's people and Ade's people, who'll be on the shuttle crews because we'll make sure of it, seal the locks and drop. And we bring them home."

"And then?" said Ruth Calloway. "They'll come down after us."

"Then they'll come down after us," said Hatch, "and we'll have four hundred children in the ice, and they'll have four days. They can come down and try to dig them out of the Sump one at a time, with the heads shut again and the whole Sump between them and the kids. Or they can leave on schedule, without their liquid assets." She smiled her thin smile. "I think they'll leave. I think they'll do the sum."

Nobody said anything.

"It's mad," said Farrant at last, almost reverently.

"Yes."

"It might work."

"It might," said Hatch. "Or they'll shoot us on the docks. I'll be honest with you; I don't know which. But I know that if we let them put those children on the *Grace*, we'll never see them again." She looked round at them. "Hands."

The hands went up. Maren's went up.

At the edge of the platform, Dov's went up too, slowly, last.

* * *

She found him afterward on the *Old Patience*, because she knew he would be there. The tender was docked in her cradle at the Number Four head's upper lock, cold and dark, her engines banked since the strike began; and Dov was in her cockpit with a hand-lamp on the console and a tally slip on his knee and a pencil, and a bottle of Ostrander's beside him that he had not opened.

"You weren't at the bar," she said, climbing in.

"I'm on strike." He did not look up. "Sit down. You're letting the cold in."

She sat in the co-seat. The cockpit of the *Patience* smelled of thirty years of Dov: oil, sweat, Ostrander's, the struck-match tang of breath. Above the console, taped to the glass, was a faded picture of a woman with black coarse hair and Maren's own jaw, in a breather's suit, laughing at whoever was holding the camera.

"Four days," said Dov.

"Four days."

"I heard." He wrote something on the slip. "And Hatch's plan. The shuttles."

"Yes."

"It's mad."

"Everybody keeps saying that."

"It's mad and it might work." He wrote again. "And if it doesn't."

She looked at him.

"I've known you thirty-eight years, girl," said Dov. "I held the light. You've had a look on your face since you came back up from that latrine this afternoon that I've only ever seen on one other person, and she's on my window." He nodded at the picture. "Your mother used to get it before a deep seam. When she'd already decided and was just waiting to tell me. So tell me."

Maren was quiet for a long time.

"How long to Gallows?" she said.

He put the pencil down.

"From here?"

"From here."

He didn't answer at once. He picked the pencil up again and looked at the slip on his knee, and she saw, upside down, that there were already figures on it — that he had been working something out before she came in, and that the something was this.

"The *Grace* at convoy speed, with escort," he said, "four days. She's heavy, she's got the stockpile, she'll be careful. The *Patience*, light, at a steady burn — six. She's not fast. She was never fast." He tapped the slip. "Unless."

"Unless what?"

"Unless you take her down the limb." He looked at her. "You've dived Tarrow your whole life. You know what it does when you go deep — how the giant grabs you. Take the *Patience* down past the cloud tops, on the right line, at the right speed, and light everything at the bottom of the fall, and the giant throws you. Like a stone from a sling. Your mother and I did it once, on a dare, half-loaded. Took a day and a half off a run to the Number Three relay." He shrugged. "Nearly killed us. The hull's never been right since."

"How fast?"

"Get it right, three and a half days. Get it wrong, you're a smear on the red." He turned the slip over. "Three and a half days, against the *Grace*'s four. And then you're at Gallows with half a day in hand, and an escort frigate sitting on the mouth that'll want to know what you're doing there, and pickets all round the station that'll burn anything that hasn't got a Combine code." He looked at her. "And then what? You haven't got the key."

"I know."

"Without the key, the console's just a box."

"I know."

"You'd shut thirty thousand people in a room with no door," said Dov quietly. "Forever. For one girl."

"They're already shut in. They just don't know it yet." She held his eye. "And it's not one. It's four hundred and twelve."

He looked at her for a very long time in the lamplight, with the picture of her mother laughing on the glass above his head. Then he reached for the bottle of Ostrander's, and turned it in his hands, and put it down again unopened.

"You're mad," he said.

"Will you work it out? The limb. The line and the speed. In case."

"In case." He picked up the pencil. "In case." He was writing again, fast, figures she could not follow. Without looking up, he said, "When's the farewell? Exactly. What bell?"

"First bell on the twenty-second. Why?"

"Need to know when I'll have to have her fuelled," said Dov Arkwright, and went on writing.


# Chapter 11: The First Book

*The Ledger · 2794*

The way in was behind the Count.

She found it in Brother Ruel's book-mending ledgers, which turned out to record not only every rebinding in the Nave but every repair to every object the Auditory held sacred, because the Auditory held its objects the way it held its hours — written down. In the hundred and ninety-first year of the Count, a plate on the Board had jammed in its turning, a seven in the millions column, and the smiths who came up from the Lungs to free it had entered their work in the mending ledger in a smith's blunt hand: *Plate 6-7 freed. Counterweight chain 6 re-hung. Access by the smiths' stair behind the Board, from the warden's gate, down to the weights.* And beneath that, in a different hand, a Prelate's clerk's: *The smiths to be escorted. The stair continues below the weights to the Reliquary antechamber. The lower gate to be kept locked.*

Kept locked. Not walled. Not filled. Locked, because the Prelate went down that way himself, once a year, on Founding Day, to pray.

She read the entry four times in the Edict Library with Brother Ruel humming at his desk across the room, and thought: *the smiths had to reach the weights. The weights hang below the Board. The Board stands over the Reliquary.* And she thought of the Ledger Hall at the first bell, the great plates turning, and how she had always felt the sound of it come up through the soles of her feet, as though the floor were hollow.

* * *

"No," said Corin.

"I wasn't asking."

"You're asking me to hide a grey robe in a smuggler's tank for a night and a day afterward. That's asking." He was behind his counter with the shop door locked and a valve in pieces in front of him, and he did not look up from it. "You want to break into the Reliquary of the Nave. The one place in this whole rotten system that even Bailiffs don't go without the Prelate holding their hands. You want to go down a stair that's been locked since before your grandmother's grandmother was born—"

"A hundred and fifty years. Exactly."

"—and steal a book that nobody but a Prelate has ever seen, and bring it down the ladders to the Lungs, to a bottle shop with *Nullist* practically painted on the door. And then what?"

"Then we'll have it."

"Then we'll have it." He set down his driver. "And what will we do with it, Sister? Read it out in the corridors? Tack it to the junction boards? You think the Lungs'll believe a page because you tell them it's from the First Book? They'll believe what the Prelate tells them on Sorrow Day. They always have."

"Then I'll make the Prelate read it."

He looked up at that.

"I don't know how yet," said Ysolde. "But Anselm was right. Sums won't do it. The Prelate's got sums of his own, and his are bigger, and he's very good at them. It has to be something older than the Count. Something the Count itself is built on." She held his eye. "The Stewards swore something when they took the account. That's what Anselm said. It's in the Book. If it's what I think it is—"

"What do you think it is?"

"I don't know," she said. "That's why I have to read it."

Corin Asch looked at her across his counter, among the bottles. His face was healing; the cut over his eye had closed, leaving a red line that would become a scar. He looked older than he had a week ago.

"Tamsin used to look at me like that," he said finally. "When she'd made up her mind to do something stupid and was just waiting for me to stop arguing." He picked up the driver again. "When?"

"Tonight. The middle bell. The Hall's empty but for the warden."

"And if they catch you?"

"Then I'll have been to the Breath," said Ysolde, "and you'll have one less auditor to worry about."

He didn't laugh. He bent over the valve.

"The tank'll be open," he said. "Come to the back. Don't come to the front."

* * *

The warden of the Ledger Hall at night was a lay brother called Haskel, who was very old and very deaf and who sat in a booth by the great doors with a lamp and a pot of tea and a book of Okafor, reading the *Reckoning of the Starving* for perhaps the fortieth time in his life. Ysolde had been coming to the Turning for twenty-two years. She had passed Haskel's booth ten thousand times. He knew her face. When she came in at a little after the middle bell, in her grey robe, with her satchel on her shoulder, he looked up and smiled at her vaguely and said, "Early for the Turning, Sister," and she said, "I couldn't sleep," and he nodded and went back to his book, and that was all.

She walked the length of the Hall in the dark. The vault went up over her head into blackness, ribbed and curved. Her footsteps rang. At the far end the Count Board stood like a cliff, its plates dim grey shapes in the light of the two night lamps that burned at its foot, and above it, invisible, the words she did not need to see. She stood at the rail where the pilgrims stood and looked up at the number. She could read it from here, just.

**3,906,792,0—**

The warden's gate was at the Board's left-hand edge: a low iron door in the frame of the great machine, painted the same black as the plates, so that you would only see it if you knew to look. It had a lock. She had a keeper's key, which did not fit it, and a smith's pick that Corin had given her from a drawer in his shop without comment, which did. It took her eleven minutes, crouched in the dark with her heart going and her ears straining for Haskel's tea-cup. Then the lock turned, and she slipped through, and pulled the door to behind her.

Inside, behind the Board, it was another world.

She had always imagined the Count Board as a wall. It was not. It was a forest. Behind the plates, filling a space as deep as a Chancery and as high as the Hall itself, stood the machinery that turned them: great wheels and spindles of black iron, ratchets and pawls as big as her body, and hanging between them, from the top of the dark all the way down, the chains. Ten of them — one for each column — each link as long as her forearm, and on the end of each chain, far below, in the dark, a weight. The air smelled of oil and iron and cold. When she touched the nearest chain it was so cold it burned.

The smiths' stair was a ladder. It went down past the weights.

She climbed. It was further than she had thought. The weights hung in the dark around her like the bodies of great animals, iron cylinders the size of drop shuttles, motionless, waiting for the first bell to drop them a link or two and turn the Count; and below them the ladder went on, down through a hole in the deck into a darkness that was colder still. She went down it hand over hand with her lamp swinging from her wrist. She counted rungs. A hundred and twelve. A hundred and thirteen. At a hundred and forty her feet found a floor.

She held up the lamp.

It was a gate — iron bars, floor to ceiling, with a lock as big as her two fists — and beyond it a short corridor, and beyond that, an opening into somewhere very large and very dark. The corridor walls were not like the Nave's walls. They curved. Not outward, the way the Hall's vault curved, but inward, toward each other, so that the corridor was round, like the inside of a pipe; and they were ribbed, every half-metre, with heavy metal hoops; and set into them at intervals, at odd heights, were fittings she did not recognise — brackets, clamps, little round hatches with wheel locks, a row of hand-grips running along the ceiling as though whoever had built this place had expected to walk on the walls.

The lock on the gate was older than the warden's, and the pick took longer. Twenty minutes. Twenty-five. Her fingers were stiff with the cold and twice she dropped the pick and had to grope for it on the frozen floor. And then something gave inside the lock with a clank that rang down the round corridor like a bell, and she waited, crouched, not breathing, for a full count of a hundred, and nothing came.

She pushed the gate open, and went into the Reliquary.

* * *

She had imagined, as a novice, that it would be beautiful. All the novices did. They told each other stories in the dormitory: a hall of white stone; a great golden book on an altar; the First Auditor's bones in a crystal case, still holding her pen. It was nothing like that.

It was a cave of metal. Long and low and ribbed, like the corridor but vastly bigger, with a curved ceiling that came down to meet the walls without corners, so that she felt as though she had walked into the bones of something — the ribcage of some enormous dead creature, long since picked clean. The air was dead still and very cold. Her lamp found shapes in the dark, and she went among them slowly, one at a time.

There were seats. Rows of them, bolted to the floor, deep padded seats with straps and buckles, the padding long since rotted to dust and the straps gone stiff and black. They faced the far end of the space, all of them, the way the benches of the Ledger Hall faced the Board. There were consoles along one wall, dead screens of dark glass in frames of grey metal, with ranks of switches under them and labels in the old square hand, the Combine's hand, too faded to read. There were hatches in the floor with wheel locks. This, she supposed, was what the Nave had been before it was the Nave: the Stewards' own station, the old Combine hub, buried at the heart of everything that had been built around it since, the way the stone of a fruit is buried in the flesh.

And there were the niches. Twelve of them, cut into the curved wall to her left, each the size of a cot and sealed with a plate of grey metal. Each plate bore a single line of lettering, cut deep. Not a name. A title. STEWARD OF BREATH. STEWARD OF SEED. STEWARD OF AIR. STEWARD OF WATER. STEWARD OF STORES. STEWARD OF LAW. Six more, down the row. The last plate, at the end, nearer the far wall than the others, said only STEWARD, and nothing else; and beneath it, smaller, a line she had to hold the lamp close to read.

*He kept the account.*

She stood before it for a long moment. Then she went on.

At the far end of the space, where all the seats faced, there was a chair. It was plain, metal, with a straight back and no padding, the kind of chair that stood at every desk in every Chancery in the Nave; it had been bolted to a low platform where some larger thing had once been mounted, and the bolts that had held the larger thing were still there, cut off flush. Something hung from the chair's left arm on a cord.

She held up the lamp.

It was a tag. A diver's tag, like the ones the Lungs folk wore, like Piet's: a half-disc of steel with a broken toothed edge. Its face had been worn so smooth that it shone like water in the lamplight, every stamping gone, kissed away, she understood, by three centuries of Prelates kneeling where she was standing now and pressing their lips to it on Founding Day. The cord it hung from was new. Someone had replaced it, recently. Someone replaced it, she supposed, every few years, as it rotted, and had done for three hundred years, and would go on doing.

The First Auditor's chair. The First Auditor's tag. She had worn it, then. She had come from the Lungs.

And beside the chair, on a lectern under a dome of clouded glass, lay the Book.

* * *

It was not golden. It was a great block of pale grey sheets, thicker than her arm was long, bound between boards of the old Combine plastic with heavy black thread, and it had been printed — not written: printed, by a machine, the way the Combine had printed things — in tiny square characters, column after column, page after page. She lifted the glass dome off it with both hands. It was lighter than she expected. She set it down on the floor, and drew her sleeve across her eyes, because they had started, unaccountably, to sting, and opened the Book.

The first page was a heading, printed.

> HALLORAN-VASQUEZ COMBINE
> ACCOUNT HV-VIGIL-0001
> CONSOLIDATED STATEMENT AT CLOSURE OF PASSAGE

Below it, in columns, the account. Assets. Liabilities. She knew the forms; she had been trained in them; every Ledger in the Nave was kept in them still, though she had never until this moment understood that they were not the Auditory's forms at all but someone else's, borrowed and copied and kept. She ran her eye down the columns the way she would run it down any balance sheet in the Third. Fixed assets: the Sump, the refinery heads, the station. Stores. Seed. And under liabilities, a long list of numbers that she realised, after a moment, were bonds — individual debts, one per line, thousands of them, the debts of thirty thousand people summed to a total at the foot of the column.

And the total — the consolidated liability of every bond in the Sump to the Combine, at the moment the Door was shut — was a hundred and eighteen million, four hundred thousand marks.

Not four hundred and six million. Not the cost of our bringing, on the first page of every primer. A hundred and eighteen.

She turned the page. Terms. The contract interest on the bonds: a single plain rate, printed, the same for every line. Beside it, in the margin, very small, someone had pencilled a circle with a stroke through it, the little symbol she had seen on Anselm's sheet — and she understood that it was not a symbol at all but a notation, the Combine's own, printed here a dozen times in the original: the mark for *per annum*. *F.T.* Founding Terms. This was what Anselm had seen, forty years ago, on a single page left out on old Prelate Hanne's desk. This was what he had built his whole sheet on.

She did the sum in her head, standing at the lectern with the lamp hooked over her wrist, without meaning to. She didn't need to finish it. She already knew where it went. *Zero. Year 280.*

She turned the page again, and found the Compact.

It was written, not printed: a single sheet of the Combine's grey stock, covered in a large, careful, upright hand in ink that had gone the brown of old blood. It had been bound into the Book after the printed pages, sewn in on its own thread. Its heading was in capitals.

> THE COMPACT OF THE STEWARDS
>
> We, the Stewards of account HV-VIGIL-0001, being left in charge of the account upon the closing of the Door, and having no hope of relief, do swear among ourselves:
>
> That the Door is closed, and shall be taught as closed against them, for the debt.
>
> That the account shall be kept in the forms of the Combine, every hour of labour entered against the Number and every issue of stores against it likewise, so that if Recovery should ever come, it will find the books in order.
>
> That the Founding shall be taught as the cost of the bringing, and not as it is written here.
>
> That no soul shall go to Gallows. What was done at Gallows is accounted and closed. The Gallows log shall be left in the dark, and no ship shall approach it.
>
> That the Count shall bind them, for nothing else will.

Beneath it were signatures. Twelve. She held the lamp close. They were brown, illegible — scrawls, initials, a few looping marks that might once have been names and were now just ink. She could not read a single one of them.

She stood very still.

*Taught as closed against them.* Not *us.* Them. *For the debt.* She read it again, and again, the way she had read Anselm's sheet that first night, and each time it said the same thing, in the same careful hand, the same brown ink: that twelve people had sat together in this Nave in the first year after the Door was shut, with thirty thousand starving people below them in the ice, and had agreed among themselves what those people would be told. That the Door was shut because they owed. That what they owed was four hundred and six million marks. That the Count would bind them, for nothing else would.

And Gallows.

She did not know the word. She tried to place it and could not. It was not a Chancery word or a Lungs word or a word from the catechism. It was not in Okafor. *What was done at Gallows is accounted and closed.* Something had been done somewhere, at a place called Gallows, in the first days, and twelve frightened people in this metal cave had written it down as closed, and forbidden anyone ever to go there. *The Gallows log shall be left in the dark.*

A log. A record. Somewhere there was a record of what had been done.

She turned the page.

The next sheet was printed again, in the same tiny square characters as the account. Its heading was a single word.

> WARDS

Beneath it, columns. Numbers and names. She saw the first line — a number, a surname, an initial, a figure — and her eye began, automatically, to run down to the next, the way it ran down any column.

And somewhere above her, very faintly, through a hundred and forty rungs of ladder and the iron forest of the Count Board, she heard a door open.

* * *

She did not think. She was later rather proud of that. She took the Founding page in her left hand and the Compact in her right and tore them both out of the First Book, close to the spine, in two long rips that sounded in the dead air of the Reliquary like cloth splitting. She folded them twice and pushed them down the front of her robe, against her skin, where Anselm's sheet already lay. She put the glass dome back over the Book. She did not know why she did that; it made no difference. It only seemed wrong to leave it bare.

Then she put out her lamp and stood in the dark and listened.

Footsteps on the ladder. More than one pair. Coming down fast, the way men come down a ladder when they have been told to hurry. A light, swinging, far up; she could see it through the round corridor, a little glow on the ribs of the walls. And a voice, quiet, pleasant, unhurried, that she had last heard through the side of a smuggler's tank.

"Sister Marr. I know you're down there."

She did not answer.

"The warden saw you go to the gate. He's deaf, not blind. He came and found a Bailiff, because he's a good old man and he's been warden of that Hall for forty years and he'd never seen anyone go through that gate who wasn't a Prelate."
