Strand's voice came nearer. "I don't want to hurt you, Sister. The Prelate's asked me not to. He's very fond of you."

She moved. Not toward the corridor — away from it, along the curved wall, past the twelve niches, feeling her way in the absolute dark with one hand on the cold ribbed metal. She had seen something when her lamp was lit, at the far end of the row of seats: a second opening, low in the wall, a round hatch standing ajar. She had not looked through it. She found it by touch. It was cold and heavy and it moved when she pushed it, with a groan she was sure must be heard in the Ledger Hall.

Behind her the gate clanged. Light flooded the Reliquary — two lamps, three — and she saw her own shadow leap ahead of her through the hatch, long and black on the curved floor of another round corridor, going up.

"There," said Strand.

She ran.

* * *

She did not know where it went. She learned afterward, from the Nave plans in the Edict Library that she was never again able to consult, that the round corridor beyond the hatch was one of four that ran up from the Reliquary through the ancient core of the Nave to the old hub docks, and that it had not been used in three hundred years, and that its far end opened into a disused maintenance gallery under the drop dock where the night shuttle to the Lungs was loaded. She did not know any of that at the time. She knew that it went up, and that it had hand-grips on its ceiling, and that the men behind her were bigger and faster than she was and had lamps. She climbed. She used the hand-grips on the ceiling, as whoever built the place had meant them to be used, and hauled herself up the round dark throat of the corridor with the torn pages crackling against her breastbone and the light of the Bailiffs' lamps swinging on the walls below her, and she did not look back.

There was a moment — she would remember it afterward, in the long dark of the trip to the Door — when she was halfway up and her hands slipped on a frosted grip and she hung by one arm in the dark, and the light below her steadied and found her, and she looked down and saw Strand's face in it, turned up toward her, pale-eyed, almost close enough to touch.

He was not reaching for her. He was just looking.

"You can stop," he said. "You know that. You can come down, and I'll take you to the Prelate, and he'll talk to you, and it'll be all right. He'll forgive you. He forgives everybody." His face did something strange, a kind of flinch. "He forgave Anselm, right up until the end."

She let go of him with her eyes, and found the grip again with her other hand, and climbed.

He did not follow fast. She did not understand that until much later either.

* * *

She came out of the round corridor through a hatch in the floor of a gallery full of dust and broken crates, and saw lamplight through a grating, and heard the clank and shout of the drop dock beyond it, the night crews loading meal sacks into the hold of the last shuttle down. She went through the grating. She walked across the loading floor in her grey robe with her hair coming down and grease on her hands and her face set in the steadiness the testers in the Lungs had written down when she was nine, and nobody stopped her, because nobody on a drop dock at the third bell of the morning stops an auditor. She walked up the cargo ramp of the shuttle into its hold and sat down among the meal sacks in the dark.

The shuttle dropped at the fourth bell. Forty minutes. She sat with her knees drawn up and her hands pressed flat against her chest, over the pages, and felt the Nave fall away above her.

Corin had left the back door unbarred. The tank was open. He did not say anything when she came in; he only looked at her face, and at her hands, and pointed. She climbed in. Before he put the lid on she reached up and caught his wrist.

"It's real," she said. "It's all real. It's worse."

He looked down at her for a moment in the dim light of the back room.

"Of course it's worse," said Corin Asch. "It always is." And he lowered the lid.

* * *

She lay in the dark of the tank with the pages against her heart until morning. She heard the Lungs wake up around her — the pumps, the voices, the clang of the bottle racks in the corridor outside. And at the first bell, faint and tinny through the speaker-wire that ran along every corridor in the Lungs and into every shop and every cell, she heard the Turning: the far-off rain-sound of the Count Board changing, plate after plate, right to left.

It ran a little longer than usual. She did not know why until it stopped. And then the speaker-wire crackled, and a voice came down it that was not the Prelate's — a clerk's voice, dry and precise, the voice of the Chancery of Law, which made the Auditory's few announcements that were not the Turning.

"*Hear the Count,*" said the voice. "*By order of the Prelate-Auditor, in the three hundred and forty-first year: Ysolde Marr, Sister, of the Third Chancery, keeper of the Ninth Ledger, born in the Lungs on Spoke Eleven, is found in default of all her shares and all her hours. Her account is closed. She is no longer of the Count.*" A pause. "*Let no one give her bread or bed or breath. Let any who find her bring her to the Bailiffs. It is just.*"

And from the Lungs outside — from the corridors and the cells and the bottle racks, from a hundred thousand mouths, faint and far and multiplied, the way she had heard it every Sorrow Day of her childhood standing in the cold with her mother's hand on her shoulder — the whisper came back.

*It is just.*


# Chapter 12: Receivers

*The Door · 3190*

They came through on the fourth day, a little after the eleventh hour, and Haddie was watching when they did. She'd hardly stopped watching since we got to the Hulk. She'd set her little glass up at the cracked port of the dead dock where I kept the *Mendicant* and she sat beside it on an upturned crate in two coats with a blanket over her knees and a Count-book in her lap, and every hour she'd lean in to the eyepiece and look, and write a number down, and lean back.

The numbers had stopped making sense on the second day. She'd told me so, quite calmly, the way you'd tell somebody the scrubbers were failing. *It's not breathing any more, Absalom. It's opening. Like a mouth.*

I was in the hall with the children when she called me. I'd been trying to get the old scrubber to run on less, because thirty-four people breathing in a room built for none is a sum that doesn't come out. The children were sitting in the curve of the vault under the strung lamps in little knots, the way they'd sat in their bunks at Kell, and Wren was going between them with the water, and Kesse was teaching the small ones a counting game with the white numbers off the fallen plates. Somebody had made a tower out of three of them — a nine, a two, a nought. They'd been at it all morning. It was the first time I'd heard any of them laugh.

"Rook!" Haddie's voice, down the long corridor from the dock. "Rook, come here. Come *now*."

I came. Wren came behind me.

Haddie didn't take her eye from the glass. She just held out her hand behind her, flapping it, and I put mine in it, and she pulled me down and pushed me at the eyepiece.

I don't know what I'd expected. I'd looked at the Door through that glass a dozen times by then, and it had always looked the same: a little round blur of wrong stars, bigger every time, like a coin sinking in dark water. It wasn't that now. The middle of the coin had gone black — not the black of space, but a deeper black, a hole in the black — and round its rim the smeared stars were moving. Turning. Pouring inward, slowly, like water round a drain.

And out of the hole, as I watched, things were coming.

They were small at that distance — specks, sparks — but there were a lot of them. They came out of the black at the Door's heart in a steady stream, like seeds blown off a head of grass, one after another after another, and as each one came clear of the rim it seemed to *open*: to put out vanes or wings or arms, I couldn't tell which, that caught the faint far light of Vigil and glinted. And then they turned. All of them, in the same moment, the way a shoal of fish turns. Toward us. Toward Tarrow. Toward the moons and the rings and the lash and everything that lived.

"How many?" I said. My voice came out strange.

"I stopped counting at two hundred," said Haddie.

* * *

Behind us, on the dock floor in its blanket, the herald stopped humming.

None of us moved. I kept my eye at the glass, watching the sparks pour out of the black. Haddie's hand was still in mine; I could feel it shaking. And then the voice came, the calm courteous voice, but not from the herald alone. From the herald and from the *Mendicant*'s cockpit speaker, twenty metres away through the open lock, and from the hand-radio in Wren's belt, and — faint, far, tinny — from some dead speaker somewhere back in the Hulk's corridors that had not made a sound in two hundred years. All at once. All the same words.

Haddie translated as it went. She didn't need to stop and work at it any more; she'd had four days of the herald saying the same things over and over, and she'd caught the shape of it now, the way you catch the shape of someone's accent. Her voice ran a half-beat behind the machine's, low and steady.

"*This is the receiver for account HV-VIGIL-0001.*

"*The passage is open. Recovery has commenced.*

"*Debtors are asked to remain in their habitations. Debtors will be inventoried in order. Debtors will be held in custody pending reconciliation.*

"*A factor has been appointed from among the debtors to assist the receiver. The factor is—*" Haddie stopped. Swallowed. "*The factor is Teodora Kask, of Kell Deep. The factor speaks for the receiver.*

"*The fixed asset known as Hesper High Station is designated the collection depot. Debtors in custody will be brought to the collection depot for transfer.*

"*Resistance to recovery constitutes a further default.*

"*This notice will repeat.*"

The voice stopped. The herald began to hum.

I took my eye from the glass.

"Hesper High Station," I said. "What's that?"

Haddie didn't answer. She was looking past me, out through the cracked port, not at the Door but down: at the great dark curve of the moon below us, white and grey and scarred. And then at the dock around us. The dead consoles. The old hand painted on the walls.

"It's here," she said. "Isn't it."

Wren had gone very still in the dock doorway.

"It's here," said Haddie again. "This. The Hulk. It was something before it was the Hulk; it had to be. Somebody built it. Somebody owned it." She looked at me, and her old face was grey. "They're going to bring everybody here, Absalom. Everybody they take. They're bringing them *here*."

Behind Wren, down the long corridor, faint and sweet, I could hear the children in the hall still laughing at their counting game.

* * *

We went to Lanthorn. I didn't decide to. I just found myself in the *Mendicant*'s cockpit with the lock sealing behind me, and Wren in the co-seat with her helmet on her knees, having followed me without asking whether she could.

"You should stay," I said. "With them."

"Haddie's with them. Kesse's with them." She strapped in. "You're going for the old woman. The blind one. Yelland."

"And Haddie's sister's lad on Number Four. And the Fenns. And whoever else." I lit the drive. "I left them there. I walked out of that cargo pod and left them."

"They were going to give me to Kask for a tenth more breath," said Wren.

"I know."

"I'm only saying," said Wren. "So you know I know." And she put her helmet on.

* * *

It was six hours from the Hulk to the lash, and they'd beaten us there by two.

I've tried to think how to tell it. I keep wanting to make it sound like something — like a battle, like a fire, like a storm — and it wasn't any of those. It was quiet. That's what I remember. It was so orderly and so quiet.

The tenders — that's what Haddie called them, after; the word was in the notice, she said, she'd just not understood it — were big. Bigger than they'd looked through the glass. Each one was the size of the *Mendicant* three times over, black like the herald was black, so your eye slid off them, and they'd folded their vanes back now and put out legs instead. Six legs, long and jointed. And they'd come down on the hulks of Lanthorn the way a tick comes down on a dog, and *held on*, and where their bodies touched the hulls a faint red light was showing, a seam of it, where they were cutting in.

There were twenty or thirty on the lash already. More coming. As we came in through the ring shadow I watched one settle on the old ore hulk at the lash's edge, slow and careful, legs first, as if it didn't want to wake anything.

And moving among them, in and out of the light, little and quick and red: the Margrave's cutters.

"She's showing them," said Wren. Her voice in my helmet was flat. "Look. Look where they're going. They're going to the big hulls first. Six. Two. The tank hull. The ones with the most people."

She was right. Every tender that came in to land had a red cutter going ahead of it, like a dog in front of a herd, and every one landed on a hull where I knew there were hundreds living. The count. *Name, age, health, trade.* Kask had her lists already. She'd had her reeves out collecting them for four days. And now she was using them to tell the receiver where to start.

"Number Two," I said. "Yelland's hull. Is there one on it?"

"Not yet."

I put the *Mendicant* down hard on Number Two's dock and didn't bother with the lock cycle; I blew it. We went in at a run.

* * *

The corridors of Number Two were full of people, and none of them were running. They were just standing. Hundreds of them, in the passages and the doorways and the common rooms, holding on to each other and looking at the ceiling, where — through the plating, through the old foam, through two centuries of patches — they could hear it coming: a long, slow, scraping sound, metal on metal, like something very large settling itself.

I found Mother Yelland in the cargo pod, at the head of the Council table, alone. She'd sent everyone else away. She was sitting in her chair with her blind face turned toward the door and her two hands flat on the table, the way she'd sat to quiet them all for sixty years.

"Absalom Rook," she said, before I'd said a word. "I'd know that walk anywhere. You've come back."

"I've come for you, Mother. There's a boat on your dock. Come on."

"No."

"Mother—"

"I'm ninety, boy. I'm blind. I've been eldest of Number Two since before you were born." She didn't move her hands. "If they want to inventory me, they can come in here and do it. I'll tell them a thing or two." She smiled, a little. "Take the children. There's a dozen in the crèche on the lower deck. The Fenn girl's with them. Take them."

Over our heads the scraping stopped. And then, very faintly, through the deck, I heard a new sound — high and thin, like a saw.

"Go on," said Mother Yelland. "Go on. You've wasted enough of my time."

I went.

* * *

The crèche was on the lower deck, and the Fenn girl — old Fenn's granddaughter, a big serious girl of sixteen — had them all in the corner furthest from the door with a cutting bar in her hands. Eleven. The youngest was a baby in a sling across her chest. When she saw me in the doorway in my suit she raised the bar, and then she saw Wren behind me and lowered it, and her face crumpled.

"They're in the upper deck," she said. "They came through the roof. I heard them. I heard people—"

"I know. Come on."

We got them out. I'll not pretend it was anything clever. We just ran them, eleven children and a baby, down the corridors to the dock, the long way round, through the back passages behind the tank hull's fuel lines that only pickers and tenders know. And once — only once — at a junction where the back passage crossed one of the main corridors, I looked down it, and saw.

There were things in the corridor. They were smaller than the tenders, a lot smaller — about the size of a man, maybe, if a man were made of black jointed rods and walked on six legs and had no head. They were moving down the corridor in a line, not fast, not slow, and the people standing in the corridor weren't running from them. I don't know why. I think they were too frightened. Or I think that, after a whole life on Lanthorn under the Margrave, they'd learned the same thing the children at Kell Deep had learned: that you don't run, because nobody's listening.

The things reached a woman standing in a doorway. One of them put out two of its rods and touched her, very gently, on the shoulders. And something came out of it — out of its middle, where a body would be — something pale and soft and glistening, like a bubble of oil, that unfolded over her the way a hand closes, from her shoulders down, until she was inside it. Standing up. Her mouth open. I could see her through it, the way you see something through water. And then it went opaque, and hard, and grey, and the thing lifted it — lifted her — and passed it back down the line to the next one, which passed it to the next, and the first one moved on to the next doorway.

Like a chain of people passing buckets.

"Rook," said Wren behind me.

Further down the corridor, in among the black things, there was a woman walking toward them. She was old and she was wearing her best coat and she had her hands pressed together under her chin, and she was smiling. I knew her. It was Dorrit, from the Council meeting. The believer. *For the Stewards, for the Combine.* She walked right up to the nearest of the black things and stood in front of it, smiling up at where its face would be, with tears running down her cheeks, and said something I couldn't hear.

It touched her shoulders. Very gently.

"*Rook,*" said Wren.

I turned away and ran the children to the boat.

* * *

The *Mendicant* got off Number Two's dock with eleven children, a baby, a sixteen-year-old girl with a cutting bar, Wren and me, and we'd been gone from the dock perhaps four minutes when the tender that had settled on the roof of Number Two put a leg through the cargo pod. I saw it on the aft scope. One long black jointed leg, coming down through the roof of the old pod where the Lash Council had sat round its table for a century, slow and careful, like someone feeling for a step in the dark.

I didn't say anything. Neither did Wren.

We came out of the lash on the dark side, low, through the wreck-field, the way I'd come out of it running from Benno Strick, and nothing followed us. Nothing even looked. The tenders were busy, and the Margrave's cutters were busy showing them where to be busy. And as we cleared the ring shadow every speaker on my boat came on at once, the calm voice speaking its notice again, and under it — on the general band, cutting across — another voice.

"*People of the system,*" said the Margrave Kask. "*You've heard the receiver. I've spoken with it. It has made me its factor.*" A pause. "*I know you're frightened. I know it seems hard. But the receiver tells me that every debtor in custody will be held safely at the collection depot until the account is reconciled. Nobody will be harmed. And I am working, every hour, to get us the best terms.*"

Another pause. That voice. That hand on the back of your neck.

"*Don't resist,*" said the Margrave. "*It counts as a further default. I've asked the receiver what that means and it's been very clear. Stay in your hulls. Let them count you. And I'll get you home.*"

"*Home,*" said Wren, very softly, beside me.

In the cabin behind us, one of the small ones had started to cry, and the Fenn girl was hushing it, and the baby in her sling had begun to wail, and the calm voice of the receiver went on and on over all of it, word for word the same, as patient as the cold.

* * *

Haddie had found the word while we were gone.

She met us at the dead dock with a Count-book in her arms and a lamp in her fist and such a look on her face that I thought, for a moment, one of the children had died. Then I saw she was holding the book open against her chest, the way you'd hold a baby, and that her eyes were shining.

"Where have you been? No — I know where you've been. How many?" She looked past me at the children coming out of the lock, the Fenn girl and her baby. "Oh. Oh, good. Good. Get them warm." Then back to me. "Absalom. I've found it. Come and see. I've found where it was held."

"Where what was held?"

"*The Door,*" said Haddie Coyle.

* * *

She showed me in the hall, at a table she'd made out of one of the fallen plates laid across two crates — a big black seven, upside down, with the Count-book open on it under the lamp. It was the oldest of her nine. She'd always said so: the pages were a different stuff from the others, thin and yellow and brittle, the old Combine plastic, and the hand on them was different too, smaller and straighter, all in a brown ink gone the colour of dried blood.

"I never read this one properly," she said. "I never could. It's the oldest of them, and the hand's the hardest, and most of it's just numbers — columns and columns, like all the others. I always thought it was the same as the others. Labour, hours. That kind of thing." She turned the pages carefully. "But it's not. It's older than that. It's from before. It's from when the Door was still *open*."

"How do you know?"

"Because of what it's counting." She put her bent finger on a line. "Look. That word. It's on every page, in the second column. I didn't know it. I'd never seen it anywhere else. I thought it was a name. But I've been listening to that thing—" she jerked her head at the herald, humming on its blanket at the end of the table "—for four days now, saying *passage*, *passage*, *passage*, and I heard it, last night, and I thought, I know that shape. I've seen that shape." She tapped the page. "It's *passage*. It's the same word. And the word beside it, every time, is this."

I leaned in. Beside the word she'd shown me was a short run of the old figures, the same shapes as the white marks on the fallen plates.

"That's a number," said Haddie. "A charge. What it cost. Every page. And the column it's in is headed—" she turned back, to the top of the page, and put her finger under a word written larger than the others, in the same brown ink "—this."

"What is it?"

"I think," said Haddie slowly, "it says *pins*." She looked up at me. "And the heading over the whole page, the place where all of it's being charged — where the cost of the passage is being kept — is here."

There was a word at the head of the page, underlined. Seven letters. I couldn't read them. I'd never been able to read them.

"Gallows," said Haddie.

The herald hummed. Somewhere down the hall a child coughed.

"I don't know what it means," I said.

"Neither do I. Not the word." She took her spectacles off and rubbed her eyes; her hands were shaking again. "But I know what it is. Think, Absalom. *Pins.* *Passage.* *Gallows.* The cost of keeping the Door open, charged every month, in the oldest book there is. Somebody was *holding it*. Back then, back before, when the Door was open and ships went through it. Something was holding it open. And whoever wrote this book was paying the bill." She put her finger on the word again. "And the bill was charged to here. To a place. To something called Gallows."

"The Spider," said Wren.

We both looked at her. She'd come up behind us without either of us hearing, the way she did, and was standing at the end of the table with the water bulb in her hand, looking down at the page.

"The Spider," she said again. "By the Door. The old dead thing. You can see it from Kell, if you go up on the chasm lip on a clear shift with a glass — the vent-runners used to, on dares, for luck. It's tiny. It's right by the Door, just off the edge of it, and it's got legs." She held up her hand, the fingers hanging down. "Four of them. Long ones. Hanging. Like a spider on a thread. Like—" She stopped.

"Like a gallows," said Haddie softly. "With four hanged men on it."

None of us said anything for a while.

"If something at Gallows held the Door open," I said at last, "back then—"

"Then something at Gallows could close it," said Haddie. "Or did close it. Or *can*." She put her spectacles back on. "I don't know, Absalom. I'm guessing. It's a word in a book. But that thing—" the herald "—said *receiver*. It said *the passage is open*. And there are two hundred of those black things already through that Door and taking people out of their beds on Lanthorn, and there's more coming, and I looked through my glass this morning before they came out and I saw something behind them. In the black. Behind the Door." She was very quiet. "Something big. Too big to come through. Not yet. Waiting for the Door to open wider."

"The receiver," I said.

"The receiver. The thing that sent the herald and the tenders. The thing that's *coming*. And when the Door's wide enough, it'll come through, and every one of those pods they're filling on Lanthorn will be carried here, to this station, to the collection depot, and loaded onto it. And then it'll go back the way it came." She looked at me. "And we'll never see any of them again."

The hall was very quiet. The children had stopped their game. Somewhere among them, the baby from Number Two had gone to sleep.

"So we shut it," I said.

Haddie didn't answer.

"We go to the Spider," I said, "to Gallows, whatever it is, and we find whatever it was that held the Door, and we shut it. Before the big one comes through. We shut it on them."

"With what? We don't know what's there. We don't know if anything's there. It's been dead for longer than there's been a Lanthorn."

"So we go and look."

"In what? The *Mendicant*'s got two days of breath in her tanks, Absalom. I checked. The Door's five days out at a steady burn. And everything that burns breath in this system belongs to Kask, and Kask belongs to *that*." She jabbed a finger at the herald. "Where are you going to get breath?"

"The Kettles."

"The Kettles are Kask's too."

"The Kettles are anybody's who'll pay." I was already standing. "There's a breath-seller on the Number Three Kettle who used to sell to the reeves under the counter, when Kask's rations ran short. He owes me. I'll get enough for the run there and back."

"And the children?" said Wren.

I looked at her. She was standing at the end of the table with the water bulb in her burned hand, looking at me — the gangway look, the chin-up look — and behind her, under the strung lamps, in the curve of the great ribbed vault, forty-odd children sat among the fallen numbers of something none of us understood, and looked at me too.

"*The fixed asset known as Hesper High Station,*" said Haddie quietly, "*is designated the collection depot.*"

"I know."

"They're bringing everybody here, Absalom. Kask knows this place better than you do. She'll be here inside two days to get it ready. Her reeves. Those things."

"I know," I said. "So you'll take them deeper."

"Deeper where?"

"Into the core. Below this. There's a gate at the end of this hall, behind the plates, and a stair, and below it there's rooms nobody's ever picked because the stair's half fallen and the air's gone. I've been down there once. There's a place with seats." I looked at Haddie. "Round corridors. Old. You can seal them. I'll leave you the suit patches and the spare scrubber. It'll be cold. But they won't look there first. They'll fill the docks and the halls and the rings first. It'll take them days to get down that far."

"And you'll be back."

"In two days," I said. "With breath. And then we'll take everybody we can carry, and go to Gallows, and see."

Haddie looked at me over her spectacles. Then she closed the oldest Count-book, very carefully, and held it against her chest.

"Two days," she said.

"Two days."

Wren didn't say anything. She just turned away and went back down the hall to the children, and began, in a low voice, to tell them to get their boots.

* * *

I took the *Mendicant* out of the dead dock an hour later, alone, with the drive banked down and her lights dark. I looked back once, as I cleared the Hulk's outer ring. I don't know why. Habit, maybe. I've always looked back.

The station hung there against the moon the way it always had, black and dead and enormous, spokes and rings and towers, a great dark chandelier somebody had dropped and never swept up. And beyond it, out past Hesper's limb, low against the stars — so far off it was hardly more than a speck, so small that if I hadn't known to look I'd never have seen it — something was moving. Several somethings. Tiny glints, catching the faint far light of Vigil.

Coming this way.


# Chapter 13: The Scholarship

*The Bond · 2431*

The convoy dock at Hesper High was the biggest room Maren had ever stood in, and it was full of children.

She had seen it from the outside a hundred times, coming up on the visitation shuttle: a great bulge on the station's spindle, ribbed with gantries, its outer face one long port of armoured glass. She had never been inside it. Bonds were not taken through the convoy dock; bonds came up through the ward locks at the other end of the station and went back down the same way. But this morning the six ward shuttles had docked at the convoy dock's lower berths, one after another, and the parents had come out of them in a long uncertain file into a space so high and wide that the far wall was hazy, and had stopped, all of them, just inside, and stared.

Through the long port, filling it from end to end, lay the *Grace*.

Maren had known she was big. Lucan had told her: a kilometre and a quarter, the largest ship in the Combine's Corvid fleet, built to carry a colony's staff and stores in a single run. Knowing it was nothing. She lay alongside the dock in Hesper's shadow like a whale beside a raft, her flank a curved grey cliff studded with ports and hatches and the black mouths of cargo bays, and down her side in letters each taller than a refinery head, white on grey, ran her name.

HALLORAN'S GRACE

A boarding tube ran from the dock's centre to a hatch in her flank, wide enough to drive a shuttle down. At its mouth stood a desk, and at the desk sat three clerks with slates. And between the parents and the desk, across the whole width of the dock floor, a line had been painted in yellow, and behind the yellow line, in rows, in their new blue uniforms with the H-V crest on the breast, stood four hundred and twelve children.

They were so quiet. That was what Maren remembered afterward, more than anything. Four hundred and twelve children and not one of them making a sound.

"*Families of Vigil,*" said a voice from the dock's speakers. It was a woman's voice, warm and pleasant. Deputy Factor Vole. "*Welcome. You may cross the line.*"

* * *

She found Pell by not looking.

She had known she would not be able to see her in the rows, so she did not try. She crossed the yellow line with the others in a rush that was half a stumble, four hundred parents breaking all at once into the rows of children like water into sand, and she stood still in the middle of it with her eyes shut and listened, the way she listened in the dark of a dive for the change in the hull's note; and somewhere off to her left, among all the voices that had suddenly started at once — the sobbing and the names and the laughter — she heard one that said, perfectly clearly, in a tone of mild exasperation:

"You're standing in the wrong *row*."

She opened her eyes and turned, and her daughter walked into her.

There was no glass.

She had thought about it every night for four days, since Vole had read out the terms in Siggard's office. What it would be like. She had imagined it a hundred ways. None of them had been anything like it. Pell was taller than she was ready for, and thinner, all bones under the stiff new uniform, and she was warm. That was the thing. She was *warm*. Maren had not touched her daughter in six years, and she had forgotten, somehow, that she would be warm — that she would have weight, and a smell, soap and something under it that was only Pell, the smell of the top of her head that Maren had breathed in every night for six years in the crèche on Level Four and then never again. Pell's arms went round her neck and Pell's face went into her shoulder, and Maren held her, and held her, and could not get her close enough.

"You're squashing me," said Pell, into her shoulder, muffled.

"I know."

"It's fine. I'm just telling you." Pell did not let go. "You smell like the head."

"I've been on strike."

"I know. Everybody knows. Matron was furious." Pell pulled back a little and looked at her, and her small straight mouth was doing something it did not usually do; it was shaking. "You look terrible."

"So do you."

"I've been *crying,*" said Pell, with disgust, and put her face back into Maren's shoulder.

They stood like that for a long time. Around them, all through the rows, it was the same. Lin Okoro was on her knees on the dock floor with a boy of ten in her lap, rocking him like a baby, and Ade standing over them with his great arms round them both. An old man Maren did not know was holding a girl's face between his two hands and just looking at it. Somebody was laughing in a high strange way that did not stop.

And all round the edge of the dock, up on the gantries and along the walls and in the shadows of the berths, in their black armour, with their rifles held across their chests, the marines stood and watched.

* * *

"I know about Brannock," said Pell.

It was half an hour later. They were sitting on the dock floor, the two of them, against the base of a gantry leg, a little apart from the others, with Pell's back against Maren's chest and Maren's arms round her. Pell had her hands on Maren's wrists, holding them there.

"How?"

"Matron leaves her slate on the desk in the study room. I can read upside down." Pell's voice was very quiet and very level. "I saw the manifest. Four weeks ago. Before the Scholarship was even announced. I didn't know what it meant, at first, it just said *Schedule C* and *consigned* and a lot of numbers. So I looked up the words. And then I looked up Brannock in the library." She was quiet. "There were pictures."

Maren held her tighter.

"I didn't tell the others," said Pell. "I didn't know if I should. The little ones are so happy. They think they're going to have a sky." Her fingers tightened on Maren's wrists. "I worked out my number. Eighty-eight thousand four hundred. I'm the third highest. Because I can count." She laughed, a small wet laugh. "You always said I wasn't worth anything."

"You're not."

"You said I was yours."

"You are."

"That's not how it works," said Pell, but softly, and leaned her head back against Maren's shoulder.

Maren put her mouth against her daughter's hair.

"Listen to me," she said. "Listen. When the hour's up, they'll call for embarkation. And when they do, somebody's going to start singing. The breathers' hymn. You know it?"

"Everybody knows it."

"When you hear it, you hold my hand. Don't let go. Whatever happens. We're going to walk into the shuttle at Berth Four, the one we came up in, and they're going to seal the lock, and we're going home."

Pell was very still.

"Is that the plan?" she said at last.

"That's the plan."

"Whose plan is it?"

"Sister Hatch's. The union's."

"How many marines are there?"

Maren looked up, at the gantries. At the black shapes along the walls.

"I don't know," she said.

"I counted," said Pell. "When they brought us in. There's a hundred and six I can see. There's more in the berths, behind the shuttle locks. I saw them go in this morning, before you came." She turned her head, and looked up at Maren, and her eyes were her father's eyes, whoever he had been, and very clear. "They went into the shuttles. Into the berths. Before you came up."

Maren looked across the dock at Berth Four. At the shuttle she had come up in, nosed into its lock, its hatch closed. At Ruth Calloway's crew, who should have been standing at that hatch, waiting.

There was no one standing at the hatch.

* * *

She would go over it afterward, again and again, in the dark of the caretakers' room and the long dark of the run to the Throat, and she would never be able to make it come out differently. If she had seen it sooner. If she had moved. If she had found Hatch in the crowd and said *the crews are gone, the shuttles are taken, it's a trap* — and what would Hatch have done? There was no other way off the dock. There was no other plan. Maybe they would have stood there and let the hour run out and let the clerks call the names and let the children walk one by one down the boarding tube into the *Grace*, and Maren would have stood on the yellow line and watched Pell go. Maybe that would have been better. She never knew.

What happened was this.

The light over the boarding tube turned from green to amber, and the warm voice of Deputy Factor Vole said from the speakers, "*Families, thank you. The hour is ended. Wards will now form their rows for embarkation,*" and all through the dock a sound went up, a great helpless moan, four hundred parents at once. And across the floor, from somewhere near the centre, Maren heard an old cracked voice begin to sing.

*Down in the red where the weight comes on—*

It was Hatch. Maren saw her: a small grey figure in her cut-down habit, standing in the middle of the rows with her hands at her sides and her chin up, singing at the top of her voice, flat and loud and terrible.

*—down where the light's a bruise—*

And the parents moved. Every one, all at once, the way a shoal turns: they took their children by the hand and turned from the boarding tube and began to walk, not run, walk, toward the shuttle berths. Maren was on her feet with Pell's hand in hers before she knew she had stood. They walked. All round them the hymn was going up, ragged, four hundred voices, the breathers' hymn in a dozen keys, *bring up the breath for the ones who are owed*, and the children were singing too, the ones who knew it, and they were walking toward the berths, and the marines on the gantries had not moved, and for nine or ten steps — Maren would remember every one — it seemed possible.

Then the shuttle hatches opened, all six of them at once, and the marines came out.

* * *

There were perhaps sixty of them in the shuttles. Pell had been right. They had been waiting there since before the parents came up — sitting in the jump seats where the parents would sit, in the dark, with their rifles across their knees. They came out of the hatches at a run, in a black flood, and spread across the front of the berths in a line, and stopped, and raised their rifles.

The hymn faltered. The walking slowed. The four hundred parents and their children came to a ragged halt ten metres from the black line, and stood there, swaying, and the hymn went down to a few voices, and then to one. Hatch's. Still singing, alone, flat and cracked, *and bring yourself up if you choose.*

"*Families,*" said Vole's warm voice from the speakers. "*Please return the wards to their rows. No one will be harmed. Please return the wards to their rows.*"

Nobody moved.

"*Please.*"

And it was Farrant who broke it. Of course it was Farrant. He was at the front, near Berth Two, with a girl of about eight by the hand — his daughter, Maren supposed, or someone's — and he looked at the black line of marines ten metres in front of him, and he looked back at the *Grace* lying at the dock's port like a grey cliff, and he said, quite loudly, in a voice that carried across the whole dock, "*No,*" and pulled the girl behind him and ran at the line.

They shot him. Maren heard it — a flat hard crack that came back off the dock's high ceiling three times — and saw him stop as though he had walked into a wall, and sit down, and then lie down. The girl he had been holding stood beside him for a moment and then began to scream.

And then everyone was running.

* * *

She did not know afterward how much of it she saw and how much she put together later from the things other people told her and from the feed. She knew there was more shooting. She knew there were marines among the parents, wading in, pulling children away by the arms and the collars, and parents fighting them, and going down. She saw Ade Okoro lift a marine off his feet bodily and throw him into a gantry leg, and then disappear under three more. She saw Lin on the floor with her boy under her, covering him with her body. She did not see Hatch.

She had Pell's hand. That was all. She had Pell's hand and she was running, away from the berths and the black line, back across the dock toward the gantries at its far side, through the crowd, through the screaming, with no idea where she was going except *away*.

"*Not that way!*" Pell was pulling at her. "Mum! *Not that way*, there's a lock, they'll—*this* way!"

Maren let herself be pulled. Pell ran like a hare, low and fast, jinking through the crowd, toward the dock's inner wall where it met the station spindle, and there — behind a stack of cargo cradles, in a corner Maren would never have seen — was a hatch. A small square service hatch at knee height, with a grille over it.

"Laundry," gasped Pell. "Goes to the Nursery. We used to hide in it." She was on her knees, pulling at the grille. It came away in her hands. "Get in. Get in, Mum, you'll fit, I've seen Matron's fat cook—"

Maren got in. It was a chute, square, metal, slanting down into the dark. Pell came in after her feet first and pulled the grille closed behind them, and for a moment they lay there together in the chute in the dark, breathing, with the noise of the dock coming through the grille behind them like the roar of the head.

"Where does it go?"

"Down. To the Nursery laundry. Then there's a service way to the ward locks." Pell's voice was shaking. "The ward locks are where the shuttles come in on visitation days. There's always a shuttle on the ward lock. For the Nursery. For emergencies."

"Can you fly a shuttle?"

"No. Can you?"

"I can fly anything," said Maren, and let go of the edge of the chute.

* * *

They slid. It was a long way. They came out at the bottom in a tangle on a pile of grey smocks in a hot steamy room that smelled of soap, and Pell was up before Maren and pulling her by the hand again, through a door, down a white corridor that Maren half recognised — the visitation corridor, the children's side, the side of the glass she had never been on — and round a corner, and down a ramp, and they were nearly there. She could see it ahead, the lock to the ward berth, the little Nursery shuttle sitting beyond its window like a fat white grub.

And the marine came out of the side passage into the corridor ahead of them, and stopped, and raised his rifle.

He was young. That was what Maren saw. Under the black helmet he was perhaps twenty, with a soft station-born face and frightened eyes, and he had been running too; he was breathing hard. He must have been sent down from the dock to cover the ward locks, in case anyone thought of it. He looked at them over the barrel of his rifle — a breather in a coverall with blood on her face from somewhere, and a girl in a blue uniform — and his mouth opened and nothing came out.

"Let us by," said Maren.

"I can't."

"Let us by. Please. She's my daughter."

"I *can't*." His voice cracked. "They'll — I'll go on a bond, they'll — get down. Get down on the floor. Both of you. Please."

Maren let go of Pell's hand.

She did not remember deciding. She remembered the yellow lever on the *Gannet*'s console, under its hinged guard, in the downdraft, and her hand already on it. She went at the boy across four metres of white corridor, low, the way you go into a seam, and she heard the rifle go off over her head and felt the heat of it on her scalp, and then she was inside his reach and her shoulder was in his chest and they went down together, hard, on the white floor, and the rifle went skittering away. He was bigger than her. He was trained. She was a breather who had spent nineteen years hauling bottles. She got her hands on his helmet and slammed it into the floor, once, twice, and he got a knee into her stomach and she lost all her air and rolled away, and he was on his feet, and so was she, and she heard Pell scream.

There were two more marines at the top of the ramp. And between them, in her grey Nursery coat, with her hand already out, Matron Odile Brask.

"Pell," said Matron Brask. "Come here, dear."

Pell stood in the middle of the corridor, between Maren and the ramp, with her hands in fists at her sides.

"No," she said.

"Pell. Don't be silly. Come here."

"*No.*" Pell turned round. She looked at Maren. Her face was white and set and perfectly calm, and she was twelve, and she looked exactly like Maren's mother. "Mum—"

One of the marines behind Brask came down the ramp and took her by the arm.

Maren went for him. She got two steps. The young marine she had fought came off the wall behind her and hit her across the back of the head with something — his helmet, his fist, she never knew — and the white corridor tilted and went grey, and she was on her knees, and she was reaching, and she had Pell's hand. She had it. She had her daughter's hand in hers on the white floor of the Nursery corridor and she held on to it with everything she had, the way she held the yoke of the *Gannet* in the shear, the way she had held the dump lever, and the marine pulled, and Pell was pulled, and Maren held on.

"Let go," said someone above her. Brask. "For heaven's sake, let go, you'll break her arm."

She held on.

"*Mum,*" said Pell. "Mum, let go. Let go. It's all right." Her voice was very steady. "It's all right. I'll count. I'll count it all. Let go."

Somebody kicked her in the side. Somebody else knelt on her wrist. And her fingers opened, because fingers will, and Pell's hand slid out of hers like a rope going down into the red.

She lay on the white floor. She saw, sideways, from very low, the marine carry Pell up the ramp — not dragging her; Pell was walking, with his hand on her arm, very straight — and at the top of the ramp, beside Brask, Pell turned and looked back. Maren saw her lift her free hand. She saw her knock, three times, on the white wall of the corridor beside her: *still here.*

Then they were gone.

* * *

She did not know how long she lay there. She knew that at some point the young marine was gone, and the other one, and that she was alone in the corridor, and that she could not seem to make her legs work. She knew that there was a sound, very far away, that might have been more shooting or might have been the *Grace*'s boarding tube cycling. She knew that she was crying, and that it did not feel like crying; it felt like breathing, like the giant breathing out through her on a full draw, a long rushing exhale that went on and on.

And then there were hands on her. Not a marine's. Thin, soft, frightened hands, under her arms, pulling. And a voice, close to her ear, that she knew.

"Get up. Maren. Maren, get up, they're coming back, they're sweeping the whole Nursery level for anyone from the shuttles, you have to *get up*—"

Lucan Vey. His narrow face above her, whiter than the corridor.

"She's gone," Maren said. "They took her. She's on the *Grace*."

"I know. I know. Get up." He was hauling at her. He was not strong. "Please. I can't carry you. Please."

She got up. She did not know how. He got her arm across his shoulders, and they went — not toward the ward lock but away from it, through a door Maren had not seen, into another corridor, grey, plain, a staff corridor, and down it, and through another door, and down a stair, and through a door with a lock that Lucan opened with his palm, into a small dark room that smelled of dust and machine oil.

He let her down on the floor. He locked the door. And then he sat down on the floor too, with his back against it, and put his face in his hands, and shook.

* * *

The room was a storeroom on the Residence's lowest level, in the block that had been assigned, Lucan told her later, to the caretaker detail: twelve small rooms for twelve officers who would stay behind. Nobody came down here. Nobody had any reason to. The caretakers had not moved in yet; they would not be needed until the *Grace* had gone.

There was a feed screen on the wall. At the eighth bell, Lucan turned it on.

It was the Factor. He was standing in his office in the Residence with the long window behind him, and through it, in Hesper's shadow, Maren could see the grey cliff of the *Grace*'s flank. He was in his grey coat. He looked tired. He spoke to the screen the way he had spoken from the separator platform: gravely, and gently, as though to people he loved.

"*Families of Vigil. I am sorry to speak to you tonight in sorrow.*

"*This morning, at the farewell for the Scholarship wards, a small group of agitators attempted to disrupt the embarkation. They endangered the lives of the very children they claimed to protect. We were warned of their intention in advance, and were able to prevent the worst. But I must tell you that eleven people were killed, among them two marines. I grieve for every one.*

"*The wards are safely embarked. The convoy will depart as scheduled, at first bell tomorrow.*

"*The ringleaders are in custody. They are Agathe Hatch, Ruth Calloway, Adebayo Okoro, Simon Pym, and four others whose names will be posted. The Combine's law is clear on the penalty for inciting violence against its officers. I take no pleasure in it. Agathe Hatch will be liquidated at first bell tomorrow, before the convoy departs, and the others in the days that follow. The liquidation will be shown on the Sump's feed. I wish it were otherwise.*"

He looked into the screen for a moment, silent. Behind him the *Grace* lay in the dark like a promise.

"*Without the ledger,*" said Imre Castellane softly, "*there is only the knife.*"

The screen went dark.

Maren sat on the floor of the caretakers' storeroom with her back against a crate. She did not say anything. Across from her, Lucan was looking at the dead screen.

"We were warned," he said. "He said it. *We were warned of their intention in advance.*"

"I heard."

"They had the shuttle crews before the hour started. Ruth's people. Ade's people. They took them at the berths before you even came up — I saw them bring them in, at the start of shift, I didn't know what it meant. They knew which shuttles. They knew the signal. They had marines in the hulls." He looked at her. "Somebody told them. Somebody told them everything."

Maren did not answer.

She was counting. She could not help it; she had always counted, and Pell had always counted, it was the one thing they had in common that no one had ever given them. She was counting the lamps on the catwalks of the Drowned Decks, going out one by one along the water as the union went home. Fourteen people on an iron platform in the middle of a black lake, where nobody listened.

Hatch. Ruth. Ade. Pym. Farrant, who was dead. Moss. The Ellerys, two. Corley. Brand, Teo's mother. The two from the scrubber crews whose names she had never learned. Herself.

Thirteen.

She counted again. She came to the same place. She stopped there, and did not count the last one, and sat in the dark with her daughter's warmth still on her hands, and the fourteenth lamp going out at the edge of the platform before all the others, and did not let herself think the name.


# Chapter 14: The Audit of Piet Marr

*The Ledger · 2794*

There were five of them in Corin's back room on the second night, and they read the Compact by the light of one lamp, passing the page from hand to hand as though it were hot.

Ysolde had not known there would be so many. She had thought, in so far as she had thought about it at all in the long cold hours in the tank, that the Nullists of the deep spokes were Corin and perhaps one or two others, old divers with grudges and knives. But on the second night he had brought them down the dead-end corridor one at a time, after the last shift-change, and sat them on the bench and the crates among the hollow tanks: a grey-haired refinery hand named Hollis with burns up both forearms; a diver's widow called Bett, small and brown and fierce, who had lost a husband and two sons to the red; a boy of perhaps seventeen named Aurek, who said nothing at all and watched Ysolde as though she might explode; and an old, old man in a scrubber's coat whom the others called Grandad, who was blind in one eye and had, Corin said, been scratching circles on the walls of Spoke Nineteen for sixty years and never once been caught.

She read it to them first. Then she let them hold it.

Bett read it twice with her lips moving and then put it down on her knee and sat looking at it. "*Taught as closed against them,*" she said. "*Them.*"

"Yes."

"They wrote that. The Stewards. They sat up there in the Nave and wrote that about us." She looked up at Ysolde. "Why would they write it down? If it was a lie. Why write it down where somebody could find it?"

"Because they were keeping an account," said Ysolde. "They kept everything. Even that."

Hollis had the Founding page. He was holding it very close to the lamp, squinting, his scarred forearms trembling. "A hundred and eighteen million," he said. "That's what it says? That's the real one?"

"That's what the Combine left."

"And the primers say four hundred and six."

"Yes."

He put the page down carefully, as if it might break. "My mother," he said, "used to tell us — when we were small, when she put us to bed — she used to say the Count was what it cost to bring us here. Our ships and our air and our food on the way. Four hundred and six million. She said it like a prayer. She said: you're worth that, my loves. You cost all that, and they brought you anyway." He wiped his face with the back of a burned hand. "She died at fifty on the separators."

Nobody said anything.

"We should put it up," said Aurek suddenly. It was the first thing he had said. His voice was high and cracked. "On the boards. In every spoke. We should copy it out and put it up everywhere so they can't grind it off fast enough—"

"No," said Corin.

"Why not? It's *true*. It's in their own—"

"Because they'll grind it off and say it's a forgery," said Corin, "and then they'll find out who put it up and send them to the Breath, and on Sorrow Day the Prelate will read it out in the Hall and say that the Nullists of the Lungs are so wicked they forge the words of the Stewards themselves, and everybody in the Lungs will say *it is just*." He looked at the boy, not unkindly. "I've been doing this twenty years, Aurek. I know what they do with the truth. They count it."

The old man they called Grandad had said nothing at all. He had not asked to hold the pages. He was sitting on a crate near the lamp with his blind eye toward it and his good eye on Ysolde, and when the room had gone quiet he said, in a soft, dry, papery voice:

"Gallows."

They all looked at him.

"It says Gallows," said Grandad. "Doesn't it? On the paper. *No soul shall go to Gallows.*"

"Yes," said Ysolde. "Do you know the word?"

"I know a word like it." He turned his good eye up toward the ceiling, toward the Nave, a hundred kilometres over their heads. "When I was a boy I ran breath to the outer relays. Before they shut them down. There were divers on the long runs who used to say there was something out by the Door, on the far side of the Shear, where nobody went. A dead thing. Hanging there. You could see it on a clear day from the far relays if you had a good glass." He smiled faintly. "The old ones called it the Hanged Men. Because it had four legs, see. Hanging down."

"The Hanged Men," said Ysolde.

"I never saw it myself. I never had a good glass." He folded his old hands. "But I'll tell you this, Sister. I knew a diver once, a woman from Spoke Twenty-one, Nell Asher her name was — no relation — who went out past the Shear on a dare in a stolen skimmer, sixty years ago, to look at it close. And she came back. And she never would say what she'd seen. Only that it was dark, and it was a long way, and there was nothing alive there. And the Bailiffs came for her a month later and she went to the Breath for theft of a skimmer." He looked at Ysolde. "They don't send you to the Breath for a skimmer, Sister. They take your share."

* * *

They came for Piet on the third morning.

She heard it on the speaker-wire. She was in the back room alone, writing — she had begun, the night before, to write the pages out again from memory on reckoning sheets, every word of the Compact and every column of the Founding, in case anything happened to the originals; she could do it, she found, as easily as she could have written out the catechism; she had read them once under a lamp in the Reliquary with Strand coming down the ladder, and they had gone into her like a dye — when the wire crackled at the second bell and the dry voice of the Chancery of Law came down it.

"*Hear the Count. By order of the Prelate-Auditor: Piet Marr, diver, of Spoke Twelve, crew of the* Dutiful*, is taken into the Count's keeping for audit. It is found that forty-one hours of false tithe were entered to his account in the Ninth Ledger, in the hand of the defaulter Ysolde Marr, his sister. He will be audited in the Ledger Hall at the fourth bell tomorrow before the Prelate-Auditor and the nine Chanceries.*"

A pause.

"*If the defaulter Ysolde Marr gives herself into the Count's keeping before the audit, with all that she has taken from the Nave, the audit of Piet Marr will be set aside. It is just.*"

And down every corridor of the Lungs, faint and far, the answer.

She sat at Corin's bench with the pen in her hand and the half-written page in front of her, and did not move until the ink on the nib had dried.

* * *

"No," said Corin.

"I wasn't asking."

"That's twice you've said that to me." He was standing in the doorway of the back room with his arms folded. Behind him the shop was dark and shut. Bett was there, and Hollis, sitting on the bench, not looking at either of them. "And twice I've let you. Not this time."

"He's my brother."

"He's a diver. He's twenty-two. He knew what this was when he sent you that note." Corin's voice was very hard. "*Don't do anything stupid.* That's what he wrote. You told me. He's not a fool, Ysolde. He knew what you'd do for him and he asked you not to."

"They'll send him to the Breath."

"They might. They might not. The Prelate doesn't want your brother. He wants those pages back, and he wants you, and he's using what he's got." He came into the room. "If you go up there, he'll have both. And you'll go to the Breath, and Piet'll go back to the *Dutiful* with forty-one hours struck out and a default on his line that'll follow him the rest of his life, and nobody in the Lungs will ever read a word of what's on those pages. That's the sum. Do it yourself. You're the counter."

She did it. She did it standing there with her hands at her sides and her face, she knew, as white as Hollis's knuckles on the bench. It came out the way he said.

"I'm not going to give myself up," she said.

Corin's shoulders went down a fraction.

"I'm going to give him the pages," she said.

* * *

The audit was at the fourth bell the next day, and the speaker-wire carried it into every corridor of the Lungs. She listened to it in the back room among the tanks, sitting on the bench with her arms around her knees, with Bett on one side of her and Grandad on the other, and Corin standing at the curtain with his back to them, not moving.

It was very short. She had attended audits as a keeper, standing in the ranks of the Third Chancery in her grey, and she knew the form: the charge, the Ledger, the response, the Prelate. The defaulter stood at the rail before the Count Board, under the great plates, and the keeper of the Ledger read the entry aloud, and the defaulter was asked to answer it, and then the Prelate spoke.

The keeper who read the entry was Brother Tomas, who had taken her Seventh. She heard his voice shake.

"*Ninth Ledger, volume two hundred and six, Spoke Eighteen. The* Dutiful*, crew of five. Forty-one hours entered to the crew's account under the date of the last dive, in the hand of Ysolde Marr, keeper. The default mark struck and initialled by the same hand. The hours are false. The dive log of the* Dutiful *shows no such hours.*"

"*Piet Marr,*" said the voice of the Prelate-Auditor. It was very gentle. "*Did you ask your sister to enter these hours?*"

And then her brother's voice, high and clear and ridiculous in the great ribbed space of the Ledger Hall, with the faint ring of the vault behind it.

"*No, Prelate. I asked her not to do anything stupid.*"

There was a sound in the Hall that might have been laughter, quickly stifled. In the back room, Bett put her hand over her mouth.

"*I believe you,*" said Quist. "*I'm sure you did.*" A pause. "*Piet Marr, the Count has been wronged by forty-one hours entered falsely to your account. The Count must be made whole. By the law of the Count, a false entry is answered by the Breath, unless it be set right.*" Another pause, longer. Ysolde could hear the old man breathing. "*I will defer judgment until the fourth bell tomorrow, to allow it to be set right. It is just.*"

*It is just,* said the Hall. *It is just,* said the Lungs, down every corridor.

And then, after the response, so softly that she almost missed it — it was not part of the form; he must have leaned in to the speaking-horn and said it to the wire alone — the Prelate-Auditor said:

"*Ysolde. The drop dock, the old chapel. Tonight, at the middle bell. I'll come alone.*"

* * *

The old chapel at the drop dock was a little room off the shuttle gallery where, in the first century, divers going up to the Nave on audit had been allowed to pray. Nobody had used it in a hundred years. It had four benches and a bare wall where something had once hung, and a single window onto the dock floor, and it smelled of ice.

He was there when she came. He was sitting on the front bench in his white, with his black cane across his knees and his hands folded on it, and he was alone. She had made Corin's people watch the dock for two hours before she came, from every gallery and every grating, and they had seen him come down on the eighth-bell shuttle with two Bailiffs and send the Bailiffs back up on the ninth.

He looked up when she came in, and his broad lined face broke into a smile of such open, tired, uncomplicated pleasure that for a moment she could not move.

"Sister Marr," he said. "You've lost weight. Sit down."

She did not sit. She stood at the end of the bench with her hands in the sleeves of the diver's coat Bett had given her, a long grey thing that smelled of someone else's life, and looked at him.

"Have you brought them?" he said.

"Yes."

"May I see?"

She took them out — the two pages, torn from the First Book, folded small — and held them up where he could see them in the light from the dock window. She did not hand them to him.

He looked at them for a long moment.

"You read them," he said.

"Yes."

"Both."

"Yes."

"And you understood them, I suppose. You're a counter." He sighed. "I read them for the first time eleven years ago. On my first Founding Day as Prelate. Hanne had told me what to expect, before he died, but it's another thing to read it." He turned his cane a little in his hands. "Do you know what I think, Ysolde, every Founding Day, when I go down and kneel by her chair and kiss her tag and then get up and read those words?"

She said nothing.

"I think: *they were right.*" He looked up at her. "Twelve people. Thirty thousand starving in the ice. No hope of relief — they say so, don't they? *Having no hope of relief.* They knew no one was coming. They knew the Door was shut for good. And they had to make thirty thousand frightened, dying, furious people go on diving into the red and bringing up the breath, every day, for the rest of their lives, for no reward at all, or every one of them would die. How would you have done it?" He waited. "Truthfully, Sister. If you were Steward of Law, in the first year. How would you have done it?"

"Not with a lie."

"With what, then?"

She opened her mouth, and found that she had nothing to put in it.

"Quite," said Quist gently. "Nor did they. So they used what they had. They had the account. They had the forms of the Combine and thirty thousand people who'd been raised in them. They knew how to make people work for a number. So they gave them a number." He spread his old hands. "And it worked. It worked for three hundred and forty years. A hundred and ninety thousand people are alive tonight because twelve frightened people in the first year wrote a lie in a book. I think that's the most remarkable thing anyone has ever done in this system. I think about it every day."

"And the Interest?" she said. "The pages cut out of the Ninth? The ink? Was that remarkable?"

He was quiet for a moment.

"That was Prelate Orrin," he said. "In the two hundred and seventy-ninth year. He did the sum the way you did it, the way Anselm did. He saw the Count was going to reach zero. And he did another sum, after it — the one I showed you over dinner. And he took up his pen." Quist looked down at his hands. "I didn't do it. I want you to know that. But I would have. I've done the sum too. Every Prelate since Orrin has done it."

"Did Anselm do it?"

He looked up.

"Did he do the sum?" she said. "Your sum? Before he went to the Breath?"

The Prelate-Auditor looked at her for a long time in the cold of the old chapel, with the dock lights shining through the window on his white robe.

"He did," said Quist quietly. "He came to me with his sheet, and I showed him mine, and he did it. At my table. With my beans going cold." He shook his head slowly. "And then he said to me that the sum was right and that he didn't care. That a hundred and ninety thousand people had the right to know what they owed, even if it killed them. That the First Auditor wrote *not a mark more* and meant it." He smiled, very faintly. "He was a counter."

"And you sent him to the Breath."

"The rail at the Number Two gallery is low," said Aurel Quist. "I've had it raised." He held out his hand. "The pages, please, Ysolde. And your brother goes home tonight, and you go wherever you like in the Lungs, and I'll strike your default at the next Reckoning and say there was an error in the Chancery of Law. I give you my word. I've never broken it."

She looked at his hand. It was old and spotted, and on its third finger the black seal ring sat heavy and dull in the dock light. She had kissed that ring at nine, kneeling in the novice line, without once looking at it.

"What's Gallows?" she said.

He went very still.

"It's in the Compact," she said. "*No soul shall go to Gallows. What was done at Gallows is accounted and closed.* What was done there? What is it?"

"I don't know."

"You're the Prelate."

"I'm the Prelate, and I don't know." He said it plainly, and she believed him — she could not help it; there was something in his face she had not seen before, a kind of blankness, a door shut. "No Prelate does. It's the one thing in the Book that isn't explained. Hanne didn't know. Hanne's Prelate didn't know. The Stewards closed it, and they never wrote down what was in it, and every Prelate since has kept it closed because they did." He was looking at her very hard now. "Don't, Ysolde."

"Don't what?"

"Whatever you're thinking. Don't." His voice had changed. "I've seen that look on a counter's face before. You can't sum it, so you want to go and look. Don't. It's closed for a reason. Twelve people who had the courage to lie to thirty thousand closed it, and wrote that it was closed, and never once in three hundred and forty years has anybody gone to see why. Leave it shut."

She held the pages out.

He took them. His hand closed over them, and for a moment he held them, and her fingers on the other edge, and they were both holding the same two sheets of paper in the dock light, the old man and the young woman, like two people on either side of a glass.

She let go.

"Thank you," said the Prelate-Auditor. He put the pages inside his white robe, against his chest, the way she had carried them. "Your brother will be on the first-bell shuttle down." He got up, slowly, leaning on his cane, and looked down at her. "Go home, Ysolde. Wherever that is now. Be a diver. Be anything." He smiled a little. "Be happy, if you can manage it. Counters seldom do."

He went out. She heard his cane tap away across the dock floor, slow, and the hiss of the shuttle lock, and then nothing.

She stood in the old chapel for a long time, with her empty hands in the sleeves of a dead man's coat.

Then she went back down the ladders to the bottle shop, and sat down at Corin's bench, and took a fresh reckoning sheet and her pen, and began to write the Compact out again from the beginning, every word, in the brown ink's careful hand that she could see as clearly behind her eyes as if it were lying on the bench in front of her.

*We, the Stewards of account HV-VIGIL-0001—*

* * *

Piet came down on the first-bell shuttle, as the Prelate had said. He came straight to the bottle shop. She did not know how he knew where she was; he simply walked down the dead-end corridor on Spoke Twenty and in through the front door of Corin's shop with his hair standing up and a bruise on his cheekbone and his tag on its cord at his throat, and stood looking at her across the counter where she had come out at the sound of the bell.

"You did something stupid," he said.

"Yes."

"I *told* you—"

"I know what you told me."

He came round the counter. He was taller than she was; he had been since he was fourteen. He put his arms round her and lifted her off her feet, the way he had used to when he was a boy, to annoy her, and held her there, and did not say anything, and she felt his chest heave against hers with the small dry catch at the end of every breath that he did not notice and she noticed every time.

"They say you're a Nullist," he said into her hair.

"I'm an auditor."

"That's what I told them." He put her down. He looked at her. "What did you give him?"

"Two pages."

"Were they worth it?"

"I'm going to find out," she said.

* * *

Corin told her about the boat that afternoon, in the back room, with the others gone and the shop shut and the lamp turned down.

"*Small Mercy*," he said. "She's moored in the old ice docks under the Drowned Decks. Has been for twelve years. I run breath in her, to the far relays on the Shear's edge, the ones the Nave closed and a few families never left. Bailiffs know she exists. They don't know where she lives." He was looking at the floor. "She's got the legs. She's carried four tons of full-draw bottles to the Shear and back. She could make the Door."

"How long?"

"Nine days. Maybe ten. The Shear's bad this season." He looked up. "And you'd need breath. A lot of it. For the run, and for whatever you think you're going to do when you get there." His mouth twisted. "Lucky you've come to a bottle shop."

"Corin—"

"Don't." He held up a hand. "You're going. I can see it on you. You've been going since you heard the old man say *Hanged Men*. I'd rather you went with somebody who knew the Shear than went on your own in a stolen skimmer like that fool Nell Asher and came back with nothing to say." He was quiet a moment. "And I want to see it. I've been scratching circles for twenty years, Ysolde. I'd like to see what they were so afraid of, just once, before they send me to the Breath."

She looked at him across the back room, among the hollow tanks.

"You're as stupid as I am," she said.

"I've been told," said Corin Asch.

* * *

Piet came to the old ice docks to see them off. She had told him not to. He came anyway, down through the dark under the Drowned Decks where the ice had been cut away three hundred years ago into great caves and then abandoned, following Corin's lamp along a ledge above black water, and stood on the frozen quay beside the *Small Mercy* — a squat, scarred, ugly old hull with oversized engines and a cargo belly fat with bottles — with his hands in his pockets, watching her climb the ramp.

"Wait," he said.

She turned.

He took his tag off. Their father's tag. He pulled the cord over his head and held it out to her: the half-disc of dull grey steel with the broken toothed edge, worn smooth, its old stampings long since gone.

"You don't go out without a tag," he said. "Everybody knows that."

"Piet. That's Dad's."

"I know whose it is." He put it in her hand and closed her fingers over it. "Bring it back. That's all. You don't have to bring anything else back. Just that."

She put it on. The cord was warm from his neck. The steel lay against her breastbone where the torn pages had lain, and the reckoning sheets with her copies of them lay folded inside her coat over it, and she stood on the ramp of the *Small Mercy* and looked at her brother on the quay in the lamplight and could not find a single thing to say.

So she said, "Count what's owed."

And Piet grinned his wide stupid grin, their mother's grin, and said, "And not a mark more," and stepped back from the ramp.

* * *

The *Small Mercy* came up out of the old ice docks through a shaft that Corin said had been a breath-pipe once, three hundred years ago, before the heads were moved, and broke surface on Hesper's night side in a spray of frost. Ysolde was in the second seat, strapped in, with her father's tag in her fist. Through the forward glass she watched the moon fall away below them, white and grey and scarred, and then the dark, and then — swinging up into view as Corin brought the old boat round — the Nave.

She had never seen it from the outside. Not properly. Only from the drop shuttle's tiny port, in glimpses. It hung over Hesper's night side like a great dark chandelier, spokes and rings and towers, lit here and there with the small warm lights of a hundred thousand windows, and at its heart, where the oldest parts were, the huge ribbed bulk of the Ledger Hall and whatever lay beneath it. Somewhere in there, the Count Board was standing in the dark, waiting for the first bell. Somewhere in there, an old man in white had two pages against his chest.

"There," said Corin quietly.

He was looking at the aft scope. She looked too.

Low on the Nave's lower dock, a small hard light had come on. A drive. And as she watched it lifted, and turned, and began, very steadily, to follow.

"Bailiff cutter," said Corin. "Strand's." He did not sound surprised. "Well. He knows where we're going, then." He reached forward and pushed the throttles to their stops, and the *Small Mercy* shuddered all through her old frame and leaned into the dark. "Hold on to your tag, Sister. It's a long way to the Hanged Men."


# Chapter 15: Assets

*The Door · 3190*

The breath-seller on the Number Three Kettle was a man called Teague, and he'd sold to the reeves under the counter for twenty years, whenever the Margrave's ration came up short and somebody's boat needed to be somewhere it wasn't supposed to be. He was a fat soft-spoken man with a lazy eye and a shop at the bottom of the Kettle's well, behind a door with no sign, and he owed me because I'd once looked the other way when looking the other way was worth a great deal to him. I'd never collected. I'd always thought I'd keep it for something.

When I got to his door the Kettle was being inventoried.

The Kettles are three old fuel tanks the size of small moons, lashed in a triangle off Tarrow's inner ring, hollowed out and lived in for longer than anyone remembers. Nine thousand people in them, last count. Traders, mostly, and the people who live off traders. I came in through the Number Three's lower lock in the *Mendicant*'s shadow with my drive dark and found two tenders already on the tank's skin, sitting like ticks, with that red line where their legs touched the hull; and inside, in the great hollow of the well, I could hear it before I saw it. Not screaming. Not much. That long, orderly, scraping quiet.

Teague's door was shut. I knocked the way the reeves used to knock. After a long time it opened a crack, and his lazy eye looked out at me, and then the other one.

"Rook," he said. "God help me. Come in."

He had breath. Of course he had; men like Teague always have. He had a room behind the shop stacked to the ceiling with full-draw bottles that he'd been holding back from the Margrave for years, waiting for the price to be right, and he stood among them in the lamplight wringing his soft hands while the scraping went on somewhere above us.

"They've done the upper wells already," he said. "They've done the market. They took the Hessel family out of the room next to mine an hour ago. Six of them. The little one was asleep. They didn't even wake her up, Rook. They just—" He made a closing gesture with his hands. "And Kask's people standing there with lists. Ticking them off."

"I need breath," I said. "Enough for the Door and back. Maybe more."

"The *Door*?"

"Can you do it?"

He looked at me with both his eyes. Then he laughed, a high, wet, frightened laugh.

"Take it," he said. "Take all of it. What am I going to do with it? What are they going to do with *me*?" He was pulling bottles off the stack already. "Take the lot, Rook. Get the Door. Do whatever it is you think you're going to do. I'll call it paid."

I took enough. Not all of it. I left him enough to run, if he had somewhere to run to; I don't think he did. The last I saw of Teague he was standing in his doorway at the bottom of the well with his soft hands hanging at his sides, looking up into the dark of the tank where the scraping was coming down, level by level, toward him.

* * *

I'd said two days. It took me two and a half to get back to the Hulk. The Kettles to Hesper is a long run, and I had to go the long way round, through the dark of the inner ring, because there were tenders everywhere now — coming and going between the habitats and Hesper in long black processions, each one with a grey cluster of pods hanging from its belly like eggs.

They were all going to the Hulk.

I saw it from a long way out. I'd brought the *Mendicant* round Hesper's limb low and dark, and as the station came up over the moon's edge I cut everything and let her drift and just looked, because I couldn't do anything else.

The Hulk was lit.

For two hundred years it had been dead and black. I'd known it dead and black my whole life. Now there were lights on it — not its own lights, not windows, but the hard white work-lamps of the Margrave's crews, strung along the spokes and the rings and the docking arms; and round it, thick as gnats round a lamp, the tenders, coming and going, settling on the outer docks and lifting off again. Where they settled they left the pods. I could see them, even from that far: grey clusters, piled along the outer rings and the docking arms, hundreds of them, thousands, clinging to the old station's skin the way frogspawn clings to a reed. And among them the little red sparks of the Margrave's cutters, moving from cluster to cluster.

The collection depot. Everybody they'd taken from Lanthorn and Sallow and the Kettles, brought here and stacked, waiting.

Somewhere inside it, in the core, in the old round rooms with the seats, Haddie and Wren and forty-odd children were hiding in the cold.

I'd been gone two and a half days.

* * *
