I went in through a picker's way. There are a hundred of them in the Hulk, if you've picked it long enough — holes and cracks and dead vents and half-collapsed corridors that the station's builders never meant for anybody to use — and I knew most of them, and the Margrave's crews, who'd never picked anything in their lives, knew none. I left the *Mendicant* tucked into the shadow of a dead tower on the station's dark side, where the work-lamps didn't reach, and went across in my suit on a line, and in through a crack in the tower's skin I'd found when I was thirty-four and never told anyone about.

From there it was three hours to the core. I'll not tell you about them. Dark and cold and slow, crawling through places a man my size shouldn't fit, listening. Twice I heard the drones — the scrape and click of their jointed legs on a deck somewhere above me — and lay still in the dark until they'd gone. Once, through a cracked port, I looked out onto one of the outer docking arms and saw the pods up close. They were the size of a person standing, grey and hard and faintly glistening, stacked three and four deep along the arm, held in place by some kind of webbing. I couldn't see in. I don't know if I'd have wanted to.

When I got to the hall, the lamps I'd strung were dark.

That was all right. I'd told them to go deeper. I went down the hall in the dark with my helmet lamp on low, past the benches, past the railings, to the slope of fallen plates at the far end, and found the gap behind them where the gate was, and the stair. The gate was open. I'd expected that.

What I hadn't expected was that it had been cut.

It had been an iron gate, old and heavy, with a lock as big as two fists. Somebody had taken a torch to it. The bars were sheared through in a neat curve and bent back, and the edges of the cuts were still bright.

I went down the stair.

* * *

The place with the seats was empty.

I'd found it once, years ago, picking — a long low room at the bottom of the stair, through a round ribbed corridor, all curved metal like the inside of a pipe, with rows of rotted padded seats bolted to the floor and dead consoles along one wall, and at the far end a little platform with a plain chair on it. I'd thought it was a chapel. It had that feel. It was the only part of the Hulk I'd ever been in where I didn't want to pick anything.

They'd been there. I could see it. Blankets on the floor between the seats. The spare scrubber I'd left them, still ticking. Water bulbs. A little pile of the white numbers from the fallen plates up in the hall, that the children must have carried down with them to play with — a nine, a two, a nought, a seven — stacked into a tower on one of the seats. The suit patches I'd left over the round corridor hatches, sealing the cold out, all of them torn away.

And the floor. The floor in the aisle between the seats was smeared with something dark, in a long trail, toward the corridor.

I stood there a long time with my lamp on the floor.

"Absalom."

It came from the wall. From the left-hand wall, where — I'd never looked at it properly, picking — there was a row of square plates set into the curved metal, a dozen of them, each the size of a cot, each with a line of the old hand cut into it. One of them, near the end of the row, was hanging open. Not much. A hand's width. And in the gap, in the dark, something moved.

"Haddie?"

"Get me out of here," said Haddie Coyle. "I've been sharing with a dead man for a day and a half and he's terrible company."

* * *

She'd pried the plate off with a cutting bar when she heard them coming, and climbed into the hole behind it, and pulled the plate to after her. There was a body in there. Not much of one. Bones, mostly, in the rags of a grey coat, lying on its back on a shelf of metal with its hands folded on its chest. She'd lain down beside it in the dark and held her breath. They hadn't looked.

She was very cold. She was very old. When I got her out she couldn't stand at first, and I sat her on one of the rotted seats and gave her water and put my own coat round her, and she let me, which frightened me more than anything else had.

"Tell me," I said.

"They came on the second day." Her voice was a thread. "Not long after you left. I heard them in the hall above. The drones, first. Then people. Kask's reeves. And then her." She looked at me over her spectacles; one lens was cracked. "She came herself, Absalom. The Margrave. In her red coat, with that braid. I saw her through the gap. She walked in through the round corridor with six reeves behind her and two of those black things, and she stood there in the middle of this room and looked round at the children, and she said: *Well. Here you all are.*"

"How did they find you?"

"I don't know. Heat. Breath. Those things can see in the dark, I think, like the herald could. Or someone told her. I don't know." She closed her eyes. "Wren fought. Kesse fought. Some of the bigger ones. They had cutting bars, they had the knife you left. It didn't matter. It didn't last a minute." She was quiet. "The black things took some of them. The little ones, mostly. The ones who couldn't run. Put them in the pods, right here, in front of everybody. The rest the reeves took. Marched them out up the corridor."

"And Wren?"

"Kask took her." Haddie opened her eyes. "Personally. Had her held. Walked up to her and looked at her a good while. She knew who she was. She said: *You're the one who took my seed.* And Wren spat at her." A ghost of a smile. "She'd have liked you to see that."

"Where?"

"I don't know. Up. To wherever Kask is. She said—" Haddie stopped. She put her hand inside the coat I'd wrapped round her, and drew something out, and held it in her lap.

It was the herald. Black and warm and purring in its slow breathing way, in its felt blanket.

"She asked for this," said Haddie. "She asked Wren where it was. And Wren said you'd taken it with you." She looked at me. "Wren knew I was in the wall, Absalom. She saw me go in. She looked straight at that plate and told the Margrave of Kell Deep that you'd taken it to the Kettles to sell."

I didn't say anything. I couldn't.

"So Kask doesn't know it's here," said Haddie. "She thinks you've got it. She'll be waiting for you to bring it back."

"Then I'll bring it back," I said.

* * *

Haddie argued. I'll give her that. She sat on the rotted seat with my coat round her and the herald in her lap and argued at me in a voice like paper tearing for a quarter of an hour, and every word she said was right. Kask would kill me. Kask would take the herald and kill me and keep Wren and the children anyway, because why wouldn't she? I had nothing to trade but a thing she already wanted and a life she didn't. I should take the breath I'd brought and the *Mendicant* and go to Gallows, now, alone, the way we'd planned, and shut the Door if I could, and maybe — maybe — if I did, the big thing behind it would never come through, and the pods would stay where they were, and one day somebody would cut them open. That was the only sum that came out. That was the only thing that might save anyone.

"I know," I said.

"Then *do* it."

"I can't."

"Why not?"

I looked at her. At the old woman in the rotted chapel, in my coat, with her cracked spectacles and her bent hands on the warm black seed of the thing that had come out of the Door. At the tower of white numbers on the seat beside her that some child had built. A nine, a two, a nought, a seven.

"Because I did it before," I said. "The sum. I did the sum and it came out, and I did what it said." I heard my own voice, very flat. "I'm not doing it again."

Haddie looked at me.

"What sum, Absalom?" she said quietly.

I didn't answer her. I took the herald out of her lap, blanket and all, and put it under my arm, and went up the stair.

* * *

I called Kask on the general band from the hall, standing among the fallen plates, so that every cutter and every reeve in range would hear.

"Teodora," I said. "It's Absalom. I've got your seed. I'm coming in."

There was a long pause. Then the voice, low and slow, the hand on the back of the neck.

"*Absalom. How nice. Come up to the main dock. Come slowly.*"

* * *

The main dock of the Hulk had been a great hall once, the biggest room in the station, a long space with a wall of armoured glass that looked out across the moon. I'd picked it a dozen times as a young man, for the scrap in its gantries. It was full now. Full of pods, stacked along the walls three deep under the glass, grey and glistening; full of the Margrave's reeves in their red coats, standing about with lists; full of drones, the black jointed things, standing motionless in a row along the back wall like furniture somebody had forgotten to put away.

And in the middle of it, in a chair someone had dragged out of the old dock control room, with a reeve on either side of her and Benno Strick standing at her shoulder, sat the Margrave of Kell Deep.

Teodora Kask was sixty that year. She was a big woman, heavy and slow, with a long grey braid over one shoulder and a white scar along her jaw from the night she took the Deep, and she wore the red coat with the white open hand on the breast as though she'd been born in it. She had small, clever, light-brown eyes. She had always had the stillest hands of anyone I ever knew. She sat in her dragged-out chair with those hands folded in her lap and watched me walk the length of the dock toward her between the stacks of pods, with the herald under my arm, and she smiled.

"Absalom Rook," she said. "Seven years. You look old."

"So do you."

"I am old. I've earned it." She held out one of her still hands. "Give it here."

I didn't move.

"Where is she?"

Kask looked at me for a moment longer. Then she lifted one finger, and two reeves came out from behind the nearest stack of pods with Wren between them.

They'd hurt her. Not badly — not the way I'd seen the Margrave's people hurt runners, in the Deep, in the old days. Her face was bruised down one side and her lip was split and her burned hands were tied in front of her with wire. She walked between the two reeves with her chin up, the gangway look, and when she saw me she stopped walking, and stood still, and looked at me with no expression at all.

"There," said Kask. "Now give it here."

"And the children."

"What children?"

"The ones you took from this station two days ago. Thirty-two from the Deep and eleven from Lanthorn and a baby. Where are they?"

"Inventoried," said Kask simply. "They're in the depot. With everybody else." She tilted her head. "You didn't think I could give them back, did you? Absalom. Really. They're on the receiver's list now. It knows their numbers. It's counted them. You can't take a thing off the receiver's list once it's on; I asked. It said that would constitute a further default." She shrugged her heavy shoulders. "I'm only the factor. I don't make the rules."

"Then what do you want me for?"

"I don't want you for anything. I want my seed." She held out her hand again. "It's the receiver's property. It came out of the Door, it belongs to the receiver, and when the receiver comes through I'd like very much to be the one who hands it back. It'll look well. It'll be remembered when terms are made." She smiled. "Mine, I mean. My terms. I've got very good ones."

"Passage," I said.

"Passage. For me and my household. And a commission. Factor's rate, the receiver calls it." She seemed genuinely pleased. "It's going to take everybody out through the Door, you know, once it's through. Everybody it's counted. To wherever it came from. It says there's places out there — whole worlds, Absalom, with skies. Most of them'll be put to work, I suppose. The receiver's a little vague on that. But I'll be put on a ship with a cabin and a share, and I'll see a sky before I die." She folded her hands. "Isn't that something? After all this?"

I looked at her. I looked at Wren, between the reeves, watching me with her bruised face and her blank eyes.

"Let her go," I said, "and I'll give it to you."

"No," said Kask. "You'll give it to me and I'll do what I like." She nodded to Benno Strick. "Take it off him, Benno."

He came. He was twenty-five now, I suppose, Benno, a tall lad with an earnest face, and he came across the dock floor toward me with his hand on his belt and his eyes not quite meeting mine, and stopped a pace off, and held out his hand.

"Please, Rook," he said quietly.

I gave it to him. What else was I going to do?

He took it back to Kask. She took it from him in its felt blanket and held it on her knees, and looked down at it, at the black warm seed humming there, and stroked it once with her still hand, the way you'd stroke a cat.

"Good," she said. "Good. Now." She looked up. "Since you're here, Absalom, and since it'll be a while before the receiver's through, I think there's something I'd like to do. For old times' sake."

She looked at Wren.

"Do you know why you were tithed, girl?"

Wren didn't answer.

"I asked you a question. You were Lanthorn's twenty-first year. Four-three-seven. Do you know why?"

"Because the lot fell on me," said Wren. Her voice was flat. "That's how the tithe works. One in ten. By lot."

"That's how it's supposed to work," said Kask. "One in ten, by lot, drawn by the reeve in front of the Lash Council, so everybody can see it's fair. And it was drawn that way. Twenty-first year, Lanthorn. Absalom drew it himself. And do you know whose name came up fourth on the roll?" She was watching my face. Not Wren's. Mine. "Jory Rook. Nine years old. The reeve's own boy."

I didn't move. I didn't look at Wren. I looked at Kask.

"He came to me," said the Margrave. "That night. Up to the house at Kell, with the roll in his hand. I'd never seen him like that. He'd been my reeve for six years — the best I ever had, the one who never asked — and he stood in my study with the roll in his hand and tears on his face and asked me to let him change one name." She smiled, very slightly. "I said yes. Of course I said yes. I liked him. And I knew that if I let him do it, he'd belong to me for the rest of his life. A man who's done that, Absalom, can't ever go back. Can he?"

Wren was looking at me. I could feel it. Like heat on the side of my face.

"He went back down to Lanthorn," said Kask, "and he took his boy's name off the roll, and he looked down it for somebody else to put on. Somebody nobody would miss. Somebody with no father and no friends on the Council. A scrubber's girl from the Number Six hulk, whose mother had nobody to ask questions for her." She turned her head at last, and looked at Wren. "He chose you."

The dock was very quiet. The reeves had stopped moving. Along the back wall the black drones stood in their row like furniture.

"Rook," said Wren.

I made myself look at her.

She was standing between the two reeves with her bound hands in front of her. Her face had changed. Not much. I'd been watching faces my whole life, and I've seen a lot of them change, and I'll tell you that the worst ones don't change much at all. Hers just went still, the way water goes still, all the way down. And her eyes on mine were the eyes of a nine-year-old girl on a gangway, writing me down — except that now, at last, she'd finished. She'd got the whole of it.

"Is it true?" she said.

There was a lie on the bench in front of me. There always is. I'd told myself in that skiff, the day I pulled her out, that I'd tell the truth in this, wherever it cost me. I'd not known then what it would cost.

"Yes," I said.

She didn't say anything for a moment. Then she spat. Not at Kask. At me. Across the two metres of dock floor between us, at my feet.

"Your Jory died anyway," said Teodora Kask softly. "Didn't he, Absalom? Two years later. Red fever. All for nothing." She stroked the herald on her knee. "Benno. Take him somewhere and teach him some manners, and then put him with the others. I'd like to give him to the receiver myself, when it comes. I think it'll appreciate a reeve."

* * *

Benno Strick taught me some manners in a dead storeroom off the main dock with two other reeves holding my arms. He didn't enjoy it. I could tell. He kept saying he was sorry. I'd trained him, after all; he did it properly. When they were done they left me on the floor in the dark and locked the door, and I lay there for a long time with my face against the cold deck and my mouth full of blood and listened to the station.

Through the walls, faint, the receiver was speaking its notice again. All bands, all speakers, the same calm words in the same pleasant voice. *Debtors will be inventoried in order. Debtors will be held in custody pending reconciliation.* And under it, through the deck, through my cheek, a vibration I couldn't place at first: a deep, slow, rhythmic shudder, as if something enormous a long way off were breathing.

I thought about Jory.

I've not said much about him in this account. I've tried not to. He was nine. He had his mother's hair and my hands, and he was frightened of the dark and pretended not to be, and he wanted to be a picker when he grew up, because pickers found treasure. When his name came up fourth on the roll in the cargo pod on Lanthorn, in the twenty-first year, in front of the whole Lash Council, I read it out in my reeve's voice the way I'd read out three hundred others, and nobody in the pod moved, and I rolled the paper up and put it in my coat and went home and sat on the edge of his bunk and watched him sleep for six hours. And then I went to Kell.

He died two years later. Red fever. In my arms, in our cell on Lanthorn, on a hot night when the scrubbers were failing. He was eleven. His mother had already gone by then. I buried him on the Lanthorn lash, the way we bury people, out of a lock into the ring shadow, and Garra Moss put his big hand on my shoulder and said nothing, and I went back to Kell the next week and told the Margrave I was done.

Seven years. Every night. I'd done the sum every night for seven years — Jory's life against a scrubber's girl's — and every night it had come out the same, and every night I'd told myself that it came out, that any father would have done it, that it was a sum, and sums are only numbers, and nobody blames a number.

And it had all been for nothing, because he died anyway.

I lay on the floor of the dead storeroom with the receiver's voice coming through the walls and that vast slow breathing coming up through the deck, and for the first time in seven years I didn't do the sum. I just lay there and let it be what it was.

Some time later, I don't know how long, the door opened.


# Chapter 16: What Dov Sold

*The Bond · 2431*

"He doesn't sleep," said Lucan. "That's the first thing."

It was the middle of the night on the twenty-second day, in the caretakers' storeroom, with the feed screen dark and a single hand-lamp on the floor between them. Lucan had a slate on his knees with a plan of the Residence on it, drawn by his own hand from memory, and he was talking very fast and very low, the way he had talked in the latrine at the tally house, as though the words had been piling up behind his teeth for days.

"Four hours a night, maybe. Never more. He reads. He sits at the window in his study and looks at the *Grace* and reads, or does sums, or just looks. His staff say he's done it for nineteen years. Some of the officers think it's a sign of genius." He laughed, a short dry sound. "I think he just can't stop counting."

"Where?"

"Top level. The Residence spire. The study's on the outer wall, the bedroom behind it, a dressing room behind that." He touched the slate. "Two marines on the corridor door, all night. Nobody gets past them. But the dressing room backs onto the service way."

"What service way?"

"The cleaning drones. There's a crawlway behind every suite in the Residence, for the drones that do the linen and the floors. It runs up the spire from the laundry. The hatches open on a staff palm." He held up his hand, pale and narrow in the lamplight. "The caretaker detail was given staff access to every service way in the Residence yesterday. Somebody has to clean it after they've gone." He looked at his own palm as though it belonged to someone else. "It never occurred to anyone to take it off me. I'm staying. Why would I want to go anywhere?"

Maren looked at the plan. At the little square he had drawn for the dressing room, and the line of the crawlway behind it, and the study beyond with its window.

"He wears it," she said. "The key. All the time."

"All the time."

"Then I'll take it off him."

Lucan looked at her for a long moment in the lamplight.

"And then?" he said.

"Then I need a boat."

"There's nothing on High that can get you to Gallows. Every launch on the station's tethered into the convoy's departure plan; they'd be flagged the second they undocked." He hesitated. "There's only one boat I know of that could make it. That you could trust to fly it."

She didn't answer.

"Maren—"

"I know," she said. "Call him."

* * *

He called from the caretakers' office down the corridor, on a line the Combine used for tally-house queries, and she stood in the doorway and listened. He did not say much. He said that there was a breather on High who needed collecting. He said service lock nine, on the Residence's lower ring, at the fourth bell. He said: *She says, bring the* Patience *fuelled for a long run.*

There was a pause on the line. Then Dov's voice, thin and far and cracked, said, "*All right,*" and nothing else, and the line clicked off.

Lucan put the handset down very carefully.

"He didn't ask why," he said.

"No."

"He didn't ask anything."

"No," said Maren. "He didn't."

* * *

The crawlway was a square metal tube half a metre across, lit every few metres by a tiny green guide-light for the drones, and it went up the Residence spire in a long slanting climb with rungs set into its floor. It smelled of hot dust and laundry soap. Maren went up it on her hands and knees, behind Lucan's narrow back and the soles of his staff shoes, for what felt like an hour. At every junction he stopped, and checked the slate, and put his palm to a plate, and a hatch slid open in front of them with a sigh, and they went on.

At the top he stopped, and turned, awkwardly, in the narrow space, and looked back at her with the green guide-light on his face.

"This is it," he whispered. "The dressing room. I'll wait here. If anyone comes I'll—" He stopped. "I don't know what I'll do."

"Wait," she said.

He put his palm to the plate.

* * *

The dressing room was dark, and full of grey coats. They hung in a row along one wall, a dozen of them, all cut exactly the same, and she pushed between them in the dark like a diver pushing through weed and came out at a door that stood a hand's width ajar. Beyond it, a bedroom, also dark, with a bed that had not been slept in. And beyond that, through an open archway, light.

She had the knife in her hand. It was the breather's knife from her suit belt, a short heavy blade for cutting scoop lines, and she had never in her life held it like this, point forward, low. She went through the bedroom on the balls of her feet, and stopped at the archway, and looked.

The study was long and quiet and lined with shelves. At its far end was a window as tall as the room, and through it, filling it, lit along her flank by the docking lights of High, lay the *Grace*. Pell was in there. Somewhere behind that grey cliff of hull, in a ward deck, asleep or not asleep.

And in a chair by the window, with a slate on his knee and a cup on the table beside him, sat Imre Castellane, in his shirtsleeves, looking out at her.

He did not turn round.

"Mrs. Tull," he said. "I thought it might be you."

She did not move.

"Come in," said the Factor of Vigil. "I won't call anyone. I'd rather talk to you than have you shot in my bedroom. It's been a long day, and I've been sitting here wondering whether you'd come, and I find I'm rather glad you have." He turned, then, in the chair, and looked at her: at the knife, at her face, at the blood dried in her hair. His long grave face did not change. "You're hurt."

"Give me the key."

"Ah." He did not touch it. It lay at his throat on its fine steel chain, against the open collar of his shirt, white ceramic and grooved steel. "Yes. I wondered if Mr. Vey had told you about that. He's in the crawlway, I suppose. He's a decent young man. I should have sent him to Corvid; I'd have liked to see what he'd have made of himself." He tilted his head. "Do you understand what it does?"

"Yes."

"Then you understand that it's no use to you. Let me explain, so that we're clear." He set the slate aside, gently, on the table by the cup. "When the *Grace* passes Gallows tomorrow, I was going to set the pins to invert behind her. You know that. Two hours after we cleared, the passage would close. Without the key, I can't do that. Very well. Then the pins will go on doing what they've been doing, which is failing — slowly, expensively, without fresh breath — and in eighteen months, perhaps two years, the passage will narrow and close on its own." He spread his clean hands. "So you see. All you will have done, by taking it from me, is give the Sump eighteen months of something to hope for. Eighteen months of thinking someone might build a ship. Eighteen months of looking at the Throat." He looked at her with what seemed like real pity. "That's not a kindness, Mrs. Tull. I promise you. I've seen colonies hope."

She crossed the study in four steps. He did not move. She put the point of the breather's knife under the fine steel chain, against the soft old skin of his throat, and he looked up at her past it, perfectly calm.

"Pell's in the ward deck," he said. "If you were wondering. Deck Nine, forward. She asked to see you once more, this evening. I said no. I'm sorry. It would only have made it worse." His voice was very gentle. "She'll be all right, you know. She'll count beautifully. With an aptitude like hers she'll be a gang-tally inside a year. She'll never go into the galleries at all."

Maren cut the chain.

It parted with a tiny sound, a click, like a tally wand on a bottle. The key slid down the front of his shirt and she caught it in her left hand, and it was heavier than she had thought it would be, and warm from his skin.

Castellane looked down at the place where it had been, and then up at her.

"There's a thing about debts, Mrs. Tull," he said, "that your people have never understood. You think they die with the debtor. They don't. They outlive everyone. They outlive the debtor and the creditor and the clerk who wrote them down. They're the most durable thing human beings have ever made." He smiled, very slightly. "You can take that key to the bottom of Tarrow if you like. It won't change what's owed."

"Count what's owed," said Maren.

He looked at her. For the first time, something moved in his face — puzzlement, perhaps, or something like it, a man hearing a word in a language he thought he knew and finding it means something else.

"And not a mark more," said the Factor of Vigil politely, and she hit him across the side of the head with the hilt of the breather's knife, as hard as she could, and he went sideways out of the chair onto the floor of his study and lay there.

She did not check whether he was breathing. She tied his wrists with the sash of a grey coat from his own dressing room and his ankles with another and pushed a third into his mouth, and then she stood over him for a moment in the light from the long window, with the *Grace* lying beyond it like a cliff, and the key in her fist.

Then she went back through the coats to the crawlway.

* * *

They were three junctions down when the alarm went.

It was not loud. The Residence was not built for loud. It was a soft insistent chime that came from everywhere at once, through the walls of the crawlway, and under it a voice, calm and female, saying something Maren could not make out. And the green guide-lights in the crawlway, every one of them, at the same moment, turned red.

"He's awake," said Lucan, ahead of her. His voice was perfectly steady. "Or someone's found him. Keep moving."

They kept moving. Down the long slant of the crawlway, hand over hand, rung over rung, with the red lights pulsing round them and the chime going on and on. At the next junction Lucan put his palm to the plate, and it slid open, and they went through. At the next, the same. At the next he put his palm to the plate and nothing happened.

He stared at it.

"They've locked the staff access," he said. "They've locked the—" He put his palm to it again. Nothing. "No. No, no. They can't have locked all of it, it's a whole-system change, it'd take—" Again. And on the third try the plate went green, and the hatch sighed open, and he let out a breath like a sob. "Partial. They're locking it section by section. From the top down." He looked back at her over his shoulder. "We have to be faster."

They were faster. She did not know afterward how long it took; it felt like the whole night and it felt like no time at all. Rungs and red light and the chime, and Lucan's narrow back ahead of her, and his palm on plate after plate, and twice a plate that took three tries, and once, far above them, a sound of boots in the crawlway — someone coming in after them from the top, slowly, carefully, the way you go down a ladder in the dark.

The last hatch let out onto a service corridor on the Residence's lower ring. Lucan went through first. She heard him say *clear*, and came after him into a grey corridor lit red, and saw at the end of it, twenty metres off, the round armoured door of service lock nine with its little window, and beyond the window, against the black, the scarred grey nose of the *Old Patience*.

"Go," said Lucan.

They ran.

They were ten metres from the lock when the marines came round the corner behind them.

* * *

There were two of them. She heard them before she saw them — the clatter of boots, a voice shouting — and Lucan, beside her, did a thing she would not understand for a long time afterward. He did not run faster. He stopped. He turned round. He put himself in the corridor between her and the marines, with his arms a little out from his sides, the way you might stand to stop a child running into the road, and he said, quite loudly, in his flat clerk's voice:

"I'm staff. Caretaker detail. Lucan Vey, actuarial. Don't shoot."

They shot him.

She saw him jerk, and stagger, and not fall. She was at the lock. She had her hand on the plate and it would not open — red, red, the lock's plate pulsing red under her palm, because it was not her palm, because she was a bond and had no access to anything — and behind her she heard a second shot, and turned, and Lucan was coming down the corridor toward her. Not running. Walking, very carefully, with one hand pressed flat against his stomach and the other held out in front of him. His face was the colour of paper.

He reached the lock. He put his palm on the plate beside hers.

It went green. The door rolled open.

"Go," said Lucan Vey.

"Come on—"

"No." He was leaning against the frame. His palm was still on the plate. She saw that he was holding it there; that if he took it off, the door would close. "No. I'm staying. I was always staying." He smiled at her, a terrible, sweet, frightened smile, with blood on his teeth. "Somebody has to keep the account."

Behind him the marines were shouting. Something hit the wall beside the door and threw sparks.

"*Go,*" said Lucan, and she went.

She went through the lock and into the docking collar and the door rolled shut behind her, and through its little window, in the instant before the *Patience*'s outer hatch opened at her back and Dov's hands caught her and pulled her through, she saw him slide down the frame of the lock with his palm leaving a long dark smear on the plate, and sit, and lean his head back against the wall, and close his eyes.

* * *

Dov did not say anything. He pulled her through the hatch and slammed it and threw the collar release before she was on her feet, and the *Patience* fell away from High with a jolt that knocked her down again, and by the time she had got up and into the co-seat he had the old tender's nose pointed down into Hesper's shadow and the throttles open and the station falling away behind them on the aft scope, all its lights pulsing red.

She sat in the co-seat with the key in her fist and did not look at him.

He did not look at her either. He flew. He took the *Patience* down into the moon's shadow and round its limb, low, fast, through the clutter of ore tugs and relay buoys and old dead satellites that the Combine had been leaving in Hesper orbit for two hundred years, and nothing followed them. Nothing, she thought, would think it worth following. A bond and a drunk in an old tender, running for the dark. Where could they go?

On the console, the Sump's feed was running. Dov had it on low, the way he always did; she had never known him fly without it. At the first bell, as they came round Hesper's far side and the great banded curve of Tarrow came up over the moon's edge to fill the forward glass, the feed changed.

It showed a lock.

It was a plain lock, a cargo lock, somewhere on High; Maren did not know which. Its inner door was open, and inside it, small and grey in the bare white light, stood Agathe Hatch. She was in her cut-down habit. Her hands were tied in front of her. Her face was bruised down one side and her lip was split, and she stood very straight in the middle of the lock and looked at the camera with her pale steady nun's eyes, as though she could see through it — as though she could see every one of them, in every cell in the Sump, sitting in front of their feeds in the cold.

A voice off-screen read the charge. Inciting violence against officers of the Combine. Eleven dead. It was a clerk's voice, flat and quick, and Hatch did not seem to hear it.

When it was finished there was a pause, and the voice said, "Do you wish to speak?"

"Yes," said Hatch.

She looked into the camera.

"They've never once in two hundred years thought we could add," she said. Her voice was perfectly calm. "That's all. Add it up."

The inner door closed.

Maren did not watch the rest. She heard it — the long falling hiss of the lock cycling, on the feed's thin speaker — and she looked out through the forward glass at Tarrow, at the giant's banded face filling the whole of the sky, the colour of an old bruise, and did not look at the screen. When the hiss stopped she reached over and turned the feed off.

In the pilot's seat beside her, Dov Arkwright was weeping. Without a sound. His hands on the yoke were perfectly steady and his eyes were on the glass and the tears were running down his face into his collar, and he did not wipe them away.

"Thirteen," said Maren.

He did not answer.

"I counted," she said. "On the water. Every night. Fourteen of us. And on the docks this morning, I counted again. Who they'd taken. Who they'd shot. Who they'd known to look for." She did not look at him. "I came to thirteen. Every time. I came to thirteen and I stopped."

"Maren—"

"Tell me."

He told her.

* * *

He had gone to them after the first meeting on the water. After the vote. He had walked out of the Drowned Decks before anyone else — before the lamps had started going out along the catwalks — and gone up through the Sump to Level One, to the bond office, which was shut, and banged on its door until a marine came; and he had said that he had information for the Factor about the union, and that he wanted to trade it.

"I told them the strike date," he said. "The eighteenth. I told them about Hatch, which they knew. I told them about you." His voice was thick. "They said it didn't matter. They said they'd let you have the strike. That it suited them. They gave me a chit." He took it out of his breast pocket — a little square of grey plastic with the H-V crest on it, and a number — and dropped it on the console between them. "Passage on the *Grace*. Seat forty-one, Deck Two. For services. They said there'd be more if I brought them more."

Maren looked at the chit and did not touch it.

"And then Hatch told us about the docks," said Dov. "The shuttles. The signal. Who'd be on the crews." He was looking at the glass. "And I went back up. That night. After I'd done your sums for you on the back of a tally slip. I went back up and I told them all of it. Every name. Every berth. The hymn." He made a sound that was not a laugh. "I asked you what bell, and you told me, and I went and told them."

"Why?"

"Because I'm dying." He said it flatly. "Because I've got the rattle and I've had it for four years and Hatch says I've got another one, maybe, if I stop drinking, which I won't. Because I've spent fifty-five years in a hole in the ice watching Tulls go down into the red and come back up again and go down again, and I've never once seen a sky. A real one. A blue one." His voice cracked. "Castellane said the Inner Worlds had skies. He said it on the platform. I thought — just once. Just once before I go. I thought, I'll sit in seat forty-one on Deck Two and go through the Throat and I'll get off at Corvid and I'll find somewhere with a sky and I'll lie down under it and that'll be that." He wiped his face at last, with the back of one hand. "That's all. That's all it was."

"And this morning?"

"This morning I took the chit down to the convoy dock at the third bell," said Dov, "before the farewell, before any of you came up, with my bag packed. Thirty years of my life in one bag. And I showed it to the clerk at the desk." He looked at the chit on the console. "And the clerk looked at it, and looked it up, and called her supervisor. And the supervisor came, and looked at me, and laughed. Not cruelly. She just laughed, the way you'd laugh at a dog that's learned to open a door. And she said: *I'm sorry, Mr. Arkwright. The Factor regrets. You're a fixed asset.*"

The *Patience* hummed round them. Tarrow filled the glass.

"Fixed," said Dov softly. "That was all. I went back down to the head with my bag and sat in this seat until your clerk called." He turned his head and looked at her at last. "I came. I want you to know. I didn't have to come. I could have stayed in the ice and let you get shot on that station. I came."

She hit him.

She did not decide to. Her fist was moving before she knew it, across the space between the seats, and it took him on the side of the jaw and his head went sideways into the cockpit frame with a crack she felt in her own hand. She hit him again. He did not lift his arms. He sat in the pilot's seat with his hands still on the yoke and let her, and she hit him a third time and felt something in her knuckles give, and stopped, and sat back in the co-seat with her broken hand in her lap and her breath coming in great tearing sobs, like the rattle, like the giant breathing out.

"Farrant," she said. "Hatch. Lucan. Eleven on the docks. Ruth and Ade and Pym, tomorrow, the day after. *Pell*." She could hardly speak. "You held the light. You held the light when I was born."

"I know," said Dov.

"She said *count what's owed*. My mother. She held me up and said it, to you, and you never asked what she meant."

"I know."

"She meant *look at her*. She meant: this is what's owed. This. Not the bond. Not the Combine. *Her.*" She was shaking. "And you sold it for a seat."

"I know," said Dov Arkwright, with blood running from the corner of his mouth into his collar where the tears had gone. "I know, girl. I know."

They sat in the cockpit of the *Old Patience* with the giant filling the glass, and neither of them said anything for a long time.

* * *

It was the scope that broke it. A chime, from the console — a new contact, far behind them, at Hesper. Dov looked at it, out of long habit, and Maren saw his face change.

"There she goes," he said.

She looked. On the aft scope, very small, at the edge of Hesper's disc, a light had come on that was brighter than anything else in the moon's orbit — a long blue-white flare, steady, enormous, growing. The *Grace*'s drives. The convoy was leaving.

And ahead of it — well ahead of it, already far out from Hesper and moving very fast, on a line that ran straight across the system toward the Throat — a second light. Smaller. Hotter.

"*Indemnity*," said Dov. "The escort frigate. She's running ahead. She's running hard." He turned and looked at Maren. "Your Factor's not stupid. He's guessed where you're going, girl. He's sending her on to sit on Gallows and wait for you."

Maren opened her left hand. Her right was swelling, two knuckles broken, useless. In her left lay the key, white ceramic and grooved steel, still faintly warm. She looked at it.

Then she looked at Dov. At his bruised face, his wet beard, his steady hands on the yoke.

"How long?" she said. "To the limb."

He did not answer at once. He looked at her the way he had looked at her in this same cockpit three nights before, when she had asked him the same thing and he had already had the figures on his knee.

"Nine hours to the dive line," he said. "If I start now."

"Then start now."

"You're going to let me fly you?"

"I'm going to make you," said Maren. "You're the only pilot in the Sump who's ever taken a tender down the limb and lived. You and my mother. And she's dead." She closed her hand on the key. "So you'll fly. And you'll get me there. And then we'll see what's owed."

Dov Arkwright looked at her for a moment longer. Then he reached down and picked the grey chit up off the console — seat forty-one, Deck Two — and turned it over once in his fingers, and opened the side vent beside his seat, and dropped it out into the dark.

"Strap in," he said. "And put the *Gannet*'s systems on warm. You'll want her ready." He pushed the throttles forward, and the *Old Patience* shuddered all through her old frame and turned her nose down toward the giant. "Your mother never went past a thousand. She'd have hated this."

"She'd have loved it," said Maren, and strapped in.


# Chapter 17: The Long Fall

*The Ledger · 2794*

On the second day out, with the Nave a bright star behind them and Strand's cutter a fainter one behind that, Ysolde took Anselm's sheet out of her coat and unfolded it on the chart table in the *Small Mercy*'s cramped cabin, and began to write the receipt.

She did not call it that at first. She called it the audit. It was what an auditor did when a Ledger was closed: she took the account from its opening to its last line and set it out fair, in the forms, year by year, every debit and every credit, so that anyone who came after could check it and find it true. She had done it for small things a hundred times — a family's line closed by death, a crew's account closed when their boat was lost. She had never done it for this.

Anselm had done most of it already. That was what she saw, now, with the sheet spread out under the cabin lamp and the old boat humming round her. Every line on it was a year: the year's tithe from all nine Chanceries, summed, which he must have carried out of the stacks in his head night after night, the way she had carried the Compact; the Founding Terms charged on what remained; the balance carried. Three hundred and forty-one lines in his small upright pencil, from *Founding* to the present, and the box drawn round Year 280.

What he had not done was prove it. He had not had the First Book. He had built his whole sheet on a single figure he had read once, forty years ago, on a page left out on a dead Prelate's desk, and carried in his memory ever since; and he had been right — she had stood in the Reliquary and seen it printed, *a hundred and eighteen million, four hundred thousand* — but a figure carried in a man's memory was not a proof. Not to an auditor. Not to the Nave.

So she proved it. She wrote it out fresh on reckoning sheets from Corin's stock, in ink, in the Combine's own forms that every Ledger in the Nave still used, starting at the top of the first sheet with the heading she had seen printed on the First Book's first page.

*ACCOUNT HV-VIGIL-0001. STATEMENT OF RECONCILIATION.*

And under it, the Founding, as the Combine had printed it. The Founding Terms, as the Combine had printed them. And then the years, one by one, each line checked against Anselm's pencil and against her own memory of the Chancery summaries she had read in the stacks over twenty-two years, and where she could not check it, marked *per Ferrow*, so that anyone reading it would know whose figure it was.

It took her six days. She worked through the ship's nights and its days, which were the same, at the chart table with her back to the bulkhead and her knees drawn up, while Corin flew. Sometimes he came and stood behind her and read over her shoulder for a while, his lips moving, and went away again without speaking. Once he brought her a bulb of hot water with a little of the gold Nave spirit in it that he said he had been saving for twelve years, and she drank it without noticing and did not know until afterward what it was.

On the sixth day she wrote the line for Year 280, and drew a box round it as Anselm had, and wrote inside the box *Balance: nil. Account closed.*

And then she wrote the sixty-one lines after it, every one, each year's tithe entered as a credit against an account that owed nothing, until the last line, the three hundred and forty-first year, the year she was sitting in. And at the foot of that she wrote the total of all sixty-one: what had been paid into the Count since it was zero. What the Lungs had given, in hours and lives and children sent down at fourteen, to an account that had been closed before her mother was born.

She looked at the figure for a long time.

It was not a number she had a word for. It was larger than the Founding. It was larger, she thought, than anything the Combine could ever have imagined it was owed.

At the foot of the last sheet, under the total, she wrote: *Overpaid.*

And then, because it was what auditors did, and because there was a space for it in the forms, she wrote: *Reconciled by Y. Marr, keeper of the Ninth Ledger, Third Chancery, from the figures of A. Ferrow, keeper of the Ninth before her.* And under that, a line, and beside the line the words she had written at the foot of every closing she had ever made: *Seal of the Prelate-Auditor.*

The line was empty. Of course it was. Without the Prelate's seal a closing was only paper — the Nave's law, the Combine's law, every law there had ever been in the forms. She had known that when she started. She sat looking at the empty line, and the empty space above it where an old man's ring would have pressed its mark into the paper, and laughed, quite quietly, until Corin put his head round the cockpit door to see what was the matter.

* * *

He told her about Tamsin on the seventh night.

They were in the Shear's outer edge by then. She had not known what that meant until they were in it; nobody in the Nave did. It was a region, Corin said, a great slow whirlpool of wrongness round the Door, where space itself had been bent and torn when the Door was shut and had never quite healed — where the stars swam in the forward glass like reflections in a stream, and the *Small Mercy*'s instruments disagreed with each other, and every few hours the whole boat would lurch sideways as if a hand had pushed it. Corin flew it with his face set and his hands light on the yoke and did not sleep. On the seventh night she sat in the second seat beside him because she could not sleep either, and after a long silence, out of nothing, he began to talk.

"She was good," he said. "That's the thing. She was a better diver than me at fourteen than I was at thirty. She could read the red. Some people can — you can't teach it. She'd look at a deck of cloud on the scope and say *there's a seam under that, about nine hundred down*, and she'd be right, and I never knew how she knew." He moved the yoke a fraction; the stars swam. "She went down on the *Hesper's Daughter*. Old Rennet's boat, from Spoke Twenty-one. I wasn't diving any more by then, my lungs had gone. I was on the tally line at the head when they brought the boat back up without her."

"What happened?"

"Sink. Downdraft. She'd taken on a full draw from a seam she'd found and the sink took her and she had to dump." He was quiet a moment. "She didn't dump. Rennet said so after; he was on the tender. He said she came on the link and said, *I'm not dumping it, Rennet, it's three months of our share, they'll take the shop*, and he told her to dump and she didn't. She was fourteen. She'd done the sum." He looked at the glass. "Three months of our share. Against her. And she thought it came out."

Ysolde was silent.

"I went up to the Nave," said Corin. "After. I went up on the drop shuttle with her tag in my fist and I walked into the Ledger Hall and I stood at the rail in front of the Board at the Turning. And I watched it change. The units and the tens and the hundreds, all rushing over." He moved his hand, a little rippling motion. "And I knew that somewhere in there was her hours. Her last dive. The draw she wouldn't dump. Somebody in the Third Chancery had entered it that morning and summed it and sent it up, and it was in there, in the number, making it smaller." He smiled, very faintly. "It fell by about two thousand that morning. I remember. Two thousand marks. That was my daughter."

"Corin—"

"I scratched my first circle that night," he said. "On the wall outside my shop. I didn't even know what it meant, really. I'd seen them. I knew it was the Nullist sign. I just stood there with my knife and cut it as deep as I could, and when the Bailiffs ground it off a week later, I cut it again." He looked at her at last. "Twenty years. I've never once known whether it was true. Whether there was anything to it. Whether the Count was a lie, or just a weight. I just knew I had to cut it." He was quiet. "And then you came into my shop with Anselm's tag and his sheet, and sat down in my back room and told me Year three hundred and two."

"Two hundred and eighty."

"Two hundred and eighty." He nodded slowly. "Tamsin went down in the three hundred and twenty-sixth year." He turned back to the glass. "Forty-six years after it was paid."

They flew for a while without talking. The stars swam.

"When you write it," Corin said at last. "Your audit. Your closing. Put her in it."

"She's in it. All the years are in it."

"Not like that. Not in a sum." He did not look at her. "Put her name. Somewhere. In the margin. *Tamsin Asch, aged fourteen, Year three twenty-six.* So that whoever reads it knows that some of the numbers were people." His hands were very still on the yoke. "That's all. I'd like her written down somewhere that isn't the Count."

Ysolde got up, and went back to the chart table, and found the line for the three hundred and twenty-sixth year, and dipped her pen.

* * *

On the eighth day they saw it.

Corin saw it first. He had the boat's old long-glass rigged to the forward scope and he had been sweeping the dark ahead of them for hours, through the swimming stars, looking for what Grandad had called the Hanged Men; and in the middle of the ship's afternoon he made a small sound in his throat and sat back from the eyepiece and said, "There."

She looked.

The Door first. She had seen it all her life — a faint smudge low in Tarrow's sky, a place where the stars were a little wrong, that the novices were taught to bow to at the Turning. From here, at the end of the Shear, it was not faint. It filled the middle of the scope: a great round lens of bent light, stars smeared round its rim in long arcs like the grain in polished wood, and at its heart a place where there was nothing at all. Not black. *Nothing.* The eye would not stay on it. It slid away, the way it slid off a thing in the corner of a room that was not there when you turned.

And at its edge, small against that enormous wrongness — so small she would never have found it if Corin had not put the crosshair on it — something hung.

It was a station. She could see that much. A long thin spine, dark against the bent light, lying along the Door's rim like a bar laid across the mouth of a well. And from the spine, at four points, on long threads so fine she could only see them where they caught the light, hung four great dark cylinders. They did not move. They hung there round the rim of the Door at equal distances, quite still, their tethers taut, like weights on a set of scales.

Like four bodies on a gallows.

"Gallows," she said.

"The Hanged Men." Corin's voice was strange. "Grandad's Hanged Men. He never saw them. He said he never had a good glass." He was quiet a moment. "Old man would've liked to see this."

She looked at it. There were no lights on it. There was nothing to show that anyone had been there in three hundred and forty years. Frost on the spine, maybe, where the faint far light of Vigil caught it — a pale rime along its upper edge. A darker patch near its centre that might have been a docking ring.

"What was done there?" she said. "What could anyone have done there?"

"We'll know in a day," said Corin. And then, without changing his tone at all, looking not at the forward scope but at the aft: "If we get there."

* * *

Strand's cutter had been closing all through the Shear. It was faster than the *Small Mercy* — anything in the system was faster than the *Small Mercy*, Corin said, except possibly the Count Board — and in open space it would have caught them in three days. In the Shear it had been slower, because the Bailiffs did not know the currents and Corin did. But Strand had good pilots, and the Shear's outer edge was behind them now, and the inner reaches round the Door were calmer, and the cutter's light on the aft scope had been growing all through the eighth day like a slow sunrise.

At the ninth bell of the ship's evening it hailed them.

"*Small Mercy.*" Strand's voice, pleasant and patient, crackling through the Shear. "*This is the Prelate's cutter* Recompense*. Heave to and prepare to be boarded. Sister Marr, Mr. Asch. You've had a long trip. Let's not make it longer.*"

Corin did not answer. He was watching the scope.

"*Mr. Asch,*" said Strand. "*I know you can hear me. You've no weapons on that boat. I've four. I've been asked not to use them, but I've also been asked not to let you reach that station, and I've been told which request comes first.*"

Still Corin did not answer.

There was a pause on the band. Then a different voice, older, slower, very tired, that made Ysolde's hands close on the arms of her seat.

"*Ysolde.*"

It was the Prelate.

"*Ysolde, it's Aurel Quist. I'm on the cutter. I came myself.*" A pause; she could hear him breathing. "*I asked you not to. In the chapel. Do you remember? I asked you to leave it shut. You gave me your pages and I thought — I hoped — that meant you'd heard me.*" Another pause. "*Come back. Please. It's not too late. Come back with me and we'll go home, and I'll strike the default, and no one will ever know you came out here. I'll say you were lost in the Shear. I'll say anything.*"

She reached for the band switch. Corin's hand closed over hers.

"Don't," he said quietly. "He's keeping you talking. He's got you on a bearing."

"*I don't know what's on that station,*" said the Prelate's voice. "*I've told you that. No one knows. But I know the Stewards closed it, and I know they were wise, and I know that the one thing they ever forbade — the only thing in three hundred and forty years that they ever wrote down as* forbidden — *is the place you're going. Think, Ysolde. You're a counter. What sort of thing would twelve people who'd lie to thirty thousand without flinching be too frightened to say?*"

She sat with Corin's hand over hers on the band switch and did not move.

"*Come back,*" said Aurel Quist. "*Leave it shut.*"

The band clicked off.

Corin let go of her hand. He looked at the aft scope, and then the forward, where Gallows hung tiny against the Door's bent light. And then — she saw it happen; she saw him do the sum — he looked down at the third screen on the console, the one he had been glancing at all through the Shear, a little dark display with a scatter of faint red points on it that she had never asked about.

"Ysolde," he said. "Can you fly the pup?"

* * *

The pup was the *Small Mercy*'s jolly-boat: a squat little cargo skiff that lived clamped in a cradle under the old boat's belly, with room in her for a pilot and six full-draw bottles and not much else. Corin used her for running the last kilometres into the far relays, where the *Mercy* was too big to dock. He had shown Ysolde how she worked on the fourth day, because there was nothing else to do and because, he said, nobody should be on a boat they couldn't get off. Yoke, throttle, a docking arm, a simple scope. She had flown her once round the *Mercy*'s hull, at a crawl, and been sick afterward.

"Why?" she said.

He tapped the dark screen. The scatter of red points.

"Pickets," he said. "Mines. Old ones. Combine. They're all over the inner reaches round the Door — have been forever. Most of them are dead. Some of them aren't." He put his finger on one, a little apart from the others, glowing a little brighter. "That one's not. It's been tracking us for an hour. If we go near it, it'll wake up and do what it was made to do." He looked at the aft scope. "The *Recompense* doesn't know it's there. Strand's never been out here. Nobody has."

"Corin—"

"I'm going to take the *Mercy* past it," said Corin Asch. "Close. Slow. Like I'm trying to slip through the picket line. And Strand's going to follow me, because he'll think I've found a way through, and because he's been told not to let me reach that station. And when he's close enough—" He opened his hand, slowly, fingers spreading. "It'll wake up."

"And you?"

"I'll be closer."

She stared at him.

"It's the sum, Ysolde," he said. "Do it. You're the counter." He was very calm. "The *Mercy* can't outrun that cutter. Not in clear space. Not for the last day. He'll be on us by morning, and then he'll board us, and then the old man will take you home, and nobody will ever know what's on that station. And everything in your coat goes into the Prelate's stove." He looked at her. "Or you take the pup, and the breath, and you go in on your own while he's chasing me. He won't see you. The pup's too small and too cold. And then you're at Gallows, and he's not."

"I can't fly the pup."

"You flew her round the hull."

"I was *sick*."

"So be sick at Gallows." He was already unbuckling. "Come on. The bottles are loaded, I did it this morning. I've been thinking about this since we entered the Shear." He stood, stooping under the cockpit's low roof, and held out his hand. "Come on, Sister. Your brother told you not to do anything stupid. I'm telling you to do this."

She did not move.

"Tamsin did the sum," she said. "You told me. Three months of your share, against her. And she thought it came out."

"Yes."

"And it was wrong. You said. It was wrong because she'd done it with the Count's numbers, and the Count's numbers were lies." She was shaking. "How do you know yours aren't?"

Corin Asch looked down at her for a long moment in the dim cockpit, with the Door's bent light coming through the glass behind him and laying its long smeared arcs across his ruined face.

"Because they're mine," he said. "Not the Count's. Not the Nave's. Mine. I've counted them myself." He held out his hand again. "Come on."

* * *

The pup's cradle was under the cargo belly, through a hatch in the floor of the hold, among the racked bottles. He helped her down into the little cockpit and strapped her in himself, with his scarred hands, tugging every buckle twice. Six full-draw bottles were lashed in the space behind the seat — breath, he said, enough to start an engine or wake a dead machine or do whatever she thought she was going to do — and her satchel was wedged beside them, with her audit in it, and Anselm's sheet, and her copies of the Compact.

He checked the board. He set the pup's simple scope to Gallows. He showed her, again, the throttle and the yoke and the docking arm, slowly, as if she were a novice.

"Cold drift for the first hour," he said. "No drive, no heat, nothing. Let the *Mercy* carry you clear and then just fall. He'll be watching me. After an hour, light her up gentle and steer for the spine. There's a docking ring near the middle; you'll see it. The arm'll find the collar on its own if you get it close." He looked at her. "Don't hurry. You've got all the time in the world, once I'm done."

"Corin."

"What?"

She did not know. She had thought there would be something to say and there was not. She looked up at him out of the pup's cockpit, at his long hollowed face and the scar down his throat and the red line over his eye where the Bailiffs had cut him in his own shop, and found that she had put her hand up and laid it flat against his chest, over his heart, the way she had once laid it against the glass of the novice dormitory's window on her first night in the Nave, to feel the cold.

He covered it with his own.

"Put her in the margin," he said. "That's all."

"I did."

"I know. I watched you." He took her hand off his chest and put it on the pup's yoke, and closed her fingers round it. "Count what's owed, Sister."

"And not a mark more," she said.

He smiled — the first time, she realised, that she had ever seen him smile; it made him look like someone else entirely, someone young — and closed the pup's canopy over her, and she heard him go up through the hatch, and heard the hatch seal, and then nothing.

* * *

The *Mercy* dropped her at the ninth bell of the ship's night. A clank, a lurch, and the pup fell out of her cradle into the dark, tumbling slowly end over end, and through the canopy Ysolde watched the old boat's scarred belly slide away above her and turn and go.

She did not light the drive. She did as he had said. She lay in the pup's cold cockpit with her hands off the controls and her father's tag in her fist, and let the dark take her, and watched.

The *Mercy* went out toward the picket line slowly, her drive lit low, like a boat feeling her way through shallow water. On the pup's little scope Ysolde could see her — a bright point — and behind her, coming on, the brighter point of the *Recompense*. And off to the side of both, faint and red and alone, the mine.

She watched the two lights draw together. She watched the *Mercy* turn, gently, toward the red point, as if she had seen something there — a gap, a way through. She watched the *Recompense* turn after her.

On the band, very faint, Strand's voice: "*Mr. Asch. Heave to. Mr. Asch—*"

And Corin's voice, for the first time, answering. She would remember it for the rest of her life. Not loud. Not angry. Just a little dry, a little tired, the voice of a man behind his counter with a valve in pieces in front of him, telling an auditor the shop was shut.

"*Count it, Captain,*" said Corin Asch.

The red point on the scope went white.

She saw the light of it through the canopy — not on the scope; with her own eyes, far off, a soundless flower of white fire opening in the dark among the swimming stars, very bright, and then less bright, and then fading, the way a lamp fades when you turn it down. In its light, for an instant, she saw the *Recompense*: tumbling, its drive out, a long gash torn down one flank, venting a pale plume of air into the dark. Crippled. Not dead.

And of the *Small Mercy* there was nothing at all.

* * *

She lay in the pup in the dark for the hour he had told her to, and then for another, because she could not make her hands move. Then she made them move.

She lit the drive gently, the way he had said. She steered for the spine. She was sick once, as she had known she would be, and cleaned it up with the sleeve of Bett's coat, and steered again. The Door grew in the canopy until it filled it, that great lens of bent light with nothing at its heart; and against it, growing too, the long dark spine of Gallows, and the four great cylinders hanging from it on their threads.

They were enormous. She had not understood how enormous. Each of the Hanged Men was the size of a Chancery, of a refinery head, of the Ledger Hall itself — dark smooth cylinders as tall as towers, hanging motionless on tethers as thick as the Count Board's chains, round the rim of a hole in the world. The spine they hung from was longer than the Nave. It was frosted along its whole length, a pale rime thick as snow, and it was dark. Not a light. Not a sign.

She found the docking ring near the middle. She got the pup close, and closer, and then the arm found the collar on its own, as Corin had said it would, with a clank that went through the little boat like a struck bell. She sat in the cockpit with her heart going and listened to the seals take.

They held.

She put on the pup's spare suit, which was Corin's, and too big. She put her satchel over her shoulder. She looked at the six breath bottles lashed behind her seat, and decided they could wait. She opened the pup's hatch, and the docking collar beyond it, and stood in front of the station's outer door: a round plate of grey metal, rimed with three hundred and forty years of frost, with a wheel at its centre and a line of the old square lettering across its top.

*H-V PASSAGE CONTROL. GALLOWS.*

She put her gloved hands on the wheel. It would not turn. She put her weight on it, and then her whole body, braced against the collar's frame, and felt something give deep inside the door with a long groan that she heard through her boots.

The wheel turned.

The door swung inward. Beyond it was a darkness so complete that her lamp, when she lifted it, seemed to make no impression on it at all — only a little pool of light on a frosted floor, and frost on the walls, and air so cold it came through the suit, and silence.

She stepped through.


# Chapter 18: What I Owed

*The Door · 3190*

It was Benno Strick who opened the door, and Haddie Coyle standing behind him with a lamp.

I didn't understand it at first. I was on the floor of the dead storeroom where they'd left me, with one eye swollen shut and my ribs telling me things I didn't want to hear, and the light came in round the edge of the door and then flooded in, and there was Benno's long earnest face looking down at me, white, and behind his shoulder an old woman in two coats and cracked spectacles with her mouth set in a line like a cut.

"Get up, Absalom," said Haddie. "You're making a spectacle of yourself."

"How—"

"I walked." She came past Benno into the storeroom and knelt down beside me, slowly, with a crack of old knees. "Up from the core, the long way, through those corridors of yours. It took me most of a day. And then I came out on the main dock in front of a hundred of Kask's reeves and sat down on a crate and asked for Benno Strick by name." She put her hand on my face, the cold bent fingers, turning it to the light to look at my eye. "His grandmother was a friend of mine. Did you know that? Ilse Strick. We dived together on the *Fenwick* when we were girls. I held him when he was a day old." She looked up at Benno. "I told him that. And then I told him what you went to the Deep for, and what you were going to do at the Spider. And then I asked him what his grandmother would have said."

Benno was looking at the floor.

"She'd have said I was a bloody idiot," he said. "She said it most days anyway."

"There," said Haddie. "Now help me get him up."

* * *

They told me while I got my breath, sitting on a crate in the storeroom with the door shut and Benno's lamp turned down. They told me fast, because there wasn't time to tell it any other way.

The receiver was coming through.

Not the tenders. Not the drones. The thing itself — the big one Haddie had seen behind the Door, waiting. The Door had opened enough. Benno had heard it from Kask's own mouth, in the dock control room, four hours ago, where she'd been talking to the receiver on the band the way she'd talk to an old friend. It had told her that the passage was at threshold. That it would begin its transit within the day. That the factor should have the depot ready for loading.

"It'll take everybody," said Benno. His voice shook. "Every pod. There's eleven thousand on the outer arms already, Rook, and more coming in every hour. It'll load them all, and Kask's household, and go back through. She says it'll take two days to load. She says—" He stopped.

"Says what?"

"She says once it's through, she'll go out to the Spider." He looked at me. "To Gallows. The receiver told her. About the station. It said Gallows controls the passage — that whoever holds Gallows can close it. She asked it a lot of questions about that." He swallowed. "She said to me, Benno, once that thing's through and loaded, and it's sitting on this side of the Door with everything it's taken, it's going to want to go home. And I'm going to be sitting at Gallows with my hand on the Door. And then we'll talk about my commission."

"She's going to hold it hostage," said Haddie softly. "The receiver. She's going to sit at the Spider and threaten to shut the Door on it, unless it pays her more."

"And if it doesn't?"

Benno didn't answer.

"She'll shut it," I said. "With all of them on the wrong side. With every pod aboard." I looked at him. "She'll shut it and keep Kell Deep and be Margrave of a system with nobody in it but her reeves."

"I don't know," said Benno Strick miserably. "I don't know what she'll do. I've never known. Nobody has."

"Where's Wren?"

"In the holding bay on Arm Four. With the children. All the ones from the core." He looked up. "She's keeping them back. Not podding them. She wants to give them to the receiver herself, when it docks. As a gift. She says it'll look well." His face twisted. "She says it'll be remembered when terms are made."

I got up. My ribs said a number of things. I didn't listen.

"Arm Four," I said. "How many reeves?"

"Six. And drones." Benno looked at me. "You can't. You can barely stand."

"I've barely stood before," I said. "Get me a knife."

* * *

Arm Four was one of the Hulk's old docking arms, a long tube half a kilometre out from the station's main ring with berths along its length. They'd stacked pods along its whole outer wall, three deep, under the dead ports, so that walking down it was like walking down a corridor in a cemetery with the graves standing up. At its far end there was a bay — an old cargo hold with a heavy door — and outside the door, under a work-lamp, sat two of Kask's reeves on crates, playing cards.

Benno walked down to them. I watched from behind the last stack of pods with Haddie at my shoulder. He walked down the middle of the arm in his red coat with his lamp held up, easy, the way a reeve walks when he's on his own side, and the two of them looked up from their cards and one said *Benno*, and Benno said *Margrave wants a count of the kids*, and they both got up.

He'd told me there were six. There were six. Two at the door and four inside, and drones — three of them, standing in a row along the bay's back wall, quite still, like furniture. I went in behind Benno while the first two reeves were looking at his lamp.

I'll not tell it all. I've told enough of that kind of thing in this account, and I'm tired of it, and it doesn't get easier to set down. I'll tell you that I was fifty-two years old with two cracked ribs and one eye, and that it didn't matter as much as it should have, because the reeves at the door had been trained by me and I knew every single thing they were going to do before they did it. I'll tell you that Benno put one of them down with his lamp, and then stood there staring at what he'd done. I'll tell you that one of the four inside got a shot off, and it took Benno high in the left shoulder and spun him round, and he sat down against the bay wall with his hand pressed to it and said *oh* in a small surprised voice. And I'll tell you that when it was done, the six reeves were on the floor and I was standing in the middle of the bay with my knife in my hand and my breath coming in great tearing gulps, and the children were looking at me.

They were in the back half of the bay, behind a cargo net strung floor to ceiling like a fence. Forty-odd. The ones from the Deep and the ones from Number Two, the Fenn girl with her baby, Kesse with her burned ear and a split lip, Odo with his squint. They were pressed against the net, staring.

And Wren, at the front, with her wired hands, looking at me through the mesh the way she'd looked at me through the gap between two reeves on the main dock. No expression. Water gone still all the way down.

I didn't say anything to her. I didn't think I had the right. I went to the net and started cutting.

* * *

The drones moved while I was cutting.

I'd forgotten them. That's the truth. They'd stood so still along the back wall through all of it — the reeves, the shot, Benno going down — that I'd stopped seeing them, the way you stop seeing a stack of crates. And then I heard the click. One click, small, the sound of a jointed leg moving on a metal deck. And then another.

I turned round.

They were coming. All three. Not fast, not slow, in that patient orderly way, across the bay toward the net. Toward the children. Toward me.

"*Debtors will remain in custody,*" said the calm voice of the receiver, from somewhere, from everywhere — from the bay's dead speakers and the reeves' fallen radios and the little hand-band in Benno's belt where he sat bleeding against the wall. "*Resistance to recovery constitutes a further default.*"

"Rook," said Wren behind me, through the net. Very quietly. "Rook, they're going to pod us."

I had my knife. I stood between them and the net with my knife up, and they kept coming, and I knew — I knew the way you know the floor of a dive is about to drop away — that a breather's knife against those things was going to mean exactly nothing at all.

"*The door!*" That was Haddie. "Absalom! The bay door! It's on the panel — the old hand — it closes the inner hatch, it'll seal the back of the bay—"

I looked. She was at the wall by the bay entrance, at an old control panel I hadn't even seen, black glass and grey switches with lettering on them in the square dead hand that only she could read. She had her spectacles pushed up and her face an inch from the panel, and her bent old fingers were moving over the switches, fast.

"There's a hatch," she said. "A pressure hatch. Behind the net. It comes down from the ceiling. If I can make it come down—"

"*Then make it come down!*"

"I'm *trying*, you great fool, it's three hundred years old—"

The first drone reached me. It put out two of its rods — I saw them come, slow, almost gentle — toward my shoulders. I cut at them. The knife skated off them like a blade off ice. And then they touched me, very lightly, on both shoulders, and I felt it: a cold that went through my coat and my skin and into the bone, and in the middle of the black jointed body in front of me something began to open, pale and soft and glistening, like oil.

"*Haddie—*"

Something came down behind me with a sound like the end of the world.

It was the hatch. A great slab of grey metal, a hand's width thick, dropping out of the bay ceiling behind the cargo net on old tracks that screamed, and hitting the deck with a boom that I felt through my boots and my teeth and my broken ribs. The net was on our side of it. The children were on the other — in the back of the bay, behind a pressure door that had been built three hundred years ago to hold back vacuum.

The drone in front of me paused. Its rods were still on my shoulders. The pale glistening thing in its middle had stopped opening.

"*Debtors are not accessible,*" said the receiver's calm voice, after a moment, as if it were noting the weather. "*Re-routing.*" The rods came off my shoulders. The pale thing folded itself away. And all three drones turned, together, the way the tenders had turned in the Door — and went toward the bay entrance.

Toward the panel.

"Haddie," I said.

She'd turned round. She had her back against the panel and her hands spread out on either side of her, flat against the switches, the way you'd stand to stop someone getting at a cupboard. The drones were coming at her across the bay, all three, slow and patient. She looked at them, and then past them, at me.

"The hatch stays down while the switch is held," she said. Her voice was quite calm. "I can't tell how to lock it. I haven't got the word. If I let go it comes up again." She smiled at me over her cracked spectacles. "So I'm not going to let go."

"Haddie, *get out of the way—*"

"There's another way into the back of the bay," she said. "Through the cargo lock, on the outside of the arm. Benno knows it. Go round. Get them out. Get the girl out." She was looking at the drones now. They were very close. "And take the book, Absalom. It's in my coat. The old one. Give it to her. She can read it now. She's quicker than I ever was."

The first drone touched her shoulders.

I'll not tell you about the next part. I've decided. I've told you everything else in this account, every bad thing I ever did and most of the bad things I ever saw, and I'm not going to tell you this one. I'll tell you that she didn't let go of the switch. I'll tell you that the drone that touched her paused, as it had paused with me, as if something in it were doing a sum — and then the receiver's calm voice said, from everywhere, *"Debtor is aged. Debtor is of low recovery value. Debtor is obstructing recovery,"* and the drone did something else instead. Something quicker. And I'll tell you that she still didn't let go of the switch, even then, for longer than I would have believed anybody could.

When the drones had gone — when they'd stood over her for a moment, and then turned, and walked out of the bay into the arm in their patient row, re-routing — I went across the bay and knelt down beside her. Her hands were still flat on the panel. I had to take them off it, one finger at a time, the way I'd taken Garra's off his alarm.

The hatch behind the net didn't come up. It must have locked when it took her weight. I don't know. I couldn't read the panel.

Her spectacles had fallen off. I picked them up and put them in my pocket. And in the inside of her coat, against her chest, wrapped in a piece of oilcloth, I found the oldest of her nine Count-books, the brittle yellow one with the brown ink, open at the page with the word *Gallows* on it, where she'd been reading it when she came up to find Benno.

* * *

Benno took us round to the cargo lock. He could walk, just, with his arm strapped to his chest in his own belt and his face the colour of the pods. It was on the outside of the arm, the lock — we had to go out in suits, along the arm's skin on a line, among the stacked pods in the vacuum — and at the end of it there was an old round hatch with a wheel, which I turned, and inside, in the dark, the back of the bay.

They were all there. Forty-odd, pressed against the grey slab of the pressure hatch in the dark, with Wren in front of them with her wired hands. When the cargo lock cycled and the light came in, the little ones screamed, and then saw who it was, and stopped.

I cut the wire off Wren's hands. She let me.
