She didn't look at me while I did it, and when it was done she didn't say anything; she just went to the children and started getting them into suits from the bay's old emergency locker — there were a dozen; we'd have to go in relays — and I helped her, and for a long time neither of us said a word.

It was in the cargo lock, in the end, with the last relay of children suited up and gone out along the line to Benno's cutter and only the two of us left in the bay, that I told her.

I didn't plan it. I didn't stand there and work out the words. I'd had seven years to work out the words, and I'd never once managed it, and I didn't manage it then. I just found I was saying it. Standing in the dark of the bay, with the grey hatch behind us and an old woman on the other side of it who'd held it shut with her hands, I told Wren what Kask hadn't.

How Jory's name came up fourth on the roll. How I read it out in the cargo pod in front of the Lash Council in my reeve's voice, steady, and nobody moved. How I went home and sat on the edge of his bunk and watched him sleep for six hours, with the roll in my coat, and did the sum. How the sum came out. How I got up, at the end of the six hours, and put my coat on, and went to Kell, and stood in Kask's study with tears on my face and asked her for one name.

How I went back to Lanthorn with her yes in my pocket and sat down at the table in the cargo pod with the roll spread out in front of me, alone, at the third hour of the morning, and went down the list of every nine-year-old child on Lanthorn, looking for one nobody would miss.

"I was looking for a reason," I said. "I want you to know that. I didn't just pick. I went down the whole roll, three times, and I was looking for a reason. Somebody sick. Somebody whose family had something coming to them. Somebody who'd done something. I wanted it to be *fair*." I heard my own voice, flat and dead in the bay. "There wasn't anybody. There were four hundred and twelve children on that roll and not one of them had done anything at all. So I stopped looking for a reason and started looking for a cost. Who'd cost me least. Whose mother had no man and no friends on the Council and nobody who'd come to my door. And I found your name. And I crossed out his and wrote in yours, in my own hand, and stamped your tag the next morning, and put it on you myself on the gangway."

Wren was standing very still in the dark.

"You looked at me," I said. "The whole way down the gangway. With your chin up. Like you were writing me down." I stopped. "I've thought about that look every night for ten years."

"Good," said Wren.

"And he died anyway. Jory. Two years after. Red fever." I looked at her. "So I'd done it for nothing. That's what Kask said, and she was right. I did the sum and it came out, and I did it, and it was for nothing. And I've done that sum every night since, and every night it still comes out. That's the worst of it. It still comes out. Any father would have done it. Your mother would have done it, for you." I made myself hold her eyes. "That doesn't make it anything but what it was."

She was quiet a long while. The bay was very quiet. Through the hatch behind us I thought I could hear, faint, the receiver's voice in the arm, speaking its notice.

"Do you want me to forgive you?" said Wren at last.

"No."

"Good," she said again. "Because I don't." She looked at me in the dark, steady, nineteen years old, with her burned hands at her sides. "I don't forgive you. I'm not ever going to. Ten years in the vents. My mother dead with nobody to ask. Mattie. Haddie." Her voice didn't shake. "You owe me, Rook."

"Yes."

"You owe me more than you can pay. You owe me more than you've got."

"Yes."

"So pay what you've got," said Wren, "and don't stop."

She turned round and went to the cargo lock, and put her helmet on, and stood there with her hand on the wheel, waiting for me.

* * *

Benno took the children. He had a reeve's cutter at the end of Arm Four, a newer boat than mine with room in her for twenty at a squeeze and forty if nobody breathed much, and he said he'd take them to the Deep.

"The *Deep*?" I said.

"It's empty." He was sitting in the cutter's pilot seat with his strapped arm and his grey face, and he almost smiled. "She's moved everybody to the depot. Every reeve, every runner, every hand. There's nobody at Kell Deep but the rock and the heat. Nobody'll look there for days." He looked at the children packing in behind him, the Fenn girl with her baby, Kesse, Odo, the little ones. "I know the vents. I'll find somewhere warm."

"Benno."

"I know, Rook." He looked at me. "I know. I'll keep them. Go on." He looked past me at Wren, standing at the cutter's hatch. "Go and shut it."

* * *

The *Mendicant* was where I'd left her, in the shadow of the dead tower on the Hulk's dark side, with Teague's breath in her tanks. Wren and I went across to her on the line, and in through the lock, and I sat down in the pilot's seat and she sat down in the co-seat, and I put Haddie's spectacles and Haddie's book on the console between us. Neither of us said anything about them.

I lit the drive.

We came out from behind the Hulk low, the way I'd come in, and I put the old boat's nose toward the Door and pushed the throttles to their stops. Behind us the station fell away against the moon, lit up with Kask's work-lamps, crawling with tenders, its outer arms thick with the grey spawn of eleven thousand pods. Somewhere in there was a holding bay with a grey hatch, and an old woman on the far side of it.

Ahead of us was the Door.

I'd never seen it like this. It had always been a coin — a little round blur of wrong stars, low in the sky, the reason for everything. It wasn't a coin now. It was an eye, and it was opening. The black at its heart had spread until it filled a quarter of the forward glass, and round it the smeared stars were pouring inward in long bright arcs like water down a drain, and at its centre, in the black, something was moving.

"Rook," said Wren.

"I see it."

It was coming through. I couldn't see the shape of it; it was too big, and too black, and my eye kept sliding off it the way it slid off the herald. But I could see the edge. A long curve of darkness, sliding out of the black at the Door's heart into the bent starlight at its rim — slowly, so slowly, like a whale surfacing — and catching, here and there, along that endless curve, the faint far light of Vigil. And beyond the edge, more of it. And more.

"How far?" said Wren.

"Five days at a steady burn. Four if I push her." I looked at the board. "Teague's breath'll do four."

"And when we get there?"

"We find out what's at the Spider," I said, "and we shut it."

She didn't answer. She picked up Haddie's book from the console, carefully, the brittle yellow pages in their oilcloth, and opened it on her knees at the page with the word on it, and looked at it in the light of the board.

"*Gallows,*" she said slowly. Reading it. Not just saying it: reading it, the old square letters, one by one. "*G. A. L. L. O. W. S.*"

"That's it."

"She taught me that one on the second night," said Wren. "At the Hulk. Before we went down to the seats. She said I'd need it." She touched the page with one burned finger. "I didn't know what she meant."

On the aft scope, a chime. A new contact, far behind us, coming off the Hulk.

I looked. It was a big boat — bigger than the reeves' cutters, red and white, with the open hand painted on her flank so large I could see it on the scope at that range. The Margrave's yacht. The *Open Hand*. She'd lit her drive, and turned, and was coming out after us on the same line we were on, straight for the Door. Fast. Faster than the *Mendicant*.

"She knows," said Wren.

"Benno'll have been missed. And the bay." I watched the light grow on the scope. "She knows where we're going. She was going anyway."

"Can she catch us?"

"Before the Door? No. She's faster, but we've got half a day on her." I looked at the forward glass, at the great dark curve still sliding out of the black at the Door's heart, endless, and at the tiny dark speck hanging at the Door's rim — so tiny I'd never have found it if I hadn't known to look — with four long threads hanging down from it. "At the Spider? She'll be there half a day behind us."

"Is that enough?"

"I don't know what's there," I said. "I don't know if it's enough. I don't know if there's anything there at all."

Wren closed Haddie's book and held it against her chest, the way Haddie had.

"Then we'll go and count it," she said.


# Chapter 19: Gallows

*The Bond · 2431*

They hit the cloud tops at nine hours and forty minutes, and Tarrow took them in its hand.

Maren had been inside the giant a thousand times. She had never come into it like this — not sinking, slow and careful, a fat armoured seed in the *Gannet*'s cocoon, but falling, at a speed she did not have a word for, in a tender that had never been built to touch anything thicker than vacuum. The *Old Patience* went into the upper decks nose-first in a sheet of white fire that wrapped the forward glass and turned it into a window onto the inside of a furnace. The whole ship screamed. Not the hull note she knew from the *Gannet*; something higher and worse, a shriek that came from everywhere at once, every plate and rivet and weld in the old tender's frame crying out together. The straps across Maren's chest went tight and then tighter, and the world went red at the edges and then grey.

"Talk to me," said Dov.

He was flying with his teeth bared. She could see them in the furnace-light, his lips pulled back, his bearded jaw clenched so hard that the cords stood out in his neck. His hands on the yoke were moving in tiny precise motions, never more than a finger's width, and his eyes were on the one instrument on the board that was still telling the truth, a little dial that showed the angle of their fall against the giant's curve.

"Six hundred," she said. "Down."

"Lower."

"We're at six hundred, Dov, the hull—"

"The hull'll do what it does. I need eight." His voice was quite calm. "Your mother and I went to seven-eighty. Half-loaded. That's why we only got a day and a half." He moved the yoke. "We're full-loaded with a skimmer in the cradle. I need eight. Read me the decks."

She read him the decks. She had read the giant all her life; she knew what the cloud did, how it thickened, where the chimneys stood and where the sinks fell. She called them out to him as they came up on the scope through the fire — *chimney at your ten, big one, stay off it; shear at seven-twenty, it'll grab your tail; sink under you, it's soft, it's fine, ride it* — and he flew what she said without a word, the way he had flown over her for eighteen years, a thread of laser-light from the top of the world. Seven hundred. Seven-forty. The shriek of the hull dropped into a groan she could feel in her spine. Seven-eighty. Something let go somewhere aft with a bang like a gun, and a red light came on above Dov's head, and he did not look at it.

"Eight," he said. "That's eight. Light the *Gannet*."

"What?"

"Light her! In the cradle! Everything she's got! I need her engines on top of mine, girl, *now*—"

She lit the *Gannet*. She had warmed the skimmer's systems on the run in from Hesper, as he'd told her, and her board was on the co-seat console, and Maren put her hand on it and opened the throttles of her own ship from the cockpit of his, and felt the kick of it through the cradle clamps, through the frame, through her own body — two ships' drives together, at the bottom of the giant's well, pushing.

The *Old Patience* went up out of Tarrow like a stone out of a sling.

* * *

When she could see again, they were in the dark.

The forward glass was crazed in a long star-shaped crack across its lower half. There was a smell of burning in the cockpit, and a smell under it of struck matches, which meant a breath line somewhere had gone. The red light above Dov's head was still on, and three more had joined it. And behind them, on the aft scope, filling it, was the giant — not a sky now but a planet, a vast banded ball of bruise and umber falling away behind them, and on its face, where they had gone in, a long pale scar in the cloud tops like a scratch on skin, already beginning to heal.

Dov was coughing. He was bent over the yoke with his arm across his mouth, and the cough went on and on, a deep wet tearing sound that she had heard in the clinic on Level Nine and in her own lungs on bad nights. When it stopped he sat back slowly in the pilot's seat and took his arm away from his mouth and looked at the sleeve, and then wiped it on his knee.

"Well," he said thickly. "That's done."

"How fast?"

He looked at the board. He did a sum in his head; she watched his lips move. Then he laughed, and it turned into the cough again, and when that was done he said: "Three days and nine hours. Give or take." He looked at her. "Your mother'd have been sick with envy."

"The hull?"

"The hull," said Dov, "has docked its last docking but one. She'll get us there. She'll put us on a collar, if I'm gentle. And after that I'd not take her anywhere I couldn't walk home from." He reached up and turned off the red lights above his head, one after another, with a fingertip. "We'll not need her after that, will we?"

Maren didn't answer.

* * *

The run took three days and nine hours, and it was the longest time she had ever spent with Dov Arkwright without a bottle between them.

He did not drink. There was Ostrander's in the locker; she had seen it. He did not touch it. He flew, and when the flying did not need him he slept in the pilot's seat with his chin on his chest and his breath rattling, and when he woke he flew again. He ate a little. He coughed a great deal. On the second day she saw that what he was coughing into his sleeve was red, and he saw her see it, and neither of them said anything.

They did not talk about the docks. They did not talk about Hatch or Lucan or the chit he had dropped out of the side vent into the dark. She found, somewhere on the first day, that the thing in her chest that had made her hit him three times in the face had not gone away but had gone very quiet and very still, like water at the bottom of a sump, and that she could look at him across the cockpit without it moving. She did not know what that meant. She did not try to find out.

On the second night he talked about her mother.

Not much. Not the story of her birth; she had heard that. Small things. The way Ada Tull had sung the breathers' hymn on the dive line, flat and loud and terrible, and always got the third line wrong. The way she had once brought up a seam so rich the tally-man had refused to believe it and sent the bottles back to be weighed again, three times, and Ada had stood at the rail and watched him do it with her arms folded and said, every time the number came up the same, *count what's owed*. The way she had cut Maren's hair herself with a scoop knife, badly, every month, and said it was because barbers cost marks and was secretly proud of the result.

"She was a terror," said Dov. "You're worse."

"I'm not."

"You're worse." He looked at the forward glass, at the long crack across it, and at the dark beyond. "She'd never have done this. She'd have wanted to. She'd have sat here and done every sum and known it came out. And then she'd have turned round and gone home and dived the next morning, because that's what you did." He was quiet a moment. "She never once in her life did a thing she couldn't take back."

"And I am?"

"You're going to shut a door on thirty thousand people, girl," said Dov gently, "that nobody will be able to open for a very long time. I'd call that a thing you can't take back." He turned his head and looked at her. "I'm not saying don't. I'm saying I know what it costs. I watched your face when you said it to me, that night in this seat. I know you've done the sum."

"It's not a sum."

"No?"

"No." She looked at her hands. Her right was strapped, the two broken knuckles swollen and black under the binding. In her left, where it had been for three days, was the key. "A sum's when you weigh one thing against another and see which is heavier. This isn't that." She closed her hand on it. "This is just what's owed."

Dov looked at her.

"Your mother said that," he said at last. "To me. The night before she died, in the clinic, when I came to see her. She couldn't talk much by then. But she held my hand and she said, *Dov. Count what's owed.* And I said what you say. *And not a mark more.* And she shook her head at me, and said it again. *Count what's owed.* And then she went to sleep." He looked away. "I never knew what she meant. Not till that night you said it to me in this seat, with your face like hers. *Her. Not the bond. Her.*" He wiped his eyes with his sleeve, the clean one. "She meant you, girl. She was asking me to look after you. And I sold you for a seat."

The *Patience* hummed round them.

"You're flying me now," said Maren.

"I'm flying you to die." He said it without self-pity. "I can count too."

She didn't answer that. There wasn't anything to say. After a while, Dov went to sleep, with his chin on his chest and his breath rattling, and she sat in the co-seat and watched the dark ahead, where the Throat was, and held the key.

* * *

They saw it on the third day.

It came up out of the dark ahead like a moon rising: a great round place in the sky where the stars were wrong. Not smeared — not yet, not at this distance — but different. A disc of other stars, brighter and closer together and a different colour, a faint blue-white, set into the dull red-lit black of Vigil's sky like a coin in a wall. And as they came closer, over the hours, it grew; and the stars round its rim began to bend, to curve inward toward it in long arcs, like the grain round a knot in wood.

"There it is," said Dov softly. "The Throat."

She had seen it all her life on feeds — a small bright circle, the colony's one door, the place where the convoys came from and went to. She had never seen it like this, with her own eyes, growing in a cracked window. She had never seen what was through it.

Light. That was what was through it. Not much — a glow, faint and blue, the light of a star that was not Vigil, coming through the Throat from wherever the other mouth opened, so that the disc of other stars was faintly lit from behind, like a window at night. It was the colour, she thought, of the sky in the pictures Matron Brask had shown the wards. The colour Pell had been promised.

And at its rim, small and dark against that blue — so small she could cover it with her thumb on the glass — hung Gallows.

It was a spine. A long dark bar of station laid along the Throat's edge, lit with a few small working lights. And from it, at four points round the Throat's rim, on tethers so long and so fine she could only see them where they caught the blue, hung the pins: four great cylinders, each the size of a refinery head, quite still, holding the door of the world open.

"The Hanged Men," said Dov. "That's what the tender crews used to call them. When I was a lad. Before the Combine stopped letting us fly out this far." He looked at the scope. "And there's the hangman."

She saw it. A sharp bright point near Gallows, off its far end, where the spine's docking ring was. A ship. Grey, lean, with the H-V crest on its flank and its gun ports open.

"*Indemnity,*" said Dov. "She's been here a day, by the look of her. She'll have seen us since breakfast." He reached for the band switch. "Time I did some talking."

* * *

He talked for an hour. Maren sat beside him and listened and did not say a word, and she understood, listening, for the first time in her life, what Dov Arkwright had been before the bottle.

"*Indemnity, Indemnity, this is the contract tender* Old Patience *out of Number Four head, Hesper. I've got your thief.*" His voice was rough and cheerful and slightly drunk. "*The bond who did the Factor in his own study. Tull, H-one-one-eight-seven-three. Breather. I've got her trussed in my cradle like a Feast Day bird and I've got the thing she took off him. I want my reward and I want my seat. Over.*"

There was a long pause on the band. Then a clipped voice, a man's, cold.

"*Old Patience, this is Captain Rennick, Indemnity. Identify your cargo.*"

"*I just did, Captain. Bond Tull and the Factor's key. She came to me in the ice two nights back wanting a boat — wanted me to take her out here to the Throat. God knows why. Mad as a sink.*" A laugh. "*I took her money and her boat and her word and then I put her in my cradle with a scoop line round her and turned for home. And then I thought, Arkwright, the* Grace*'s going out past Gallows. The Factor'll want his key back before he goes, won't he? And he'll want to say thank you to the man who brings it him.*" His voice dropped, sly. "*I had a seat promised me once, Captain. Forty-one, Deck Two. Somebody on your dock said I couldn't have it. I'd like to talk to the Factor about that.*"

Another pause. Longer.

"*Hold your position, Old Patience,*" said Rennick. "*I'm referring this to the* Grace."

"*You do that, Captain. I'll put the kettle on.*"

He clicked off the band and sat back. His hands, Maren saw, were shaking very slightly on the arms of the pilot's seat. His face was grey.

"He'll ask Castellane," she said.

"He'll ask Castellane." Dov wiped his mouth. "And the *Grace* is — what — eighteen hours behind us now? So the message goes out, and the Factor reads it, and the answer comes back. Light lag's a few minutes at this range. Say a quarter of an hour, with the thinking."

"And Castellane will say what?"

Dov looked at her.

"He'll say yes," he said. "Won't he? You've seen him. He wants that key more than he wants anything in the world. He can't close the door behind him without it. He's going to come past Gallows in eighteen hours with four hundred kids in his hold and every officer in the colony, and he's going to want to stop and put that key in the lock and set the timer and go through and *shut it*. Close the account. That's the whole of him." He laughed weakly. "He'll say *yes, Captain, let the drunk dock at Gallows and hold them there for me, and I'll collect it myself when I pass*. Because he doesn't think we can add."

The band crackled, eighteen minutes later.

"*Old Patience, Indemnity.*" Rennick's voice was even colder. "*You're cleared to dock at Gallows, ring two. Hold your prisoner and the item aboard your vessel. Do not enter the station. A detail from* Indemnity *will take custody. The Factor will collect the item on his passage. Acknowledge.*"

"*Acknowledged, Captain,*" said Dov happily. "*Very kind. Ring two. I'll be gentle.*"

He clicked off the band. He looked at Maren.

"How long," she said, "for his detail to cross?"

"From where she's sitting? Suited, on a line, with a squad?" He looked at the scope. "Forty minutes. If they're quick. Thirty if they're frightened of him."

"And the inversion?"

"I don't know," said Dov. "Your clerk said ten minutes, once it starts. I don't know how long it takes to start." He reached forward and took hold of the yoke. "Best not to dawdle."

* * *

He put the *Old Patience* on Gallows' ring two so gently that she did not feel the collar take. He had said he would be gentle, and he was. It was, she thought afterward, the most beautiful piece of flying she had ever seen, and nobody but her would ever know it had been done: an old drunk with blood on his sleeve and a cracked hull and three red lights burning over his head, kissing a dying tender onto a docking ring at the edge of the world with the Combine's guns watching him do it.

When the seals took he let go of the yoke and sat back.

"Go on," he said.

She was already out of the seat. She had her suit on, her breather's suit, with the helmet clipped to her belt and the key in the sealed thigh pocket where she had always kept her ration chit, and her half of the steel disc on its cord against her skin under all of it. She stopped in the cockpit door and looked back at him.

"Come with me."

"Can't." He was coughing again, quietly. "Somebody's got to talk to the captain. Tell him you're still trussed in the cradle. Tell him I'm making tea. Buy you a bit of time." He looked up at her. "Go on, girl. You've got forty minutes and a station to find your way round."

She did not move.

"*Go,*" said Dov Arkwright, the way Lucan had said it, and she went.

* * *

Gallows was empty.

She had not known what to expect. A crew, maybe, who would have to be fought; marines; Combine officers at their consoles. There was no one. The station had been stripped. She came through the docking ring into a long corridor that ran down the spine, lit only by the working lights, and it was bare — no crates, no gear, no signs of life — and she understood after a moment that the station's crew had already gone. They would have gone through ahead with the *Surety*, the pathfinder escort that had left Hesper two days before the *Grace*. Lucan had told her: *six of them, on the list for Schedule A.* Gallows had been left running on its own, holding the Throat open for the convoy's passage, with nobody aboard at all.

She ran. Down the spine, past doors standing open on empty rooms, toward the centre of the station where the master console would be. Lucan had drawn it for her, once, on the back of a tally slip in the caretakers' storeroom, from a plan he had seen in the Factor's office: *centre of the spine, a round room, a window onto the mouth.*

The round room was there. Its door stood open. She went in, and stopped.

The window filled one whole wall of it, floor to ceiling, curved, and through it, filling it, was the Throat.

She had seen it from the *Patience*. That was nothing. From here, at its rim, it was not a disc in the sky; it was a hole in the world, and it was full of light. Blue-white, soft, the light of another star — so close, it seemed, that she could have put her hand out through the glass and into it. She could see, through the Throat's mouth, a sky. Not stars: a sky. A great curved glowing reach of pale blue haze, the edge of some world on the far side lit by its sun, so bright after a whole life under Vigil's dull red that it made her eyes water. And round its rim the stars of Vigil's sky bent and curved inward in long arcs, and the four Hanged Men hung motionless on their threads, holding it open.

A sky. A blue one. For a moment she could not move at all.

Then she made herself turn from it, to the console.

It was a long desk of grey metal under the window, with dark screens set in it and banks of switches, and in the middle of it, under a hinged guard like the guard on the *Gannet*'s dump lever, a lock: a small round socket with a spiral groove cut into its inner wall. She opened the guard. She took the key out of her thigh pocket with her good hand and fitted its point into the socket, and felt the spiral catch, and turned it.

It went in like a breath going into a lung. All the way, smooth, until the ceramic sat flush with the steel of the console. And the console woke.

The screens lit, one after another, green and amber. Lettering came up on them in the Combine's square hand, lines of it, status and readings she did not understand. And then, on the central screen, a single panel, very plain.

> PASSAGE CONTROL — FACTOR AUTHORITY ACCEPTED
>
> PIN STATE: OPEN (HOLD)
>
> SELECT:
> HOLD · INVERT · RESTORE

She put her finger on INVERT.

The screen changed.

> INVERSION SEQUENCE REQUESTED.
> ALL PINS WILL REVERSE SHEAR. PASSAGE WILL COLLAPSE.
> ESTIMATED TIME TO COLLAPSE: 9 MIN 40 SEC FROM CONFIRMATION.
> COLLAPSE IS NOT REVERSIBLE BY HOLD.
> RESTORATION FROM COLLAPSED STATE REQUIRES PRIMING (HEAVY BREATH, MIN. 4 UNITS) AND FACTOR AUTHORITY.
> ESTIMATED RESTORATION TO PASSAGE: 380–420 STANDARD YEARS AT MINIMUM SHEAR.
>
> CONFIRM?

She read it twice. Three hundred and eighty years. Four hundred. She read it a third time, and then she stopped reading it, because she knew what it said, and because she had known since the latrine at the tally house what it would say.

She looked out through the window at the blue sky beyond the Throat.

Somewhere behind her — eighteen hours behind her now, sixteen, fifteen — a grey ship a kilometre and a quarter long was coming across the system with four hundred and twelve children in its hold. One of them was in the ward deck. Deck Nine, forward. One of them could count.

She pressed CONFIRM.

* * *

Nothing happened, at first. She had thought there would be a sound. There was only a change in the light on the screens — green going to amber, a number appearing in the corner of the central panel and beginning to fall. *9:39. 9:38.* And through the window, very faintly, the four Hanged Men stirring on their threads, the way a sleeper stirs.

She looked at the falling number. Then she looked along the console, and found what Lucan had said would be there — *every station keeps a log, every console has a recorder* — a small panel to the left of the lock, with a grille and a single switch. She did not know if anyone would ever hear it. She did not know if it would last. She thumbed the switch, and a little light came on beside the grille, red, and she leaned down to it with her helmet in her hand and her broken hand against the console's edge, and talked to her daughter.

"Pell. It's Mum."

*9:12.*

"I don't know if this'll ever reach you. I don't know if anyone will ever come here again. I hope they do. I hope it's you." She stopped. Started again. "I said I'd tell you. You remember? At the glass. I said I'd find out who was giving what to who, and I'd tell you, even if it was bad. So this is me telling you.

"You know about Brannock. You know about the Scholarship. You worked it out before I did, you clever thing. All four hundred and twelve of you, and the price of every one. What you don't know is what they were going to do after. When the *Grace* went through, they were going to stop here, at this station, and turn this key, and shut the Throat behind them so the rest of us couldn't ever follow. Thirty thousand people. They were going to shut us in the ice and forget us."

*8:21.*

"So I've shut it first. That's what I've done. I've shut it in front of them instead of behind. I'm sitting here at the console and the number's going down and in about eight minutes it'll be shut and the *Grace* will be on our side of it. On *your* side. With you on it, and the other children, and the Factor and all his officers. They're not going to Corvid. Nobody's going to Brannock. Nobody's going anywhere." She swallowed. "You're staying with us.

"I'm sorry about the sky. I can see it, Pell. Through the window. It's blue. It's — it's blue, love. Like the pictures. I'm sorry. I'd have given you that if I could. I couldn't give you that and keep you, and I chose keep."

*7:05.*

"They'll tell you things about me. After. The Factor will, and his people. They'll say I was mad, or wicked, or that I did it out of spite, or for the union. Some of it'll be true. I hit the Factor with a knife handle and I'd do it again. But the rest, you'll know. You can count. Count what's owed, love. Count it right. Not their way — not the bond, not the marks. Count *you*." Her voice did something and she let it. "My mum said that, the day I was born. Dov told me. *Look at her. Count what's owed.* I didn't know what she meant until I had you.

"I've got my half of the tag. I'm holding it now. You hold yours. One day somebody'll put them together."

*5:40.*

Through the window, the Hanged Men had begun to move. Not stirring now — swinging, slowly, on their long threads, out from the Throat's rim and back, out and back, like pendulums. And they were glowing. A faint deep red at first, like the cracks in a cooling forge, and then brighter.

And on the band, faint, from the *Patience* at ring two, she heard Dov's voice.

"*—no, Captain, she's still in the cradle, I've just checked, I don't know what your instruments are telling you but I'm sitting here with a cup of tea and—*"

And Rennick's, cutting across it, very cold and very loud. "*Arkwright, the pins are inverting. The pins are inverting. What has she done? Arkwright—*"

Maren leaned back to the grille.

"Whoever hears this," she said. "If it isn't her. If it's somebody else, a long time after. The console says it can be opened again. RESTORE. It says it needs breath and the key and three or four hundred years." She took a breath. "So if you've come here, maybe you've got three or four hundred years. Maybe everybody's dead who'd sell a child. I don't know. I don't know what you are. But if you are — if there's nobody left on either side of it who'd do that — then open it. Let them go and see the sky." She closed her eyes. "And if there is, don't. Not till there isn't. Don't let them take the children."

*3:10.*

"*Arkwright, you lying bastard—*"

"*Ah, Captain,*" said Dov's voice, mild and rough, through the band, "*you've found me out. Well. I always was a poor liar. Ask anybody.*" A cough, long and wet. "*Tell you what, while we've a minute. Do you know the breathers' hymn? You'll not have heard it, on a frigate. Goes like this.*"

And he began to sing.

*Down in the red where the weight comes on—*

He sang it flat and loud and terrible, the way her mother had, the way Hatch had on the docks, and he got the third line wrong, the way Ada always had, on purpose, she thought, and she stood at the console with her hand on its edge and listened.

*—down where the light's a bruise—*

She felt it before she heard it. A jolt through the deck, through her boots, through the console under her hand: a deep hard shudder that ran down the whole length of the spine. And then the sound, coming down the corridor from the docking ring, a long rolling boom like the head's intakes starting, and then a scream of air.

The band went silent.

Not crackle. Not static. Silent, the way a held breath stops.

*2:00.*

She did not go to look. She knew. She stood at the console in her suit with her helmet in her hand and the alarms beginning to sound all through the station, and the air in the round room beginning to move — a wind, a pull, toward the door, toward the corridor, toward whatever the *Indemnity*'s guns had opened in the spine where ring two had been — and she put her helmet on and sealed it, one-handed, the way she had sealed it ten thousand times in the *Gannet*'s cradle, and turned back to the window.

The Hanged Men were burning.

All four. White now, white-hot, swinging on their threads in great slow arcs, and the Throat between them was changing. The blue sky through its mouth was still there, still glowing, but the mouth was getting smaller. Closing. Not like a door. Like an eye. The bent stars round its rim were pouring inward now, faster and faster, the long arcs tightening into spirals, and the blue at the centre shrank, and shrank, a disc and then a coin and then a bright blue bead.

*0:40.*

On the scope beside the window, far off down the system, a light. The *Grace*. Too far to see. Fifteen hours out. Coming on, braking already for Gallows, for the key she thought was waiting for her. On this side.

*0:10.*

The blue bead flickered.

And went out.

* * *

It was very quiet, afterward.

The Hanged Men stopped burning. She watched them through the window over a long time — she did not know how long — go from white to red to a dull deep glow, and then to dark, and hang still on their threads again, round the rim of a place in the sky where there was nothing at all. Not black. Not stars. Nothing. Her eye would not stay on it.

The station's air was gone. She knew that from her suit's board, and from the silence, and from the frost that had begun to form on the inside of the window in long feathered lines. The breach at ring two had taken it all, down the spine, out into the dark. She had her suit's air. A breather's suit carried eight hours for a dive and an hour's reserve. She had come aboard with — she looked — six hours and twenty minutes.

The marines did not come. She had thought they would. She stood at the console for a long time waiting for them, with her helmet sealed and her good hand on the key, ready to do — she did not know what. They did not come. After a while the band crackled, faintly, through her helmet speaker, on the open channel, and she heard Rennick's voice, flat and dead.

"*Indemnity to* Grace. *The passage is closed. I say again, the passage is closed. Collapse confirmed at—*" a time "*—by inversion from Gallows master console. The* Surety *had cleared. The* Grace *is—*" A pause. "*The* Grace *is on this side, sir.*"

Silence. Then, faint, much fainter, with the few minutes' lag of distance, another voice. Old and grave and very quiet. She knew it.

"*Understood, Captain.*" A long pause. "*Recall your detail. Rejoin the convoy. Leave the station.*"

"*Sir, the bond—*"

"*Leave her,*" said Imre Castellane. "*Leave her to it. No one is to go to Gallows. Do you understand me, Captain? No one. Not now. Not ever.*" Another pause, longer. When he spoke again his voice had changed; it was the voice of a man doing a sum, very carefully, that had just come out in a way he had never once imagined it could. "*It's closed.*"

The band clicked off.

* * *

She sat down, after that, because her legs would not hold her any more. There was a chair at the console, a plain metal chair with a straight back, and she sat in it with her back to the room and her face to the window and the dead dark place in the sky where the blue had been.

On the scope, very small, very far, the light of the *Grace* had stopped braking. As she watched — it took a long time; it took, she thought, an hour, two — it began to turn. Slowly, the way a great ship turns, a kilometre and a quarter of grey hull swinging its nose round in the dark. Away from Gallows. Away from the closed Throat. Back across the system, toward Tarrow, toward Hesper, toward the ice.

Home.

She watched it all the way round.

Then she reached over and thumbed the log switch again, and the little red light came on beside the grille.

"It's turning, Pell," she said. "The *Grace*. I can see it. It's turning round. You're going home."

She did not say anything else for a while. She sat and watched the light on the scope. Her suit's board said four hours. Three and a half. Her broken hand had stopped hurting, or she had stopped noticing. The frost on the window had grown into a forest of white ferns, and through it the dark place where the Throat had been hung in the sky like a closed eye, and round it the Hanged Men hung on their threads, quite still.

She reached into the neck of her suit, awkwardly, with her good hand, and found the cord, and drew it out: her half of the steel disc, H-11873, SURETY, with its broken toothed edge. She held it up in front of the window, against the dark, the way she had held it up to the Nursery glass once a month for six years, and looked at the jagged line where the other half should fit.

"Count what's owed," she said, to nobody.

Her daughter's voice answered her, at the glass, a long way off and six years ago. *And not a mark more.*

She let the tag fall into her lap, still in her good hand, on its cord. And she lifted the other — the broken one, strapped and swollen in its glove, that had stopped hurting — and laid it flat on the console, beside the key, beside the little red light of the log, on the cold grey metal under the frosted window.

And knocked three times.


# Chapter 20: The Seal

*The Ledger · 2794*

The corridor ran both ways from the docking ring, along the spine, into the dark. Ysolde stood in the frost with her lamp up and looked left and then right, and then, because she had to choose and there was nothing to choose by, she went right, toward the station's centre, because that was where Corin had said the docking ring was nearest to, and a station's heart, she supposed, would be in its middle.

It was very cold. Corin's suit was too big for her and the cold came in at the joints, at the wrists and the knees, and her breath fogged the inside of the visor at the edges in a ring of white. The frost on the walls was thick and soft and feathered, like the frost on the inside of the novice dormitory's window on her first night in the Nave, and where her lamp touched it it glittered. There was nothing in the corridor. Doors stood open on bare rooms. There were no bodies, no wreckage, nothing to say what had been done here — only the frost, and the dark, and the cold, and far down the spine behind her, in the direction she had not taken, something she glimpsed once when she turned her lamp that way: a ragged place where the corridor ended in torn black metal and beyond it, nothing. Stars. An old wound, open to the dark.

She turned away from it and went on.

The corridor ended in a round room.

She knew it was the place before she had properly seen it. The door stood open. The lamp found a curved wall, and in the curved wall a window — huge, floor to ceiling, so thick with frost that it was white, a great pale screen of feathered ice — and through the frost, faint and grey, a light that was not light: the Door. Its bent arcs of stars. Its nothing at the centre. And under the window, a long desk of grey metal with dark screens set into it. And at the desk, in a chair, with its back to her and its face to the window, someone sitting.

She stopped in the doorway.

The figure did not move. Of course it did not move. It was in a suit — a heavy, old-fashioned suit, much patched, of a kind she had seen only in the oldest diving families' cells in the Lungs, hung on walls as relics — and a helmet, and it sat in the plain metal chair quite straight, with one gloved hand flat on the console in front of it and the other in its lap. Frost had grown over it. Over the shoulders and the helmet and the arm on the console, the same feathered white as the walls, so that it seemed at first to be made of frost — a figure of ice someone had carved and set at the desk and left.

She went to it. She did not know how. Her feet took her across the round room the way they had taken her to Piet's door on Spoke Twelve.

She stood beside the chair and held up her lamp and looked through the visor.

It was a woman. The cold and the dark had kept her. Her face was thin and dark and drawn, the skin gone tight over the bones, but it was a face — a woman of perhaps forty, with coarse black hair cut short and badly, and a long jaw, and eyes that were closed. Her mouth was a little open, as though she had been about to say something. There were lines on her face that Ysolde had seen on the faces of every diver in the Lungs: round the eyes, from squinting into the red; at the corners of the mouth, from clenching the teeth against the weight. On the breast of the suit, half under the frost, stencilled in faded white, letters and a number. Ysolde brushed the frost away with her glove.

TULL — H-11873

She was a diver.

Ysolde knew it the way she would have known her own brother's hands. A diver from the Lungs, three hundred and forty years ago, in her diving suit, sitting in this chair at the edge of the world in front of a frosted window. Her right hand, the one on the console, was strapped under the glove — Ysolde could see the bulk of a binding, a splint, as if it had been broken. It lay flat on the grey metal beside a small round lock, and in the lock, set flush, was something white and steel: a spindle, the length of a finger, with a spiral groove.

Her other hand lay in her lap. Something was in it. A cord, and on the cord, half under the frost of the glove that held it, a piece of dull steel.

Ysolde knelt. She brushed the frost away. She did not need to lift it to see what it was; she had seen it every day of her childhood at her father's throat and every day since at Piet's, and three nights ago in the dark of the Reliquary hanging from the arm of a plain metal chair. A half-disc of steel, the size of a palm, with a broken toothed edge.

But this one had not been worn smooth. Its stampings were still there. Sharp and clean, as clean as the day they were cut.

H-11873. SURETY.

* * *

The console was dead. She had thought it would be. Three hundred and forty years in the cold and the dark; there was nothing in it to wake. She went back down the frosted corridor to the pup and found the cable Corin had shown her, the umbilical that the jolly-boat used to feed power to the far relays when their own plants failed, and dragged it up the spine behind her, metre after metre, in the too-big suit, with her breath roaring in the helmet. It reached. Just. She found a socket in the console's flank by touch, the old square Combine shape, and fitted the cable's end into it, and went back to the pup and opened its plant to the line.

When she came back the screens were alight.

Not all of them. Three, of nine. Green and amber, flickering, with lines of the old square hand scrolling across them faster than she could read. And then they steadied, and on the central screen a single plain panel came up, and held.

> PASSAGE CONTROL — FACTOR AUTHORITY PRESENT
>
> PIN STATE: COLLAPSED (INVERTED)
>
> SELECT:
> HOLD · INVERT · RESTORE
>
> LOG: 1 RECORDING

She stood in front of it. She read the words she knew and guessed the ones she did not. *Pin.* That was in the oldest volumes of the Ninth, she remembered now — a charge, once, at the very beginning, before the Starving, for something called *pin operations*. *Collapsed.* *Inverted.* And *passage*, which was on the first page of the First Book: *Consolidated Statement at Closure of Passage.*

The Door. This was the Door. This desk, this frosted window, this lock with its white-and-steel spindle. Whatever had been done to the Door, it had been done from here, by someone sitting in this chair.

She looked at the woman in the chair.

Then she put her finger on the last line of the panel. LOG.

* * *

The voice was very close. That was the first thing. It came from a small grille in the console to the left of the lock, and the grille was so near the woman's helmet that for a moment Ysolde, kneeling there with her lamp, had the terrible conviction that it was coming *from* her — that the dead mouth behind the frosted visor had opened and spoken.

"*Pell,*" said the voice. "*It's Mum.*"

It was a woman's voice, low and rough. It spoke the old tongue — the tongue of the First Book, of the oldest volumes of the Ninth, of the Compact — but spoken, not written, and Ysolde had only ever heard it read aloud by Chancery elders who had learned it from books and pronounced it as they thought it should be pronounced. This was not that. This was someone for whom it was simply speech. The vowels were wrong, flattened; the words ran together; it was like hearing the catechism sung in an accent from a spoke she had never visited. But she understood it. She had read every page of the First Book's tongue she could find for twenty-two years. She understood every word.

She knelt on the frosted floor beside the dead woman's chair, and listened.

She heard about the Scholarship. She heard about a place called Brannock, and galleries in a heavy world, and children whose bones were soft enough to take the weight. She heard the voice say: *When the* Grace *went through, they were going to stop here, at this station, and turn this key, and shut the Throat behind them so the rest of us couldn't ever follow. Thirty thousand people.*

Thirty thousand. *There were thirty thousand people in the ice of Hesper and twelve Stewards in the Nave.*

She heard: *So I've shut it first.*

Ysolde put her hand over her visor, as if to cover her mouth, and it was not there.

*I've shut it in front of them instead of behind. In about eight minutes it'll be shut and the* Grace *will be on our side of it. On your side. With you on it, and the other children, and the Factor and all his officers. They're not going to Corvid. Nobody's going anywhere. You're staying with us.*

She heard the voice say *I'm sorry about the sky,* and say that it was blue, and stop, and say it again.

She heard it say: *They'll tell you things about me. After. The Factor will, and his people.*

She heard it say: *Count what's owed, love. Count it right.*

She heard it talk about a tag. *I've got my half. You hold yours. One day somebody'll put them together.*

She heard a man's voice, very faint, through some other channel — singing, flat and loud and terrible, a song she knew. The divers' hymn. *Down in the red where the weight comes on.* She had sung it in the Lungs as a child. She had not known it was so old. She heard him get the third line wrong. And she heard a great deep boom, and a scream of air, and then the singing stop.

She heard the voice say: *Whoever hears this. If it isn't her.*

And then, a long time later — the recorder had kept running; she heard the silence, the faint hiss of it, minute after minute — she heard the voice again, quieter, closer, as though the woman had leaned down to the grille with her helmet on: *It's turning, Pell. The* Grace. *I can see it. It's turning round. You're going home.*

And then nothing for a long time. And then, very small and clear, so close to the grille that the sound of it filled the round room, three knocks. A gloved hand, on metal.

Then silence, for as long as the recorder had run.

* * *

Ysolde did not know how long she knelt there.

She was thinking about the Reliquary. She could not stop. She was thinking about the rows of seats with their rotted padding and their straps, all facing one way. The dead consoles. The round corridors with their hand-grips on the ceiling, for walking on the walls. The hatches in the floor with wheel locks. *What the Nave had been before it was the Nave: the Stewards' station, the old Combine hub.*

Not a station. A ship.

The *Grace*. *It's turning round. You're going home.* A ship a voice had called the *Grace*, turning back from a shut Door with every officer of the Combine aboard it, and the Factor, and children — *you, and the other children* — and coming home to Hesper, to the ice, where thirty thousand people were waiting. And then the twelve people on that ship, the Factor and his officers, who had been on their way out through the Door with the children to sell and had found it shut in their faces by a diver from the Sump: what had they done? They had brought the ship home. They had grafted it into the station above the ice. They had sat down together in its hold, in the first year, with no hope of relief, and written: *The Door is closed, and shall be taught as closed against them.* Against *them*. For the debt.

*No soul shall go to Gallows. What was done at Gallows is accounted and closed.*

What was done at Gallows was this. A woman in a diving suit had sat in this chair and shut the Door on the Combine to keep her daughter, and four hundred others, and the Stewards had never once in three hundred and forty years let anyone know that they had not chosen to stay. That they had been caught. That the Door they taught the Lungs to bow to, the Door the Count would open when it was zero, had been shut not by any creditor's judgment but by one of the owed, against them, in the act of their running away.

And the children. *One of a number of children who came into the Stewards' care in the first years.* Quist's voice, over the beans, with the giant at her back. *She learned her numbers at the Stewards' knees.*

Ysolde looked at the steel half-disc in the dead woman's lap. H-11873. SURETY. Its broken toothed edge.

She thought of the plain metal chair at the far end of the Reliquary, on its platform where something larger had once been bolted, and the half-disc hanging from its arm on a cord, kissed smooth.

She did not know. She could not know. There had been four hundred children. There were tags at every diver's throat in the Lungs, every one of them broken along a jagged line, every one of them half of something, and no one had ever known what. But she knelt there on the frosted floor of the round room at Gallows and looked at the dead diver's tag, and at the dead diver's face, the long jaw and the coarse black hair cut short and badly; and she thought of the words cut into the vault above the Count Board, which the First Auditor had written at the head of the first page of the first Count, in the Starving, when she was about Ysolde's age.

COUNT WHAT IS OWED AND NOT A MARK MORE.

*Count what's owed, love. Count it right.*

She found that she was weeping, inside the helmet, where she could not wipe it away. She had not wept since her seventh night in the novice dormitory. She knelt beside the dead woman's chair in the too-big suit and wept, because it did not balance, and because there was no one in the whole of the dark to see her do it.

* * *

It was the console that brought her back. A change in the light. She looked up, and the central panel had dimmed — the pup's plant feeding it was not strong — and in the dimness the three words stood out more clearly than before.

HOLD · INVERT · RESTORE

She got up. She stood in front of the console with the lamp hooked over her wrist and looked at them.

*The console says it can be opened again. RESTORE. It says it needs breath and the key and three or four hundred years.*

She put her finger on RESTORE.

> RESTORATION SEQUENCE REQUESTED.
> PINS WILL RESUME FORWARD SHEAR AT MINIMUM. PASSAGE WILL REFORM.
> PRIMING REQUIRED: HEAVY BREATH, MIN. 4 UNITS, AT PIN FEED.
> ESTIMATED RESTORATION TO PASSAGE: 380–420 STANDARD YEARS AT MINIMUM SHEAR.
>
> CONFIRM?

Four hundred years.

She stood and looked at it. She thought, because she could not help it, of the Prelate's sum. A hundred and ninety thousand people. The breath that kept them warm. The Count that held them together. *We'd be back in the Starving inside a year.* She thought of Corin's answer: *Your Prelate's sum has a wrong figure in it. He's counted us as cattle that only walk while you whip them.* She thought of Bett and Hollis and Grandad in the back room, passing the Compact from hand to hand as though it were hot.

And she thought of the dead woman's voice. *If there's nobody left on either side of it who'd do that — then open it. And if there is, don't. Not till there isn't.*

There was. Of course there was. There was the Nave, which took children from the Lungs at nine; there were the divers, sent down at fourteen to pay a debt that did not exist. There was a Prelate who had had a rail raised. She could not open the Door tonight. She could not open it for Piet, or for Bett, or for any child now alive in the Lungs. Nobody could. *Four hundred years.*

But she could begin it.

She could set it going here, tonight, in the dark, where no one would know, and leave it to breathe. And in four hundred years, when it opened — when whatever was on the far side of it came through, as the Compact had feared it might, *if Recovery should ever come* — then whoever was alive in the Lungs then would have the choice that the dead woman in this chair had made for them. Whether to go through. Whether to shut it again. Whether there was anyone left who would sell a child. It would be theirs. Not the Stewards'. Not the Count's.

And if Recovery came, and came to collect—

She looked at her satchel, where it lay on the frosted floor beside the dead woman's chair, with her audit in it.

*So that if Recovery should ever come, it will find the books in order.*

"Let it," said Ysolde Marr, aloud, in the dead air of the round room. "Let it find them."

* * *

The pin feed was on the console's far side, a row of four valves under a hinged plate, each with the old Combine socket for a full-draw bottle. It took her two hours to bring the bottles up from the pup, one at a time, in her arms, along the frosted spine. Six of them. Corin's breath, smuggled up out of the old ice docks under the Drowned Decks for twelve years, to the far relays where a few families had never left. She fitted four of them to the valves. She kept two back; she did not know why.

Then she went to the console and pressed CONFIRM.

> RESTORATION SEQUENCE INITIATED.
> PRIMING... ACCEPTED.
> PINS RESUMING FORWARD SHEAR AT MINIMUM.

Through the frosted window, so faint that she would not have seen it if she had not been looking, the four Hanged Men stirred. A glow, deep in each of them. Deep red, like the last coal in a stove. It did not brighten. It held. And she felt, through the deck under her boots, a hum, very low, so low that it was more a pressure than a sound, as though something enormous a very long way off had begun, very slowly, to breathe.

* * *

She found the account at the fourth hour, when she had stopped expecting to.

She had been searching the console's screens, one after another, in the dim light of the pup's failing plant, for anything that looked like a ledger. She had not known if there would be one. But the oldest volumes of the Ninth had charged *pin operations* to the Holy Number, every month, at the very beginning; someone, somewhere, had kept that account. And on the seventh screen, under a heading she could just read — ACCOUNT INTERFACE — there it was.

HV-VIGIL-0001.

The same number. The same forms. She would have known them anywhere; she had kept them for twenty-two years. Assets. Liabilities. A ledger, in the Combine's own machine, at the edge of the world. And at its foot, among the functions she could guess at, one she did not have to guess at at all, because it was the first thing every novice in the Third Chancery was taught to find on a closing sheet.

STATEMENT OF RECONCILIATION — ENTER.

She sat down on the frosted floor beside the dead woman's chair, with her audit on her knees and the lamp hooked over her wrist and the console's little keyboard pulled down to her on its cable, and began to enter it.

It took her the rest of the night. Three hundred and forty-one lines. The Founding, as the First Book had printed it: a hundred and eighteen million, four hundred thousand. The Founding Terms. Every year's tithe, every year's charge, every year's balance carried. *Per Ferrow,* where she could not check it. At the two hundred and eightieth year, the balance: nil. And then the sixty-one years after, credit on credit on credit, to the foot of the column, and the total of them all, and the one word: *Overpaid.*

And in the line for the three hundred and twenty-sixth year, in the field the machine provided for a keeper's note, she typed slowly, letter by letter, in the old square hand: *Tamsin Asch, aged fourteen.*

And at the very end, when the last line was in, the console thought, and then put up a single line of its own at the foot of the statement, in amber.

> AUTHORITY SEAL REQUIRED. PRESENT FACTOR SEAL.

She looked at it. She looked at the key in the lock, white and steel, beside the dead woman's strapped hand. *Factor authority present.* The key opened the Door. It did not, apparently, close an account. For that, the machine wanted a seal.

*Seal of the Prelate-Auditor.*

She laughed, as she had laughed in the *Small Mercy*'s cabin, quite quietly, until it hurt.

* * *

They came at the ninth hour of the next day.

She heard them before she saw them — a jolt through the deck, a long way down the spine, the shudder of a hull meeting a docking ring that had not been built for it. And then, a long time after, lamps. Coming up the frosted corridor toward the round room. Four of them, swinging. Boots on the deck, slow, careful, the way men walk in a dead place.

She did not hide. There was nowhere to hide, and she found she did not want to. She stood up from the floor beside the dead woman's chair and stood in front of the console with her hands at her sides, and waited.

Strand came first. His suit was scorched down one side and his visor was cracked in a long line across its corner, and behind it his pale eyes found her in the lamplight and held. Two Bailiffs behind him, young men, in the black suits of the Prelate's household. And behind them, slowly, leaning on the arm of the last Bailiff, in a white suit too big for him with a black cane in his other hand, the Prelate-Auditor.

He stopped in the doorway of the round room. He looked at Ysolde. He looked at the console, at the lit screens, at the four bottles on the pin feed.

And then he looked at the woman in the chair.

"Oh," said Aurel Quist, very softly, through his suit's speaker. "Oh, my dear. What have you found?"

* * *

"Prelate," said Strand. "Stand back from her, please. Sister Marr. Step away from the console."

"No," said Ysolde.

"Sister—"

"Captain." Quist did not raise his voice. He had not taken his eyes off the woman in the chair. "Wait." He came forward, slowly, past Strand, leaning on his cane, until he stood beside the chair as Ysolde had stood, and looked through the frosted visor at the dead face behind it. For a long moment he was silent.

"Who is she?" he said at last.

"A diver," said Ysolde. "From the Sump. That's what they called the Lungs, then. Her name's on her suit."

He looked. *TULL — H-11873.*

"She shut it," said Ysolde.

Quist turned his head and looked at her.

"The Door," she said. "She shut it. From here. With that." She nodded at the key in the lock. "The Stewards didn't stay with us, Prelate. They weren't left to keep the account. They were *running*. They were on a ship called the *Grace* with every officer of the Combine and four hundred children they were taking out through the Door to sell. And they were going to stop here and shut it behind them so we couldn't follow. And she got here first, and shut it in front of them. That's what was done at Gallows." She held his eyes. "That's what they closed."

The Prelate-Auditor stood very still.

"You don't believe me," said Ysolde. "Listen to her."

She reached past him to the console, and put her finger on LOG.

* * *

He listened to all of it. She watched him do it. He stood beside the dead woman's chair with both hands on the head of his black cane, and his old broad face behind the visor did not change at all while the voice from the grille spoke of the Scholarship, and Brannock, and thirty thousand people in the ice. It did not change while the voice said *so I've shut it first*. It did not change while the voice said *the Factor and all his officers*. Behind him, Strand stood with his hand on his weapon, and the two young Bailiffs stood in the doorway, and Ysolde saw one of them — the younger, a long-faced boy with the look of the deep spokes about him — put his gloved hand to his throat, where, under the suit, a diver's tag would be.

It was the name that did it.

She saw it happen. She had heard the recording twice now and she knew where it came, and she was watching his face when it did, and so she saw it. The voice from the grille said, gently, at the very beginning, *Pell, it's Mum*, and Quist did not move. But near the end, when the voice said *whoever hears this, if it isn't her*, and paused, and said again, more quietly, *I hope it's you, Pell*, the Prelate-Auditor's face went white.

Not pale. White, as though every drop of blood in it had gone somewhere else at once. His hands on the head of his cane shook. He took a step back from the chair, and another, and his cane slipped on the frost, and the young Bailiff from the deep spokes caught him by the arm before he fell.

"Prelate—"

"*Pell,*" said Aurel Quist.

He was staring at the dead woman. He said it again, in a whisper, as though it were the first word of a prayer.

"*I swear,*" he said, "*by the Holy Number and the Count that keeps it, and by the First Auditor—*" His voice broke. "*By the First Auditor, Pell, who counted us.*"

Nobody in the round room moved.

"It's the oath," said Quist. He was holding the boy's arm with both hands now, his cane fallen on the frosted floor. "The Prelate's oath. Only the Prelate swears it. Only the Prelate knows the name. Hanne gave it me on his deathbed and I've said it to no one, ever, but alone, in the Reliquary, on Founding Day, kneeling at her chair." He was looking at the woman in the suit, at the steel half-disc in her lap. "*Pell.* Her name was Pell. She was a child when the Door was shut. She was one of the children in the Stewards' care. She learned her numbers at their knees, and she wrote the first Count, and she wrote—"

"*Count what is owed,*" said Ysolde, "*and not a mark more.*"

"*Count what's owed, love,*" said the voice from the grille, on its long silent loop, faint, a minute before the end. "*Count it right.*"

The Prelate-Auditor of the Nave closed his eyes.

* * *

"Prelate," said Strand. "We have to finish it."

His voice was quite steady. He had not moved through any of it; he stood in the middle of the round room in his scorched suit with his hand on his weapon and his pale eyes on the console, on the four bottles at the pin feed, on the faint deep red glow through the frosted window where the Hanged Men hung.

"She's done something to it," he said. "To the Door. I don't know what. But she's done something, and you can feel it in the deck." He looked at Quist. "The Compact. No soul shall go to Gallows. We've come, Prelate, so it falls to us. Whatever she's started, we stop it, and then we break this console so nobody can ever start it again." His hand moved to his belt, where there was a bag. "I brought charges. From the cutter."

"No," said Quist.

"Prelate, she's—"

"I said no, Captain." Quist had opened his eyes. He let go of the young Bailiff's arm and stood on his own, without his cane, swaying a little. "Leave it."

"You told me yourself," said Strand. "In the Nave. Before we came. You told me what would happen if the Count were broken. A hundred and ninety thousand people. You did the sum for me. You showed me. You said it was the most important thing in the world, that the Count holds, that it's the only thing that does." He took a step forward. "You were right. I believed you. I've *done* things, Prelate, because I believed you." His voice cracked for the first time. "I put an old man over the rail at the Number Two gallery because I believed you."

The round room was very quiet.

"I know," said Aurel Quist. "I know you did, Mattias. I told you to." He looked at the Bailiff-Captain, and his old broad face was terrible. "I had the sum wrong. That's all. I had all the right numbers in the wrong places. Anselm told me so at my own table. I didn't listen." He took a step, unsteady, and put himself between Strand and the console. "Leave it."

Strand looked at him for a long moment. Then he took the bag from his belt and opened it, and took out a charge — a small grey brick with a red light on it — and stepped round the Prelate toward the pin feed.

Quist caught his arm.

It was not a strong grip. He was seventy-one and he had been in the Shear for nine days and he could barely stand. Strand could have shaken him off with a turn of his shoulder. He did not. He stopped, and looked down at the old man's gloved hand on his arm, and then at the old man's face. And in that moment, as Ysolde would remember it for the rest of her life, something in Mattias Strand's pale patient eyes gave way, the way the lock on the Reliquary gate had given way under Corin's pick: a clank, deep inside, that she seemed almost to hear.

"*Let go,*" said Strand, and his weapon was in his other hand, and it went off.

It was very loud in the round room. It threw the Prelate backward against the console, and he slid down it to the frosted floor at the dead woman's feet, and sat there with his back against the grey metal and his legs out in front of him and a dark patch spreading on the white of his suit below the ribs.

Strand stood over him with the weapon in his hand. He looked at it as though he did not know what it was.

And behind him, from the doorway, the young Bailiff from the deep spokes — the long-faced boy who had put his hand to his throat at the sound of the divers' hymn — said in a high clear voice, "Captain. Put it down," and when Strand turned, the boy shot him.

* * *

Strand died where he fell, in the middle of the round room, on his back, with his cracked visor turned up to the frosted ceiling. Ysolde did not go to him. The other young Bailiff knelt beside him for a moment and then got up and went and stood by the door with his back to the room, and did not turn round.

The boy who had shot him lowered his weapon slowly, and stared at it, and then at Ysolde.

"My grandmother was a diver," he said. "Spoke Nineteen." His voice was shaking. "She sang that. The hymn. She got the third line wrong."

"So did mine," said Ysolde.

She went to the Prelate.

He was still alive. He was sitting against the console at the dead woman's feet, with one hand pressed to the dark patch on his suit, and when she knelt beside him he turned his head and looked at her through his visor, and smiled — the open, tired, uncomplicated smile she had seen in the old chapel at the drop dock, as though nothing in the world had pleased him so much as seeing her.

"Sister Marr," he said. His voice was thin. "You've lost weight."

"Don't talk."

"I've a few minutes. I can feel it. I'll talk if I like." He turned his head, slowly, and looked up at the console above him: the lit screens, the three words, the amber line at the foot of the seventh screen. "What's that? Your audit?"

"Yes."

"In the machine." He was quiet a moment. "Of course. The forms. They kept the forms. So that if Recovery should ever come—" He coughed, and something dark showed at the edge of his mouth. "What does it want?"

"A seal," said Ysolde. "A factor's seal."

Quist looked at her.

Then, slowly, with a care that seemed to cost him everything he had left, he lifted his right hand from the frosted floor and held it up in front of his visor and looked at it. At the black seal ring on its third finger, heavy and dull, that every novice in the Nave was taught to kiss and none was permitted to look at closely. He turned it a little, in the light of her lamp. And she saw, for the first time in her life, what was on its face, worn almost to nothing by three hundred and forty years of Prelates' hands: two letters, interlocked. An H. A V.

"Help me up," said the Prelate-Auditor.

* * *

She helped him up. It took both her and the boy from the deep spokes. They held him on his feet in front of the console, one on each side, while the dark patch on his white suit spread, and he stood swaying with his gloved right hand over the seventh screen, where the amber line said PRESENT FACTOR SEAL, and beside the screen, the small round plate of grey steel that it meant.

"Hanne told me," he said, "that it was the Stewards' own. The ring. Passed hand to hand, Prelate to Prelate. He said it was the seal of the one who kept the account." He was breathing in short shallow gasps. "*He kept the account.* That's what it says. On the last niche. You'll have seen it."

"I saw it."

"I always thought it meant he kept it *well*." He laughed, a terrible small sound. "It doesn't, does it? It means he *kept* it. Held on to it. Wouldn't let it go."

He pressed the ring to the plate.

The console thought for a moment. Then the amber line at the foot of the seventh screen went green, and above it, in the same plain square hand, a new line came up.

> FACTOR SEAL ACCEPTED: CASTELLANE, I. — FACTOR, VIGIL.

Quist stared at it.

"*Castellane,*" he said. "*The Factor.*" He looked at the dead woman in the chair beside him, at her strapped hand on the console. "*They'll tell you things about me. The Factor will, and his people.*" He closed his eyes. "Oh, she knew him. She knew him. And he sat in the hold of that ship and wrote that she'd shut it against us." His knees went. They held him. "Read it to me, Ysolde. I can't see it."

She read it to him. The screen had changed again.

> STATEMENT OF RECONCILIATION — SEALED.
> ACCOUNT HV-VIGIL-0001: CLOSED (LOCAL YEAR 280).
> BALANCE: CREDIT TO DEBTORS.

"Credit to debtors," said Aurel Quist. "*Credit.*" And then, so faintly that she had to put her visor against his to hear it: "Not a mark more."

They let him down, gently, to the frosted floor. He sat against the console where he had been, at the dead woman's feet, and the boy from the deep spokes knelt on his other side.

"Is there a name?" said Quist. "For the record. The boy's."

"Teodor," said the boy. "Teodor Brand. Spoke Nineteen."

"Teodor Brand." The Prelate turned his head. "And you heard me, Teodor. And — him." He nodded at the other Bailiff by the door, who had turned round at last. "Witnesses. Good. Hear the Count, then." He took the ring off his finger, slowly, and held it out to Ysolde in his gloved palm. "By order of the Prelate-Auditor. In the three hundred and forty-first year. Ysolde Marr is restored to the Count. And is named—" He coughed, long and wet. "Named Prelate-Auditor after me. Take it."

"I can't."

"You can. You must. Somebody has to keep the account." He closed her fingers over the ring, as Corin had closed them over the pup's yoke, as Piet had closed them over her father's tag. "And somebody has to stop keeping it. Slowly. Not all at once; they'd tear each other apart, I was right about that much. But slowly. Year by year. Take the Interest off. Raise the age back to sixteen. Lower the tithe. Let it get lighter, a little every Reckoning, until they can bear to hear what's in that machine." He was looking at her. "Do the slow thing, Ysolde. It's the hardest. Anselm couldn't. I wouldn't. You're a counter. You can."

"And the Door?"

"Let it breathe," said Aurel Quist. "You've started it. Let it breathe. Four hundred years." His eyes went past her, to the frosted window, to the faint red glow of the Hanged Men. "Let them choose, when it's open. Not us." He smiled. "We've had our turn."

He was quiet after that. She held his hand. After a while she realised that she was holding it for nobody.

* * *

They laid him beside her.

There was nowhere else. They could not take him back; the *Recompense* was crippled and had air for the three of them and barely that. Ysolde would not have put him in the cold of its hold if it had had air for a hundred. So she and Teodor Brand and the other boy, whose name was Lew, laid the Prelate-Auditor of the Nave on the frosted floor of the round room at Gallows, at the feet of the diver in the chair, on his back, with his hands folded on his chest the way the Stewards lay in their niches. Ysolde put his black cane beside him.

She left Strand where he was. The boys took him away down the spine, in the end, and she did not ask where.

Then she sat down on the floor beside the two of them, the old man and the dead woman, and wrote.

She wrote on the back of the last of Corin's reckoning sheets, on her knee, in ink, slowly, because her hands would not stop shaking. She wrote it in the ledger hand, the old square letters, because she did not know who would read it or when, and the ledger hand was the only thing she could think of that might last four hundred years. When it was done she folded it and put it in the tin Corin had given her for her pens — an old breath-seal tin, dented, with ASCH stamped on the lid — and put the tin under the Prelate's folded hands. And with it, folded small, the paper audit she had written in the *Small Mercy*'s cabin: every line, every year, Tamsin in the margin. In case the machine failed. In case whoever came could not read machines.

She left the key in the lock. The console said it must stay there, to hold the restoration. She did not touch it.

She left the dead woman's hand where it lay, flat on the console, beside it.

And then she knelt, and lifted the steel half-disc gently out of the dead woman's lap on its cord, and held it in her palm. H-11873. SURETY. Its broken toothed edge.

"I'll bring it to her," she said, aloud, in the dead air. "I promise. I'll put them together."

She put the cord over her head, beside her father's.

* * *

The Reliquary was cold and dark and smelled of iron, and Ysolde came down into it alone, on the hundred and fortieth rung of the smiths' stair, on Founding Day, with a lamp, in white.

It had been forty days. She had come back on the crippled *Recompense* with Teodor Brand and Lew Okafor and three days of air to spare, and walked into the Nave with the black ring on her finger and two Bailiffs from the deep spokes at her back, and stood at the rail of the Ledger Hall under the Count Board and told the nine Chanceries that Aurel Quist was dead in the Shear and had named her before witnesses. She did not tell them anything else. She did not tell them about Gallows. Not yet. They had not believed her, and then some of them had, and then the Chancery of Law had read the Prelate's last order down the speaker-wire to every corridor in the Lungs, *Ysolde Marr is restored to the Count*, and the Lungs had said *it is just* — and then, a day later, a little uncertainly, *it is just* again, when the dry voice said who she now was.

It would not hold. She knew that. Not without work. There were elders in the Third and the Fifth and the Chancery of Law who looked at her across the Ledger Hall the way Strand had looked at her across Anselm's desk, and there would be more. It would take everything she had, and more, and it would take years, and some of those years she would not survive. She knew that too.

At the first Reckoning, in ninety days, the Interest of Sorrow would be added to the Count as it always was. But it would be a tenth less. And at the Reckoning after that, a tenth less again. That was all. That was the slow thing. She had done the sum.

She crossed the Reliquary in the dark, past the rows of seats with their rotted straps that faced one way, past the dead consoles, past the twelve niches in the curved wall. She stopped at the last one, and held up her lamp, and read the line under the title. *He kept the account.* She looked at it for a while.

Then she went on, to the far end, to the platform where something larger had once been bolted, and the plain metal chair, and the half-disc of steel hanging from its arm on its new cord, kissed smooth by three hundred and forty years of Prelates' mouths.

She knelt in front of it, where every Prelate had knelt.

She took the cord from round her neck — not her father's; the other — and held the dead diver's half-disc in her palm, its stampings sharp and clean. H-11873. SURETY. And she reached out, with her other hand, and lifted the First Auditor's half from the arm of the chair, gently, on its cord, and held it beside the first.

The broken edges were the same steel, the same thickness, the same dull grey.

She brought them together.

They fitted. Tooth to tooth, along the whole jagged line, every point into every notch, the way they had been cut to fit on a day centuries ago when a clerk of the Combine had broken a steel disc in two and given half to a mother and half to a child. There was no gap. There was no glass between them. Ysolde Marr knelt in the dark of the Reliquary with the two halves held together in her hands, a whole disc of steel the size of her palm, sharp on the one side and worn smooth as water on the other.

She tilted it to the lamp. And in the smooth half, so faint that she would never afterward be sure she had not imagined it — a shadow in the steel, the last ghost of a stamping that three centuries of mouths had not quite kissed away — four figures.

0212.
